Ch 29-30

Exam sheet: Chapters 29 and 30: Plant Diversity I and II

Memorize this, review it last
  1. Q1 plant traits (5): multicellular, eukaryotic, photosynthetic autotroph, cellulose walls, chlorophylls a and b.
  2. Q2 evidence linking plants specifically to charophyte algae: shared cellulose-synthesizing protein rings, similar flagellated sperm structure, and nuclear/chloroplast/mitochondrial DNA similarities.
  3. Q3 derived land-plant traits (4): alternation of generations, dependent embryos, walled spores in sporangia, apical meristems. Q5 extras: cuticle, stomata, mycorrhizae.
  4. Q4: plants have multicellular n AND 2n generations (meiosis makes spores, mitosis makes gametes); animals have only a 2n body and meiosis makes gametes.
  5. DRAW general cycle: gametophyte (n) -mitosis-> gametes -> FERTILIZATION -> zygote (2n) -> sporophyte (2n) -MEIOSIS-> spores (n) -mitosis-> gametophyte.
  6. DRAW moss cycle: spore, protonema, male/female gametophytes, sperm swim to egg in archegonium, zygote, sporophyte (foot, seta, capsule) on gametophyte, meiosis in capsule.
  7. Q6 bryophytes: Hepatophyta = liverworts (no stomata), Bryophyta = mosses, Anthocerophyta = hornworts (no seta). Gametophyte dominant in all three.
  8. Q8 moss benefits (3): nitrogen retention, peat as fuel, peat moss as soil conditioner (also carbon storage).
  9. Q9 vascular traits (5): dominant sporophyte; xylem carries water and minerals through dead, lignified conducting cells, phloem carries sugars through living conducting cells; roots; leaves (microphylls only in lycophytes); sporophylls.
  10. Q10: megasporangium makes megaspores (female gametophyte, eggs); microsporangium makes microspores (male gametophyte, sperm). Homosporous = one spore type.
  11. Q11: Lycophyta = club mosses, spikemosses, quillworts; Monilophyta = ferns, horsetails, whisk ferns (no true roots).
  12. Q12 seed plant adaptations (5): seeds, reduced gametophytes, heterospory, ovules, pollen. Q13: reduced gametophyte = protection + nutrition.
  13. Q14 gymnosperm phyla (4): Cycadophyta (Cycas, most endangered), Ginkgophyta (Ginkgo biloba), Gnetophyta (Gnetum, Ephedra, Welwitschia), Coniferophyta (pines). Cycads and ginkgo have flagellated sperm.
  14. Q15: naked seed = not in an ovary, exposed on cone scales; pine takes nearly THREE years.
  15. Q16: flowers and fruits. Q18: fruit = mature ovary; dispersal by wind, water, animals.
  16. DRAW Fig. 30.8 flower: sepal, petal, stamen (anther + filament), carpel (stigma + style + ovary), ovule, receptacle.
  17. Q19 double fertilization: tube through micropyle, two sperm; sperm + egg = 2n zygote; sperm + two polar nuclei = 3n endosperm.
  18. DRAW angiosperm cycle: anther meiosis -> microspores -> pollen (tube cell, generative cell); ovule meiosis -> 1 of 4 megaspores -> 3 mitoses -> embryo sac; double fertilization; seed in fruit.
  19. Q20 groups: basal angiosperms, magnoliids, monocots, eudicots. Q21 monocot/eudicot: cotyledons 1/2; veins parallel/netlike; bundles scattered/ring; roots fibrous/taproot; pollen openings 1/3; floral parts in 3s/4s or 5s.
  20. Q22: atropine from belladonna, dilates the pupil. Q23: human population growth, tropical deforestation, extinctions; diversity is nonrenewable.
29.1

Plants evolved from green algae

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

List the five plant traits and the charophyte evidenceList the five characteristics that define plants. Then list the three distinctive characteristics plants share only with charophyte algae.List from memory on paper
  • Plant traits (5): multicellular
  • eukaryotic
  • photosynthetic autotroph
  • cell walls made of cellulose
  • chloroplasts with chlorophylls a and b. Each is also found in some algae. Shared only with charophytes (3): circular rings of cellulose-synthesizing proteins in the plasma membrane (other algae have linear sets)
  • flagellated sperm structure that resembles charophyte sperm
  • similarities in nuclear, chloroplast and mitochondrial DNA.

Common mistake: Listing derived land traits (embryos, apical meristems) as the 5 defining traits; those answer Q3, not Q1.

Name the derived traits of land plantsName the four derived traits that separate land plants from charophytes and give one function of each. Then name the three additional derived traits many plants evolved on land.Write 4 to 7 lines on paper
  • Derived traits (4, charophytes lack all): alternation of generations (multicellular n and 2n generations)
  • multicellular, dependent embryos (retained in the female gametophyte, fed through placental transfer cells
  • plants are embryophytes)
  • walled spores produced in sporangia (sporopollenin walls resist drying)
  • apical meristems (cell division at root and shoot tips, so they keep elongating). Additional traits (Q5): cuticle (waxy layer, prevents water loss, some protection from microbes)
  • stomata (pores for CO2 and O2 exchange, main route of water loss, close in hot dry conditions)
  • mycorrhizae (symbiotic fungi that transfer soil nutrients to the plant).

Common mistake: Mixing the lists: cuticle, stomata and mycorrhizae are the Q5 extras, not the four derived traits of Q3.

Draw the general plant life cycle and contrast it with animalsDraw the general plant life cycle, labeling ploidy, mitosis, meiosis and fertilization. Then explain how plant alternation of generations differs from the haploid and diploid stages in animals.Draw and label, then write 2 lines
  • Cycle: gametophyte (n) -> mitosis -> gametes (n) -> fertilization -> zygote (2n) -> mitosis -> sporophyte (2n) -> meiosis in sporangia -> spores (n) -> mitosis -> gametophyte. Gametophyte makes gametes by MITOSIS
  • sporophyte makes spores by MEIOSIS. Contrast: plants alternate two MULTICELLULAR generations, a haploid gametophyte and a diploid sporophyte, each giving rise to the other. In animals the only multicellular stage is diploid
  • meiosis makes gametes directly, and single-celled gametes are the only haploid stage.

Common mistake: Writing that meiosis makes gametes in plants. In plants meiosis makes spores; gametes come from mitosis in the gametophyte.

Full notes for 29.1
★ She said this is on the test
  • Know the general plant life cycle, moss life cycle and angiosperm life cycle.Draw the general cycle: gametophyte (n) makes gametes by mitosis, fertilization, zygote, sporophyte (2n), meiosis makes spores, spores grow into gametophytes.
  • Define plants by listing the characteristics (traits) that make them plants. Some of these are shared by green algae. There should be a total of 5. (619)Multicellular, eukaryotic, photosynthetic autotroph, cellulose cell walls, chlorophylls a and b.
  • List the three distinctive characteristics that are shared with charophytes. (619)Cellulose-synthesizing protein rings, sperm structure, nuclear/chloroplast/mitochondrial DNA.
  • Name the terrestrial adaptations (derived traits) of land plants. (620-621)Alternation of generations, multicellular dependent embryos, walled spores in sporangia, apical meristems.
  • Alternation of generations occurs in plants and some algae. How [is] this different from the alternate haploid and diploid conditions found in animals? (620)Plants have a multicellular haploid AND a multicellular diploid generation; animals have only a diploid body, and meiosis makes gametes.
  • Name the additional derived traits that evolved in many plant species as a result of movement onto land. (621)Cuticle, stomata, mycorrhizae.
Big idea
Plants are multicellular, eukaryotic, photosynthetic autotrophs with cellulose cell walls and chlorophylls a and b; some algae share each of these traits. Charophyte green algae are the closest living relatives of plants because only they share cellulose-synthesizing protein rings, similar sperm, and DNA evidence with plants. Plants (embryophytes) are set apart from charophytes by derived traits that help life on land: alternation of generations, multicellular dependent embryos, walled spores made in sporangia, and apical meristems. Many plants also evolved a cuticle, stomata, and partnerships with mycorrhizal fungi.

Must know

  • Guide Q1, the 5 traits that define plants (shared with some algae): (1) multicellular, (2) eukaryotic, (3) photosynthetic autotrophs, (4) cell walls made of cellulose, (5) chloroplasts with chlorophylls a and b.
  • Guide Q2, the 3 distinctive traits shared only with charophytes: (1) circular rings of cellulose-synthesizing proteins in the plasma membrane (other algae have LINEAR sets), (2) flagellated sperm structure closely resembling charophyte sperm, (3) nuclear, chloroplast and mitochondrial DNA similarities.
  • Guide Q3, derived traits of land plants (found in plants, not charophytes): alternation of generations, multicellular dependent embryos, walled spores produced in sporangia, apical meristems.
  • Alternation of generations: multicellular haploid gametophyte and multicellular diploid sporophyte give rise to each other. Gametophyte makes gametes by MITOSIS; sporophyte makes spores by MEIOSIS.
  • Guide Q4, how this differs from animals: plants have TWO multicellular generations, a haploid gametophyte and a diploid sporophyte. In animals the only multicellular stage is diploid; meiosis makes gametes directly, and the single-celled gametes are the only haploid cells. In plants meiosis makes SPORES, and gametes come from mitosis.
  • Multicellular dependent embryo: zygote retained in tissue of the female gametophyte, which protects it and supplies sugars and amino acids. Placental transfer cells (wall ingrowths) move nutrients to the embryo. Plants are called embryophytes because of this trait.
  • Walled spores in sporangia: sporangia are multicellular organs of the sporophyte; sporopollenin walls let spores travel through dry air without harm.
  • Apical meristems: regions of cell division at the tips of roots and shoots; they divide throughout life so roots and shoots elongate and reach more resources.
  • Guide Q5, additional derived traits in many species: CUTICLE (waxy layer that prevents water loss and gives some protection from microbes), STOMATA (pores for CO2 and O2 exchange; main route of water loss; close in hot, dry conditions), and MYCORRHIZAE (symbiotic fungi that transfer soil nutrients; helped early plants that had no roots).

Terms

Charophytes
Green algae that are the closest living relatives of plants.
Embryophytes
Plants; named for their multicellular, dependent embryos.
Sporopollenin
Durable polymer that protects charophyte zygotes and plant spore walls from drying out.
Alternation of generations
Life cycle in which a multicellular haploid gametophyte and a multicellular diploid sporophyte give rise to each other.
Gametophyte
Multicellular haploid (n) generation that produces gametes by mitosis.
Sporophyte
Multicellular diploid (2n) generation that produces haploid spores by meiosis.
Spore
Haploid reproductive cell that develops into a new organism without fusing with another cell.
Sporangium (pl. sporangia)
Multicellular organ of the sporophyte that produces spores.
Placental transfer cells
Embryo cells with wall ingrowths that move nutrients from the parent to the embryo.
Apical meristem
Region of cell division at the tip of a root or shoot.
Cuticle
Waxy covering of the epidermis that prevents excess water loss.
Stomata (sing. stoma)
Pores that allow CO2 and O2 exchange and are the main route of water evaporation.
Mycorrhizae
Symbiotic associations of plant roots with fungi that transfer soil nutrients to the plant.
Vascular tissue
Cells joined into tubes that carry water and nutrients through the plant body.
Bryophytes
Informal name for nonvascular plants: liverworts, mosses and hornworts.

Examples

  • Zygnema: filamentous pond alga in the Zygnematophyceae, the charophyte group most closely related to plants (Fig. 29.3).
  • Marchantia (liverwort): embryo and placental transfer cell shown in Fig. 29.5.
  • Mnium (moss): sporophytes with sporangia whose spores have sporopollenin walls (Fig. 29.6).
  • Cooksonia: 425-million-year-old sporangium fossil (Fig. 29.9).
What she stressed in lecture
  • Guide Q1: "Define plants by listing the characteristics (traits) that make them plants. Some of these are shared by green algae. There should be a total of 5." (p. 619)
  • Guide Q2: "List the three distinctive characteristics that are shared with charophytes." (p. 619)
  • Guide Q3: "Name the terrestrial adaptations (derived traits) of land plants." (pp. 620-621)
  • Guide Q4: "Alternation of generations occurs in plants and some algae. How is this different from the alternate haploid and diploid conditions found in animals?" (p. 620)
  • Guide Q5: "Name the additional derived traits that evolved in many plant species as a result of movement onto land." (p. 621)
  • Guide, last line: "Know the general plant life cycle, moss life cycle and angiosperm life cycle." The general cycle is Fig. 29.5.
Memory hooks
  • Many Eager Plants Collect ChlorophyllGuide Q1, the 5 plant traits: Multicellular, Eukaryotic, Photosynthetic autotroph, Cellulose walls, Chlorophylls a and b.
  • Rings, Sperm, DNA: charophytes are Really Similar DNA-wiseGuide Q2, the 3 traits only charophytes share with plants: cellulose-synthesizing protein rings, sperm structure, nuclear/chloroplast/mitochondrial DNA.
  • All Embryos Want ApplesGuide Q3, derived traits of land plants: Alternation of generations, multicellular dependent Embryos, Walled spores in sporangia, Apical meristems.
  • Cuticle, Stomata, Mycorrhizae: plants Can Stay MoistGuide Q5, the additional land traits: cuticle, stomata, mycorrhizal fungi.
  • Gametophyte makes Gametes by mitosis; Sporophyte makes Spores by meiosisEach generation is named for what it produces. Meiosis happens only in the sporophyte.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Fig. 29.5 Alternation of generations (five steps): (1) gametophyte (n) makes gametes by mitosis; (2) two gametes fuse (fertilization) into a 2n zygote; (3) zygote divides by mitosis into the multicellular 2n sporophyte; (4) sporophyte makes haploid spores by meiosis; (5) spores divide by mitosis into multicellular n gametophytes. Draw as a circle: haploid half (blue) on top, diploid half (pink) on the bottom, MEIOSIS and FERTILIZATION as the two switch points.
  • Fig. 29.5 lower panel: Marchantia embryo inside maternal tissue; placental transfer cell with wall ingrowths.
  • Fig. 29.6: moss sporophyte (stalk and sporangium) growing out of the green gametophyte; spores inside the sporangium.
  • Fig. 29.7: apical meristem of a shoot with developing leaves.
  • Fig. 29.10: phylogeny marking the origins of plants, vascular plants and seed plants. Bryophytes and seedless vascular plants are not clades; vascular plants and seed plants are.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Who shares them: brown, red and certain green algae are also multicellular photosynthetic autotrophs; green algae, dinoflagellates and brown algae have cellulose walls; green algae, euglenids and a few dinoflagellates have chlorophylls a and b.
  • Closest living charophyte relatives: the Zygnematophyceae, such as Zygnema (analysis of nearly 900 nuclear genes). Plants arose from within charophytes but did not descend from these living algae.
  • Sporopollenin: durable polymer that keeps exposed charophyte zygotes from drying out; the same polymer forms the tough walls of plant spores.
  • Land offered unfiltered sunlight, more CO2, and mineral-rich soil; the challenges were scarcity of water and lack of structural support against gravity.
  • More than 325,000 known plant species. This text equates kingdom Plantae with embryophytes (plants with embryos).
  • Timeline: plants originated about 470 million years ago (fossil spores); Cooksonia sporangium fossils 425 million years ago; vascular plants 425 million years ago; seed plants 360 million years ago.
  • Ten extant phyla (Table 29.1): 3 bryophyte (Hepatophyta, Bryophyta, Anthocerophyta), 2 seedless vascular (Lycophyta, Monilophyta), 4 gymnosperm (Ginkgophyta, Cycadophyta, Gnetophyta, Coniferophyta), 1 angiosperm (Anthophyta).
  • Vascular plants form a clade (about 93% of plant species). Bryophytes and seedless vascular plants are not clades (they are groups that share features).
  • Mycorrhizal fossils are about 420 million years old.
29.2

Mosses and other nonvascular plants have life cycles dominated by gametophytes

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Build the bryophyte phyla tableMake a table of the three bryophyte phyla: phylum name, common name, key characteristics and an example. Add one row of traits all three share.Draw a table on paper
  • Hepatophyta | liverworts | liver-shaped gametophyte, thalloid or leafy
  • tiny sporophyte with short seta
  • NO stomata | Marchantia. Bryophyta | mosses | leafy gametophyte carpet from a protonema
  • sporophyte with foot, seta, capsule and stomata | Polytrichum, Sphagnum. Anthocerophyta | hornworts | horn-shaped sporophyte with NO seta, splits from the tip, has stomata
  • nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria | Anthoceros. All three: gametophyte dominant, sporophyte dependent, no vascular tissue, sperm swim through water.

Common mistake: Swapping the missing features: liverworts lack stomata; hornworts lack a seta. Bryophyta is only the mosses, not all bryophytes.

Define protonema, gametophyte, sporophyte and rhizoidsDefine protonema, gametophyte, sporophyte and rhizoids as they apply to bryophytes. For each, give its ploidy or its function.Write 4 short definitions on paper
  • Protonema: green, branched, one-cell-thick filaments from a germinating spore (n)
  • large surface absorbs water and minerals
  • makes buds that grow into gametophytes. Gametophyte: the dominant haploid generation, larger and longer-lived
  • makes eggs in archegonia and sperm in antheridia. Sporophyte: the diploid spore-producing generation, smallest of all plants, attached to and dependent on the gametophyte
  • foot, seta and capsule (hornworts lack a seta). Rhizoids: thin anchoring cells or filaments of the gametophyte
  • not roots, no conducting cells, not the main absorbers of water.

Common mistake: Calling rhizoids roots. Rhizoids only anchor; they lack conducting cells and are not the main route of water absorption.

Give three benefits of mossesGive three ecological and economic benefits of mosses. Name the moss involved in each where you can.List 3 from memory

Keep nitrogen in soil: Polytrichum cuts nitrogen leaching from sandy soil; feather moss Pleurozium hosts nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria. Peat from Sphagnum is harvested as fuel. Peat moss is a soil conditioner and packing for plant roots (absorbs about 20 times its weight in water). Also acceptable: peatlands store about one-third of the world's soil carbon.

Common mistake: Saying the moss itself fixes nitrogen. Cyanobacteria living with feather moss fix it; Polytrichum reduces nitrogen loss by leaching.

Draw the moss life cycleDraw the moss life cycle starting from a spore. Label protonema, male and female gametophytes, antheridium, archegonium, fertilization, zygote, the three sporophyte parts, meiosis and ploidy.Draw and label on paper
  • Spore (n) -> protonema -> buds -> male and female gametophytes (n)
  • antheridia make sperm, archegonia each hold one egg
  • sperm swim through water to the egg in the archegonium
  • fertilization -> zygote (2n) -> embryo in the archegonium
  • sporophyte (2n: foot, seta, capsule) grows on and depends on the female gametophyte
  • meiosis in the capsule -> spores (n), released through the peristome
  • spores grow into new protonemata. Check: gametophyte is dominant
  • water is needed for fertilization.

Common mistake: Drawing the sporophyte as a separate plant. It stays attached to and dependent on the female gametophyte.

Full notes for 29.2
★ She said this is on the test
  • Know the general plant life cycle, moss life cycle and angiosperm life cycle.Moss cycle: spore, protonema, male and female gametophytes, sperm swim to egg in archegonium, zygote, dependent sporophyte (foot, seta, capsule), meiosis in capsule.
  • Name the three bryophyte phyla. List characteristics of each. Give common name. (Make a table!) (626)Hepatophyta = liverworts, Bryophyta = mosses, Anthocerophyta = hornworts, with their sporophyte features.
  • Define protonema, gametophyte, sporophyte, and rhizoids as they apply to bryophytes. (627-628)Protonema = first filaments from a spore; gametophyte dominant (n); sporophyte small, dependent (2n); rhizoids anchor, not roots.
  • What are some of the ecological and economic benefits of mosses? (Give 3) (627-628)Nitrogen retention, peat as fuel, peat moss as soil conditioner (also carbon storage).
Big idea
The bryophytes are three phyla of small, nonvascular plants: liverworts (Hepatophyta), mosses (Bryophyta) and hornworts (Anthocerophyta). In all three the haploid gametophyte is the dominant, larger and longer-lived generation, and the small sporophyte stays attached to it for nutrition. Flagellated sperm must swim through a film of water to the egg, so most bryophytes live in moist places. Mosses help soils keep nitrogen, and Sphagnum forms peat that is burned as fuel, used as a soil conditioner, and stores large amounts of carbon.

Must know

  • Guide Q6, the 3 bryophyte phyla: Hepatophyta = liverworts; Bryophyta = mosses; Anthocerophyta = hornworts (see compare table).
  • Liverworts (Hepatophyta): liver-shaped gametophytes, either thalloid (flattened, Marchantia) or leafy (Plagiochila). Tiny sporophyte with a short seta and oval capsule. NO stomata.
  • Hornworts (Anthocerophyta): named for the long, tapered horn-shaped sporophyte (up to about 5 cm). The sporophyte has NO seta, only a sporangium that splits open from the tip; it has a cuticle and stomata. Often the first plants on open moist soil, helped by symbiotic nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria.
  • Mosses (Bryophyta): the green carpet is mostly gametophytes (most under 15 cm); "leaves" are one cell thick. Sporophytes are elongated and visible, green when young and tan or brownish red when ripe; stomata present. Examples: Polytrichum (hairy-cap moss), Sphagnum (peat moss).
  • Guide Q7, protonema (pl. protonemata): mass of green, branched, one-cell-thick filaments produced by a germinating moss spore; large surface area absorbs water and minerals; produces "buds" that grow into gametophytes.
  • Guide Q7, gametophyte (bryophyte): the dominant haploid stage; larger and longer-living than the sporophyte; forms ground-hugging carpets because body parts are thin and there is no vascular tissue. Carries gametangia: archegonia (female, one egg each) and antheridia (male, many sperm). In mosses, archegonia and antheridia are usually on separate female and male gametophytes.
  • Guide Q7, sporophyte (bryophyte): smallest sporophytes of all plants; foot (absorbs nutrients from the gametophyte), seta (stalk that conducts materials and raises the capsule), sporangium or capsule (makes spores by meiosis). Green when young but cannot live independently; stays attached to and dependent on the gametophyte for sugars, amino acids, minerals and water.
  • Guide Q7, rhizoids: delicate anchors of the gametophyte; long tubular single cells (liverworts, hornworts) or filaments of cells (mosses). Unlike roots, they lack specialized conducting cells and do not play a primary role in water and mineral absorption.
  • Guide Q8, give 3 benefits of mosses: (1) they keep NITROGEN in soil (Polytrichum on sandy soil cuts nitrogen loss by leaching; feather moss Pleurozium hosts nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria); (2) PEAT from Sphagnum is harvested as FUEL (Ireland, Canada); (3) peat moss is a SOIL CONDITIONER and packing for plant roots (its large dead cells absorb about 20 times its weight in water). A fourth defensible answer: peatlands store huge amounts of CARBON.

Terms

Protonema
Green, branched, one-cell-thick filaments that grow from a germinating moss spore and produce buds that become gametophytes.
Rhizoids
Thin anchoring cells or filaments of bryophyte gametophytes; not true roots and not the main route of absorption.
Gametangia
Multicellular, protected organs that produce gametes.
Archegonium (pl. archegonia)
Female gametangium; produces one egg and holds the embryo after fertilization.
Antheridium (pl. antheridia)
Male gametangium; produces many flagellated sperm.
Foot
Part of the bryophyte sporophyte embedded in the archegonium that absorbs nutrients from the gametophyte.
Seta (pl. setae)
Stalk of a bryophyte sporophyte that conducts materials to the capsule and raises it.
Capsule
The sporangium of a bryophyte sporophyte; makes spores by meiosis.
Peristome
Ring of tooth-like structures on a moss capsule that opens in dry air to release spores gradually.
Peat
Deposits of partially decayed organic material, mainly Sphagnum.

Examples

  • Marchantia polymorpha: thalloid liverwort, gametangia elevated on stalks.
  • Plagiochila deltoidea: leafy liverwort.
  • Anthoceros: hornwort with horn-shaped sporophytes.
  • Polytrichum commune: hairy-cap moss with cuticle-coated ridged "leaves"; reduces nitrogen loss from sandy soil (Fig. 29.14).
  • Pleurozium (feather moss): hosts nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria in northern coniferous forests.
  • Sphagnum (peat moss): forms peat; fuel, soil conditioner, preserves bog bodies such as Tollund Man.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Guide Q6: "Name the three bryophyte phyla. List characteristics of each. Give common name. (Make a table!)" (p. 626)
  • Guide Q7: "Define protonema, gametophyte, sporophyte, and rhizoids as they apply to bryophytes." (pp. 627-628, content on pp. 624-625)
  • Guide Q8: "What are some of the ecological and economic benefits of mosses? (Give 3)" (pp. 627-628)
  • Guide, last line: "Know ... moss life cycle" (Fig. 29.11).
Memory hooks
  • Hepatophyta = hepatitis organ = liverPhylum Hepatophyta = liverworts, named for liver-shaped gametophytes.
  • Anthocerophyta: a rhino's hornAnthocerophyta = hornworts, named for the horn-shaped sporophyte (keras, horn). No seta, only the horn.
  • Foot, Seta, Capsule: Fast Spore CannonThe three parts of a bryophyte sporophyte, bottom to top.
  • Liverworts are Less: no stomata, tiny setaLiverworts lack stomata; moss and hornwort sporophytes have them.
  • Protonema = the first threadsGerminating moss spore makes the protonema first, which buds into a gametophyte.
  • Mosses Never Fail the Soil: Nitrogen, Fuel, Soil conditionerGuide Q8, three benefits: keep nitrogen in soil, peat burned as fuel, peat moss as soil conditioner and root packing. Carbon storage in peatlands is a fourth.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Fig. 29.11 Moss life cycle (7 steps): (1) spores develop into threadlike protonemata; (2) haploid protonemata produce buds that grow by mitosis into male and female gametophytes; (3) sperm from antheridia swim through a film of moisture to the egg in an archegonium; fertilization inside the archegonium makes the 2n zygote; (4) zygote develops into a sporophyte embryo; (5) sporophyte grows a long seta out of the archegonium; (6) attached by its foot, the sporophyte stays nutritionally dependent on the gametophyte; (7) meiosis in the capsule makes haploid spores; lid pops off and the peristome releases them.
  • Fig. 29.13: liverwort, hornwort and moss photos with gametophyte and sporophyte labeled.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Liverwort name: hepaticus = liver; in medieval times they were thought to treat liver disease. Marchantia raises its gametangia on tree-like stalks; more liverwort species are leafy than thalloid.
  • Hornwort gametophytes are 1 to 2 cm and grow horizontally; the name comes from keras, horn.
  • Moss gametophytes range from under 1 mm to 60 cm; moss sporophytes reach about 20 cm.
  • Not true mosses: Irish moss (red seaweed), reindeer moss (lichen), club mosses (seedless vascular plants), Spanish moss (lichens or flowering plants).
  • Moss capsule can release over 5 million spores. Peristome = ring of tooth-like structures at the top of the capsule; teeth open when dry and close when moist, releasing spores gradually on wind gusts.
  • Eggs stay in the base of the archegonium; sperm swim to them, guided by chemical attractants. Embryos are retained in archegonia, fed by placental transfer cells.
  • Asexual reproduction: brood bodies, small plantlets that detach and grow into genetically identical copies.
  • Some mosses have conducting tissue in their "stems" and grow to 60 cm; this arose by convergent evolution.
  • Bowden's study (Fig. 29.14): with Polytrichum about 0.3 kg/ha of nitrogen leached per year versus about 5 kg/ha with the moss removed; the ecosystem kept over 95% of its 10.5 kg/ha nitrogen input.
  • Mosses survive losing most of their body water and rehydrate later; phenolic compounds in their walls absorb UV, so they live on mountaintops, tundra and deserts. (This is an adaptation, not a benefit.)
  • Peatlands cover 3% of Earth's land but hold one-third of the world's soil carbon (about 500 billion tons).
  • Acidic, oxygen-poor bogs resist decay and preserve bodies, such as the Tollund Man (405 to 100 BCE).
  • Overharvesting Sphagnum (peat-fired power stations) and drying peatlands release stored CO2 and add to global warming.
  • Physcomitrella patens: moss that increased chemical weathering of rock in the Ordovician experiment.
  • Fig. 29.14: bar graph, annual nitrogen loss about 0.3 kg/ha with moss versus about 5 kg/ha without moss.
  • Fig. 29.15: peat harvesting and the Tollund Man bog mummy.
29.3

Ferns and other seedless vascular plants were the first plants to grow tall

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

List the five traits common to all vascular plantsState the origin of vascular plants, then list and briefly describe the five characteristics common to all vascular plants. Contrast xylem and phloem.Write 5 to 6 lines on paper
  • Origin: ancestors had branched sporophytes that did not depend on the gametophyte (branching allowed multiple sporangia). (1) Dominant sporophyte
  • (2) xylem and phloem: xylem carries water and minerals through dead, lignified conducting cells (tracheids)
  • phloem carries sugars and organic products through living conducting cells
  • (3) roots: absorb water and nutrients and anchor, replacing rhizoids
  • (4) leaves: microphylls (one unbranched vein, lycophytes only) or megaphylls (branched veins)
  • (5) sporophylls, leaves that bear sporangia (sori in ferns, strobili in lycophytes).

Common mistake: Reversing living and dead: xylem conducting cells are dead and lignified at maturity; phloem conducting cells are alive.

Define homosporous, heterosporous, megasporangium and microsporangiumDefine homosporous and heterosporous. Then define megasporangium and microsporangium, tracing what each makes all the way to gametes.Write 4 definitions on paper

Homosporous: one type of sporophyll, sporangium and spore; the spore usually grows into a bisexual gametophyte (most ferns, club mosses). Heterosporous: two types of spores (all seed plants, spikemosses, quillworts). Megasporangium (on a megasporophyll) -> megaspores -> female gametophytes -> eggs. Microsporangium (on a microsporophyll) -> microspores (smaller, many) -> male gametophytes -> sperm.

Common mistake: Saying the megasporangium makes eggs directly. It makes megaspores, which develop into female gametophytes that make the eggs.

Describe the seedless vascular plant phylaDescribe the seedless vascular plants. Name both phyla, list the major members of each, and give one identifying trait per member.Write a 2-row table on paper
  • Seedless vascular plants: vascular tissue but no seeds
  • flagellated sperm need water, so most live in damp places
  • formed the first forests. Lycophyta: club mosses (homosporous), spikemosses and quillworts (heterosporous)
  • microphylls
  • strobili. Monilophyta: ferns (megaphyll fronds, fiddleheads, sori, homosporous)
  • horsetails (Equisetum, jointed gritty stems that do the photosynthesis)
  • whisk ferns (Psilotum, the only vascular plants without true roots).

Common mistake: Calling club mosses and spikemosses mosses. They are vascular lycophytes; true mosses are nonvascular bryophytes.

Full notes for 29.3
★ She said this is on the test
  • List and briefly describe the characteristics (origins and traits) that are common to all vascular plants (5 characteristics). (629-631)Branched independent sporophyte origin; dominant sporophyte, xylem and phloem, roots, leaves, sporophylls.
  • Define homosporous and heterosporous. Define megasporangium and microsporangium. Be sure to include what each of these structures make. (631)Homosporous plants make one spore type; heterosporous plants make two. Megasporangia make megaspores that develop into female gametophytes producing eggs; microsporangia make microspores that develop into male gametophytes producing sperm.
  • Describe the seedless vascular plants. List and briefly describe all phyla including major members. (631-632)Lycophyta (club mosses, spikemosses, quillworts) and Monilophyta (ferns, horsetails, whisk ferns).
Big idea
Vascular plants appeared about 425 million years ago with branched sporophytes that did not depend on the gametophyte. Living vascular plants have sporophyte-dominant life cycles, lignified xylem and living phloem, roots, leaves, and sporophylls. Seedless vascular plants still have flagellated sperm that swim through water, so they live mostly in damp places. They are two phyla: Lycophyta (club mosses, spikemosses, quillworts) and Monilophyta (ferns, horsetails, whisk ferns).

Must know

  • Guide Q9, origin: ancient relatives of vascular plants (such as Aglaophyton) had BRANCHED sporophytes that were not dependent on gametophytes; branching allowed multiple sporangia.
  • Guide Q9, the 5 traits of living vascular plants: (1) life cycles with dominant sporophytes, (2) transport in xylem and phloem, (3) well-developed roots, (4) well-developed leaves, (5) sporophylls (spore-bearing leaves).
  • (1) Dominant sporophyte: in ferns the leafy plant is the sporophyte; fern gametophytes are tiny and grow on or below the soil surface.
  • (2) Xylem conducts most water and minerals; includes tracheids; water-conducting cells are DEAD at functional maturity and lignified (walls strengthened by lignin). Phloem distributes sugars, amino acids and organic products; its cells are ALIVE at functional maturity.
  • (3) Roots replaced rhizoids: they absorb water and nutrients and anchor the plant; they may have evolved from the lowest stems.
  • (4) Leaves: MICROPHYLLS are small, spine-shaped, with a single unbranched vascular strand, found ONLY in lycophytes. MEGAPHYLLS have a highly branched vascular system, are larger and do more photosynthesis; almost all other vascular plants have them.
  • (5) Sporophylls = modified leaves that bear sporangia. Fern sporophylls carry sori (clusters of sporangia, usually on the undersides). Lycophytes and most gymnosperms cluster sporophylls into cones called strobili. In angiosperms the sporophylls are carpels and stamens.
  • Guide Q10, homosporous: one type of sporophyll, one type of sporangium, one type of spore, which typically develops into a bisexual gametophyte (most ferns, most seedless vascular plants).
  • Guide Q10, heterosporous: two types of sporophylls. Megasporophylls bear megasporangia, which make megaspores that develop into FEMALE gametophytes (eggs). Microsporophylls bear microsporangia, which make microspores (smaller) that develop into MALE gametophytes (sperm). All seed plants and a few seedless vascular plants are heterosporous.
  • Guide Q11, Phylum Lycophyta: club mosses, spikemosses, quillworts. Microphylls; strobili; many epiphytes; small survivors of giant Carboniferous lycophyte trees. Club mosses homosporous; spikemosses and quillworts heterosporous. Quillworts: one genus (Isoetes), marshy or submerged.
  • Guide Q11, Phylum Monilophyta (ferns, horsetails, whisk ferns and relatives): more closely related to seed plants than to lycophytes; shares megaphylls and roots that branch along their length.
  • Ferns: over 12,000 species, the most widespread seedless vascular plants; megaphyll fronds; fiddlehead (coiled young leaf); almost all homosporous; sori on leaf undersides; stalked sporangia catapult spores.
  • Horsetails: single genus Equisetum; jointed stems (arthrophytes); gritty "scouring rushes"; the stem is the main photosynthetic organ; air canals carry oxygen to roots; homosporous; separate fertile cone-bearing and vegetative stems.
  • Whisk ferns: Psilotum and Tmesipteris, the ONLY vascular plants lacking true roots; dichotomously branching stems; yellow knobs = three fused sporangia; homosporous; absorb through rhizoids. Once called living fossils, but DNA and sperm show they are close to ferns (roots were lost).

Terms

Xylem
Vascular tissue that conducts most water and minerals; its conducting cells are dead and lignified.
Tracheids
Tube-shaped xylem cells that carry water and minerals up from the roots.
Lignin
Polymer that strengthens xylem cell walls.
Phloem
Vascular tissue of living cells that distributes sugars, amino acids and other organic products.
Roots
Organs that absorb water and nutrients and anchor vascular plants.
Microphylls
Small, spine-shaped leaves with one unbranched vascular strand; only in lycophytes.
Megaphylls
Leaves with a highly branched vascular system.
Sporophylls
Modified leaves that bear sporangia.
Sori (sing. sorus)
Clusters of sporangia on the underside of fern sporophylls.
Strobili (sing. strobilus)
Cone-like clusters of sporophylls in lycophytes and most gymnosperms.
Homosporous
Producing one type of spore, which usually becomes a bisexual gametophyte.
Heterosporous
Producing two types of spores: megaspores (female gametophytes) and microspores (male gametophytes).
Megasporangium
Sporangium on a megasporophyll that produces megaspores.
Microsporangium
Sporangium on a microsporophyll that produces microspores.
Epiphytes
Plants that use other plants as a substrate but are not parasites.
Fiddlehead
The coiled tip of a growing fern frond.

Examples

  • Aglaophyton major: 405-million-year-old relative of vascular plants with dichotomous branching, sporangia at branch tips, rhizoids and stomata (Fig. 29.16).
  • Selaginella (spikemoss), Isoetes gunnii (quillwort), Diphasiastrum tristachyum (club moss with strobili): lycophytes.
  • Matteuccia struthiopteris (ostrich fern), Equisetum telmateia (giant horsetail), Psilotum nudum (whisk fern): monilophytes.
  • Hymenophyllum tunbrigense (filmy fern, megaphylls) versus Selaginella kraussiana (microphylls), Fig. 29.18.

  • Guide Q10: "Define homosporous and heterosporous. Define megasporangium and microsporangium. Be sure to include what each of these structures make." (p. 631)

Memory hooks
  • Don't eXpect Roots to Leave SoilGuide Q9 vascular traits: Dominant sporophyte, Xylem and phloem, Roots, Leaves, Sporophylls.
  • Xylem = eX-cells (dead); Phloem = Food, aliveXylem conducting cells are dead and lignified; phloem cells are alive and carry sugars.
  • Lyco toolbox: club, spike, quillPhylum Lycophyta = club mosses, spikemosses, quillworts.
  • Fern Has WhiskersPhylum Monilophyta = Ferns, Horsetails, Whisk ferns.
  • Microphyll = mini leaf, one vein, Lyco onlyMicrophylls have a single unbranched vascular strand and occur only in lycophytes.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Fig. 29.17 Fern life cycle: (1) sporangia release spores; one spore type grows into a bisexual photosynthetic gametophyte; (2) gametophyte has antheridia (sperm) and archegonia (eggs); (3) sperm swim to eggs, guided by an attractant; (4) zygote grows into a new sporophyte out of the archegonium; (5) sori on the underside of sporophylls hold sporangia, where meiosis makes spores.
  • Fig. 29.18: microphyll (one unbranched vein) versus megaphyll (branched veins).
  • Homospory versus heterospory flow diagram (p. 631): sporangium to single spore to bisexual gametophyte; megasporangium to megaspore to female gametophyte to eggs; microsporangium to microspore to male gametophyte to sperm.
  • Fig. 29.19: photos of lycophytes and monilophytes.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Aglaophyton major is 405 million years old and was under 20 cm tall.
  • Lignified vascular tissue let plants grow tall: support against gravity, water carried high, more sunlight, spores disperse farther. Nonvascular plants are typically under 5 cm. Competition led to the first forests 385 million years ago.
  • Dates: lycophytes had roots 400 million years ago; microphylls first appear 410 million years ago, megaphylls 370 million years ago.
  • Lycophyta has about 1,200 species; Carboniferous lycophyte trees were over 2 m wide and over 40 m tall; lycophyte roots branch dichotomously; oil-rich club moss spores were once ignited by magicians and photographers.
  • Some ferns produce over a trillion spores in a lifetime.
  • Carboniferous horsetails reached 15 m.
  • Significance: seedless vascular plants formed the first forests (Devonian, Carboniferous). Roots sped rock weathering that pulled CO2 into marine carbonates, causing Carboniferous global cooling. Undecayed swamp plants became peat then coal, now burned at 6 billion tons a year.
30.1

Seeds and pollen grains are key adaptations for life on land

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Name the five seed plant adaptations and reduced gametophyte advantagesList the five key adaptations of seed plants for life on land. Then give the two advantages of having a reduced gametophyte and explain each.List 5, then write 2 lines
  • Five adaptations (Q12): seeds
  • reduced gametophytes
  • heterospory
  • ovules
  • pollen. Reduced gametophyte advantages (Q13, 2 total): (1) Protection: the gametophyte develops from a spore kept inside the parent sporophyte's sporangium, so moist sporophyte tissue shields it from UV radiation and drying out. (2) Nutrition: it gets nutrients from the parent sporophyte. Contrast: free-living gametophytes of seedless vascular plants must fend for themselves.

Common mistake: Listing flowers or fruits among the five. Those are angiosperm adaptations (Q16); the five apply to all seed plants.

Full notes for 30.1
★ She said this is on the test
  • List the five key adaptations that are needed for life on land. (637)Seeds, reduced gametophytes, heterospory, ovules, pollen.
  • What are the advantages of having a reduced gametophyte? (2 total) (637-638)Protection from UV and drying inside the sporophyte, and nutrients from the parent sporophyte.
Big idea
Seed plants added five terrestrial adaptations: seeds, reduced gametophytes, heterospory, ovules and pollen. Their microscopic gametophytes develop inside the sporangia of the parent sporophyte, which protects them and feeds them. Pollen carries sperm without water, and a seed packages an embryo with food inside a protective coat.

Must know

  • Guide Q12, the five key adaptations of seed plants: (1) seeds, (2) reduced gametophytes, (3) heterospory, (4) ovules, (5) pollen.
  • Trend of gametophyte reduction: mosses gametophyte-dominant; ferns sporophyte-dominant with small free-living gametophyte; seed plants have microscopic gametophytes (Fig. 30.2).
  • Guide Q13, advantages of a reduced gametophyte (2): (1) PROTECTION: the gametophyte develops from a spore retained in the parent sporophyte's sporangium, so moist sporophyte tissues shield it from UV radiation and drying out; (2) NUTRITION: the gametophyte gets nutrients from the parent sporophyte. Free-living gametophytes of seedless vascular plants must fend for themselves.
  • Heterospory is the rule in seed plants: megasporangia on megasporophylls make megaspores (female gametophytes); microsporangia on microsporophylls make microspores (male gametophytes). Each megasporangium has ONE megaspore; each microsporangium has MANY microspores. Seed plants probably had homosporous ancestors.
  • Ovule = megasporangium + megaspore + integument(s). Seed plants are unique in RETAINING the megasporangium in the parent sporophyte. Integument = layer of sporophyte tissue around the megasporangium (gymnosperms one, angiosperms usually two). Inside the ovule a female gametophyte develops from the megaspore and makes one or more eggs.
  • Pollen grain = male gametophyte enclosed in the pollen wall (made of sporopollenin; outer layer from sporophyte cells). Pollination = transfer of pollen to the part of a seed plant that contains the ovules. A germinating pollen grain grows a pollen tube that discharges sperm into the female gametophyte.
  • Pollen removes the need for water for fertilization (carried by wind or animals), helping seed plants colonize dry habitats. Sperm of cycads and ginkgos still have flagella; most gymnosperms and all angiosperms lost them.
  • Seed = embryo + food supply + protective seed coat (from the integument). Ovule becomes a seed after fertilization.

Terms

Seed
An embryo packaged with a food supply inside a protective coat.
Reduced gametophyte
Microscopic gametophyte that develops inside the parent sporophyte's sporangia.
Integument
Layer of sporophyte tissue that envelops and protects the megasporangium; becomes the seed coat.
Ovule
Megasporangium, megaspore and integument(s) together; becomes a seed if fertilized.
Micropyle
The only opening through the integument; lets the pollen grain (or pollen tube) enter.
Pollen grain
Male gametophyte enclosed in a sporopollenin pollen wall.
Pollination
Transfer of pollen to the part of a seed plant that contains the ovules.
Pollen tube
Tube grown by a germinating pollen grain that delivers sperm to the female gametophyte.
Dormancy
Resting state in which a seed can survive until conditions favor germination.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Guide Q12: "List the five key adaptations that are needed for life on land." (p. 637)
  • Guide Q13: "What are the advantages of having a reduced gametophyte? (2 total)" (pp. 637-638)
  • Guide Q10 connects here: megasporangium makes megaspores (female gametophyte, eggs); microsporangium makes microspores (pollen, sperm). (p. 638)
Memory hooks
  • Some Really Happy Oaks PollinateGuide Q12, five seed plant adaptations: Seeds, Reduced gametophytes, Heterospory, Ovules, Pollen.
  • Reduced gametophyte gets the two Ps: Protected and Provided forGuide Q13: shielded from UV and drying; fed nutrients by the parent sporophyte.
  • Ovule = I, M, M: Integument, Megasporangium, MegasporeThe three parts of an ovule.
  • Seed = a baby in a lunchbox with a coatSeed = embryo + food supply + seed coat (from integument).
  • One mega, many microEach megasporangium has one megaspore; each microsporangium has many microspores.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Fig. 30.2: table and drawings of gametophyte versus sporophyte in mosses, ferns, and seed plants (gymnosperm and angiosperm).
  • Fig. 30.3: (a) unfertilized pine ovule: integument (2n), megasporangium (2n), megaspore (n), micropyle; (b) fertilized ovule: female gametophyte (n), egg nucleus, pollen tube discharging sperm; (c) gymnosperm seed: seed coat from integument, food supply (female gametophyte tissue, n), embryo (2n).
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Seeds versus spores: spores are usually single-celled; seeds are multicellular, have stored food, and can stay dormant for days to years (date palm seeds germinated after about 2,000 years). Seeds can be carried hundreds of kilometers.
  • A gymnosperm seed contains cells of three generations: seed coat (old sporophyte, 2n), food supply (female gametophyte, n), embryo (new sporophyte, 2n).
  • Fireweed (Chamerion angustifolium): seeds colonized the Mount St. Helens blast zone after the 1980 eruption (Fig. 30.1).
  • Date palm (Phoenix dactylifera): about 2,000-year-old seeds from a fortress near the Dead Sea germinated.
30.2

Gymnosperms bear "naked" seeds, typically on cones

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Build the gymnosperm phyla tableMake a table of the four gymnosperm phyla. For each give an example and its major characteristics. Mark which phyla have flagellated sperm.Draw a table on paper
  • Cycadophyta | Cycas revoluta | large cones, palmlike leaves, flagellated sperm, most endangered plant group. Ginkgophyta | Ginkgo biloba | one living species, fanlike deciduous leaves, flagellated sperm, tolerates pollution, smelly fleshy seeds. Gnetophyta | Gnetum, Ephedra, Welwitschia | three genera grouped by DNA
  • Gnetum tropical, flowering-plant-like leaves
  • Ephedra desert shrub, ephedrine
  • Welwitschia straplike leaves, southwestern African deserts. Coniferophyta | pines, firs, redwoods | largest phylum (about 600 species), woody cones, needle or scale leaves, mostly evergreen, wind-carried pollen.

Common mistake: Calling cycads palms. Cycads only have palmlike leaves; true palms are angiosperms. Also, not all conifers are evergreen (larch).

Define naked seed and the pine timelineDefine "naked seed". How long does a pine take, after young cones appear, to make male and female gametophytes, bring them together and form seeds?Write 2 to 3 lines on paper

Naked seed: a seed not enclosed in an ovary, exposed on sporophylls (cone scales); gymnos = naked, sperm = seed. Pine timeline: nearly THREE years from cone appearance to producing the gametophytes, bringing them together and forming mature seeds. Fertilization comes more than a year after pollination.

Common mistake: Answering one year. Fertilization comes just over a year after pollination; the whole process takes nearly three years.

Full notes for 30.2
★ She said this is on the test
  • List the four phyla of gymnosperms. List at least one example of each and all major characteristics (make a table). (642-643)Cycadophyta (Cycas), Ginkgophyta (Ginkgo biloba), Gnetophyta (Ephedra), Coniferophyta (pine), with traits.
  • Define "naked seed". How long does it take a pine tree to produce male and female gametophytes and bring them together after the young cones first appear on the plant? (640-641)Seed not enclosed in an ovary, exposed on cone scales; nearly three years.
Big idea
Gymnosperms have naked seeds exposed on sporophylls, usually in cones, not enclosed in ovaries. The four phyla are Cycadophyta, Ginkgophyta, Gnetophyta and Coniferophyta. The pine life cycle shows the seed plant adaptations: the tree is the sporophyte, pollen cones make microspores, ovulate cones make megaspores, and fertilization and seed formation take nearly three years.

Must know

  • Guide Q15, "naked seed": gymnosperm (gymnos = naked, sperm = seed) seeds are not enclosed in chambers (ovaries); they are exposed on sporophylls that usually form cones.
  • Guide Q15, timing: from the time pollen and ovulate cones appear on a pine, it takes NEARLY THREE YEARS for the male and female gametophytes to be produced and brought together and for mature seeds to form. Fertilization usually occurs more than a year after pollination.
  • Guide Q14, the 4 gymnosperm phyla (see compare table): Cycadophyta (cycads, Cycas revoluta), Ginkgophyta (Ginkgo biloba), Gnetophyta (Gnetum, Ephedra, Welwitschia), Coniferophyta (pines, firs, redwoods, sequoia, junipers).
  • Cycads (Cycadophyta, about 350 species): large cones, palmlike leaves (true palms are angiosperms), FLAGELLATED sperm; thrived in the Mesozoic; the MOST ENDANGERED plant group today.
  • Ginkgo: Ginkgo biloba is the only surviving species; maidenhair tree; flagellated sperm; deciduous fanlike leaves turn gold in autumn; tolerates air pollution (city tree); landscapers plant pollen trees because fleshy seeds smell rancid.
  • Gnetophytes (Gnetophyta): three genera grouped by molecular data. Gnetum (tropical, leaves like flowering plants), Ephedra (desert shrub, "Mormon tea", source of ephedrine, a decongestant), Welwitschia (deserts of southwestern Africa, straplike leaves, lives thousands of years).
  • Conifers (Coniferophyta): largest gymnosperm phylum, about 600 species: pines, firs, redwoods, sequoias, junipers. Mostly woody cones; needlelike or scalelike leaves; most evergreen, some deciduous (larch, tamarack, dawn redwood); pollen carried by wind; Douglas fir gives the most North American timber.

Terms

Gymnosperm
Seed plant whose seeds are naked, not enclosed in an ovary.
Conifers
Cone-bearing gymnosperms such as pines, firs and redwoods (phylum Coniferophyta).
Pollen cone
Small cone whose scales are microsporophylls bearing microsporangia.
Ovulate cone
Larger cone whose scales bear ovules (megasporangia).
Microsporocyte
Diploid cell in a microsporangium that undergoes meiosis to make microspores.
Megasporocyte
Diploid cell in a megasporangium that undergoes meiosis; one of the four products survives as the megaspore.
Evergreen
Keeping leaves throughout the year.
Deciduous
Losing leaves each autumn.

Examples

  • Cycas revoluta: cycad with a large cone and palmlike leaves.
  • Ginkgo biloba: maidenhair tree, only living ginkgophyte.
  • Gnetum, Ephedra (Mormon tea, ephedrine), Welwitschia mirabilis: the three gnetophyte genera.

  • Guide Q15: "Define 'naked seed'. How long does it take a pine tree to produce male and female gametophytes and bring them together after the young cones first appear on the plant?" (pp. 640-641)
Memory hooks

  • Cycads and Ginkgo still swimCycads and ginkgos keep flagellated sperm; most other seed plants lost them.
  • Gnets Enjoy Water-poor desertsGnetophyta genera: Gnetum, Ephedra, Welwitschia.
  • Gymno = gym clothes off = naked seedGymnosperm seeds are not enclosed in an ovary.
  • A pine needs nearly 3 yearsGuide Q15: nearly three years from cone appearance to gametophytes meeting and mature seeds; fertilization more than a year after pollination.
  • Ephedra eases stuffy nosesEphedra produces ephedrine, used as a decongestant.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Fig. 30.4 Pine life cycle: mature sporophyte (2n) with ovulate and pollen cones. In pollen cones, microsporocytes (2n) undergo meiosis to make microspores (n), and each microspore develops by mitosis into a pollen grain (male gametophyte). Ovulate scale with ovule; pollination; megasporocyte meiosis leaves one surviving megaspore; female gametophyte with archegonia; fertilization; seed (embryo 2n, food n, seed coat 2n); seedling.
  • Fig. 30.7: photos of the four gymnosperm phyla.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Pine life cycle: the pine tree is the sporophyte (2n); most trees have both cone types. Pollen cone scales are microsporophylls bearing microsporangia; microsporocytes (2n) undergo meiosis to make haploid microspores, and each microspore develops into a pollen grain (male gametophyte); wind carries the yellow pollen.
  • Ovulate cones: scales bear two ovules; megasporocyte (2n) in the megasporangium undergoes meiosis making four haploid cells; ONE survives as the megaspore; it develops into a female gametophyte with two or three archegonia, each forming an egg.
  • Pollination: pollen grain reaches the ovule (through the micropyle) and grows a pollen tube that slowly digests through the megasporangium. Fertilization: sperm and egg nuclei unite. All eggs may be fertilized, but usually only one zygote becomes an embryo. Ovule becomes a seed (embryo, food supply, seed coat); scales separate and wind disperses seeds.
  • Cycads: 75% of species are threatened; many are insect-pollinated.
  • Gnetophyte detail: Gnetum about 35 species, Ephedra about 40, Welwitschia mirabilis one species with leaves among the largest known. Some gnetophytes show a double fertilization that gives two embryos.
  • Conifer records: giant sequoia about 2,500 metric tons and 1,800 to 2,700 years old; coast redwood over 110 m; bristlecone pine (Pinus longaeva) over 5,000 years old; Wollemi pine found in 1994 (about 40 trees); juniper "berries" are fleshy ovule-producing cones.
  • History: Elkinsia, 360-million-year-old earliest seed plant; oldest extant gymnosperm lineage fossils 305 million years; gymnosperms replaced swamp plants as the Permian climate dried (thick cuticles, small needle leaf surface); dominated the Mesozoic; 110-million-year-old thrip fossil carried gymnosperm pollen (earliest insect pollination).
  • Douglas fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii), common juniper (Juniperus communis), European larch (Larix decidua, deciduous, survives -50 degrees C), Wollemi pine (Wollemia nobilis), giant sequoia (Sequoiadendron giganteum), coast redwood (Sequoia sempervirens), bristlecone pine (Pinus longaeva): conifers.
  • Elkinsia: earliest seed plant fossil, 360 million years old; Archaeopteris: heterosporous woody tree without seeds, not a seed plant.
30.3

The reproductive adaptations of angiosperms include flowers and fruits

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Name angiosperm key adaptations, define fruit, list dispersalWhat are the two key adaptations of phylum Anthophyta? What is a fruit, and what are the three major ways fruits are dispersed? Give an example of each.List from memory on paper
  • Key adaptations (Q16): flowers and fruits. Fruit (Q18): a mature (ripened) ovary
  • it protects seeds and aids dispersal (pea pod = fruit, peas = seeds). Dispersal (3): wind (dandelion parachutes, maple wings)
  • water (coconut)
  • animals (burrs such as cockleburs cling to fur
  • edible fruits are eaten and seeds pass in feces). Extra: some plants use explosive action.

Common mistake: Defining fruit as a mature ovule. The ovule becomes the seed; the ovary becomes the fruit.

Draw and label Figure 30.8, the flowerDraw and label figure 30.8, an idealized flower. Group the parts of the stamen and of the carpel, and give one function per organ.Draw and label on paper
  • Outer to inner on the receptacle: sepal (green, encloses the bud)
  • petal (bright, attracts pollinators)
  • stamen = anther (makes pollen) + filament (stalk)
  • carpel = stigma (sticky, receives pollen) + style + ovary (holds ovules). Label the ovule inside the ovary and the receptacle at the base. Sepals and petals are sterile. Stamen = microsporophyll
  • carpel = megasporophyll. Pistil = one carpel (simple) or fused carpels (compound).

Common mistake: Putting stigma, style or ovary in the stamen. Stamen = anther + filament only; stigma, style and ovary form the carpel.

Draw the angiosperm life cycle with double fertilizationDraw the angiosperm life cycle (figure 30.12). Show meiosis in anther and ovule, pollen grain and embryo sac contents, the pollen tube path, double fertilization with ploidy, and seed and fruit.Draw and label, then describe in lines
  • Anther: microsporocyte meiosis -> microspores -> pollen grain (tube cell + generative cell). Ovule: megasporocyte meiosis -> 4 megaspores, 1 survives -> 3 mitoses -> embryo sac (egg, synergids, central cell with two polar nuclei, antipodal cells). Pollen lands on the stigma and germinates
  • tube cell grows the pollen tube down the style
  • generative cell divides into two sperm. Tube enters the ovule through the MICROPYLE and releases both sperm. Double fertilization: sperm 1 + egg = 2n zygote (embryo)
  • sperm 2 + two polar nuclei = 3n endosperm (food). Ovule becomes seed
  • ovary becomes fruit
  • seed germinates into a new sporophyte (2n).

Common mistake: Writing sperm + one polar nucleus = 2n endosperm. The second sperm fuses with TWO polar nuclei, so endosperm is 3n.

Name the four angiosperm clades and compare monocots and eudicotsName the four major clades of angiosperms with one example each. Then make a column chart comparing monocots and eudicots on six traits.List, then draw a 3-column chart
  • Clades (Q20): basal angiosperms (Amborella, water lilies, star anise)
  • magnoliids (magnolias, laurels, black pepper)
  • monocots (orchids, grasses, palms)
  • eudicots (legumes, roses, oaks). Chart (Q21), trait | monocot | eudicot: cotyledons | 1 | 2
  • leaf veins | parallel | netlike
  • stem vascular bundles | scattered | ring
  • roots | fibrous | taproot
  • pollen openings | 1 | 3
  • floral organs | multiples of 3 | multiples of 4 or 5.

Common mistake: Swapping the stem patterns: monocot vascular bundles are scattered; eudicot bundles form a ring.

Full notes for 30.3
★ She said this is on the test
  • Draw and label figure 30.8. (644)Flower: sepal, petal, stamen (anther, filament), carpel (stigma, style, ovary), ovule, receptacle.
  • Describe double fertilization in angiosperms. Pay attention to detail! You are describing figure 30.12. (646)Sperm + egg = 2n zygote; sperm + two polar nuclei = 3n endosperm; pollen tube enters through the micropyle.
  • Know the general plant life cycle, moss life cycle and angiosperm life cycle.Angiosperm cycle: meiosis in anther and ovule, pollen grain, embryo sac, pollen tube, double fertilization, seed in fruit, sporophyte.
  • What are the two key adaptations of the phylum anthophyta? (644-645)Flowers and fruits.
  • What is a fruit? Name the major ways that fruit can be dispersed into the environment (3). (645)Fruit = mature ovary; dispersed by wind, water, animals.
  • What are the four major clades of angiosperms? (649-650)Basal angiosperms, magnoliids, monocots, eudicots.
  • What are the differing characteristics between monocots and eudicots? Make a chart by using columns. (649)Cotyledons 1/2, veins parallel/netlike, vascular bundles scattered/ring, roots fibrous/taproot, pollen 1/3 openings, flower parts 3s/4s or 5s.
Big idea
Angiosperms (phylum Anthophyta, more than 290,000 species, about 90% of plants) are seed plants with flowers and fruits. A flower has up to four floral organs: sepals, petals, stamens and carpels; the carpel encloses the ovules, and the ovary ripens into a fruit that protects and disperses seeds. Double fertilization makes a diploid zygote and a triploid endosperm. The major clades are basal angiosperms, magnoliids, monocots and eudicots.

Must know

  • Guide Q16, the two key adaptations of phylum Anthophyta: FLOWERS and FRUITS.
  • Flower: specialized shoot for sexual reproduction with up to four types of modified leaves (floral organs).
  • Guide Q17 (Fig. 30.8), outer to inner: SEPALS (usually green, enclose the bud), PETALS (bright, attract pollinators), STAMENS, CARPELS. Sepals and petals are sterile.
  • Stamen = microsporophyll: FILAMENT (stalk) + ANTHER (terminal sac where pollen is made).
  • Carpel = megasporophyll: STIGMA (sticky tip that receives pollen) + STYLE (leads down) + OVARY (base, holds one or more OVULES). The carpel is the "container" that distinguishes angiosperms from gymnosperms. Pistil = one carpel (simple) or fused carpels (compound). RECEPTACLE = base the floral organs attach to.
  • Guide Q18, fruit: a mature (ripened) ovary. Ovary wall thickens as ovules become seeds. Pea pod = fruit; peas = seeds (mature ovules). Fruits protect seeds and aid dispersal.
  • Guide Q18, three major ways fruits are dispersed: (1) WIND (dandelion parachutes, maple wings/propellers); (2) WATER (coconuts); (3) ANIMALS (burrs such as cockleburs hitchhike on fur; edible fruits eaten and seeds passed in feces with fertilizer). Some plants also disperse seeds by explosive action.
  • Angiosperm life cycle (Fig. 30.12): male gametophyte in the pollen grain has two haploid cells: generative cell (divides to form two sperm) and tube cell (makes the pollen tube). Female gametophyte = embryo sac, a few cells, one is the egg.
  • Megasporocyte in each ovule undergoes meiosis to make four megaspores; usually one survives and undergoes THREE mitotic divisions to form the female gametophyte (antipodal cells, two polar nuclei in the central cell, synergids, egg).
  • Guide Q19, double fertilization: pollen grain sticks to the stigma, absorbs water, germinates; tube cell makes a pollen tube down the style; tube enters the ovule through the MICROPYLE and discharges TWO sperm into the embryo sac. Sperm 1 + egg = diploid (2n) ZYGOTE. Sperm 2 + the two polar nuclei of the central cell = triploid (3n) cell that becomes ENDOSPERM (starch-rich food for the embryo). Unique to angiosperms.
  • After double fertilization: ovule becomes a seed (embryo with rudimentary root and one or two cotyledons, endosperm, seed coat from integuments); ovary becomes a fruit. Seedling uses food in endosperm and cotyledons until it photosynthesizes.
  • Guide Q20, four major clades: (1) BASAL ANGIOSPERMS (three lineages: Amborella trichopoda, water lilies, star anise and relatives); (2) MAGNOLIIDS (magnolias, laurels, black pepper); (3) MONOCOTS (orchids, grasses such as maize, rice, wheat, palms); (4) EUDICOTS (legumes such as peas and beans, roses, oak, walnut, maple, willow, birch).
  • "Dicots" are paraphyletic; most former dicots are eudicots ("true" dicots); the rest are basal angiosperms and magnoliids.
  • Guide Q21, monocots versus eudicots (see compare table): cotyledons 1 vs 2; leaf veins parallel vs netlike; stem vascular tissue scattered vs ring; roots fibrous vs taproot; pollen one opening vs three; floral organs multiples of three vs four or five.

Terms

Angiosperm
Flowering seed plant whose seeds develop inside an ovary (phylum Anthophyta).
Flower
Specialized shoot for sexual reproduction with up to four types of floral organs.
Sepals
Usually green floral organs that enclose the flower bud.
Petals
Usually brightly colored floral organs that attract pollinators.
Stamen
Microsporophyll made of a filament and an anther.
Anther
Terminal sac of a stamen where pollen is produced.
Filament
Stalk of a stamen.
Carpel
Megasporophyll made of stigma, style and ovary.
Stigma
Sticky tip of the carpel that receives pollen.
Style
Stalk leading from the stigma to the ovary.
Ovary
Base of the carpel containing one or more ovules; becomes the fruit.
Pistil
A single carpel (simple pistil) or two or more fused carpels (compound pistil).
Fruit
A mature ovary.
Pericarp
The wall of the ovary in a fruit.
Embryo sac
The female gametophyte of an angiosperm.
Generative cell
Pollen cell that divides to form two sperm.
Tube cell
Pollen cell that produces the pollen tube.
Double fertilization
One sperm fertilizes the egg (zygote, 2n) and the other fuses with the two polar nuclei (endosperm, 3n).
Endosperm
Triploid food tissue that nourishes the angiosperm embryo.
Cotyledons
Seed leaves of the embryo; one in monocots, two in eudicots.
Cross-pollination
Transfer of pollen from a flower on one plant to the stigma of a flower on another plant of the same species.
Basal angiosperms
Amborella, water lilies, and star anise and relatives; lineages that diverged early.
Eudicots
"True" dicots; the largest angiosperm clade.

Examples

  • Daffodil (radial symmetry) and orchid (bilateral symmetry), Fig. 30.9; Scottish broom tripping its stamens onto a bee, Fig. 30.15.
  • Fruits: tomato, ruby grapefruit, nectarine (fleshy); hazelnut (dry, closed); milkweed (dry, splits open).
  • Dispersal: maple wings (wind), coconut (water), cockleburs and berries (animals).
  • Amborella trichopoda: small shrub of New Caledonia, sole survivor of the branch at the base of the angiosperm tree.
  • Water lily (Nymphaea), star anise (Illicium), southern magnolia (Magnolia grandiflora), orchid, barley (Hordeum vulgare), pygmy date palm, snow pea (Pisum sativum), dog rose (Rosa canina), Armenian oak (Quercus pontica), Fig. 30.17.

  • Guide, last line: "Know ... angiosperm life cycle" (Fig. 30.12).
Memory hooks
  • Some People Smell CarnationsFloral organs from outside in: Sepals, Petals, Stamens, Carpels.
  • Anther on a Filament: A Flag on a poleStamen = anther (pollen sac) on top of a filament (stalk).
  • Sticky Stigma, Slender Style, Ovary holds OvulesCarpel parts top to bottom.
  • 1 + 1 = 2n, 1 + 2 = 3nDouble fertilization: one sperm + egg = 2n zygote; one sperm + two polar nuclei = 3n endosperm.
  • Seeds travel by the three Ws: Wind, Water, WildlifeGuide Q18 dispersal: wind (maple, dandelion), water (coconut), animals (burrs, eaten fruits).
  • Big Magnolias Make Everything; monocots love 3sGuide Q20 clades: Basal angiosperms, Magnoliids, Monocots, Eudicots. Monocots: 1 cotyledon, floral parts in 3s; eudicots: 2 cotyledons, parts in 4s or 5s.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Fig. 30.8 Idealized flower: label stamen (anther, filament), carpel/simple pistil (stigma, style, ovary), ovule, petal, sepal, receptacle.
  • Fig. 30.10 fruit structure and Fig. 30.11 seed dispersal adaptations.
  • Fig. 30.12 Angiosperm life cycle (7 steps): (1) megasporocyte meiosis gives four megaspores, one survives and divides three times by mitosis into the embryo sac; (2) in the anther, microsporocytes undergo meiosis to make four microspores; (3) microspore becomes a pollen grain with generative cell and tube cell; (4) pollen tube grows through the style and discharges two sperm in the ovule; (5) double fertilization: zygote (2n) and endosperm (3n); (6) zygote becomes an embryo packaged with food in a seed; (7) seed germinates into a mature sporophyte.
  • Fig. 30.14b angiosperm phylogeny: Amborella, water lilies and star anise (basal angiosperms) branch first, then magnoliids, monocots and eudicots.
  • Fig. 30.16 monocot versus eudicot traits; Fig. 30.17 angiosperm diversity photos.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Animal pollination is more directed than the wind pollination of most gymnosperms; grasses and dense temperate trees are wind-pollinated.
  • Flower symmetry: radial (daffodil, any line through the center) versus bilateral (orchid, only one line). Bilateral clades had on average nearly 2,400 more species than related radial clades.
  • Fleshy fruits (pericarp softens): tomato, plum, grape, ruby grapefruit, nectarine. Dry fruits: beans, nuts, grains; some split open (milkweed), some stay closed (hazelnut). Cereal grains (maize, rice, wheat) are fruits whose dry ovary wall sticks to the seed coat.
  • Cross-pollination = pollen from the anther of a flower on one plant to the stigma of a flower on another plant of the same species; enhances genetic variability.
  • Function hypothesis for double fertilization: synchronizes food storage with embryo development, so the plant does not waste nutrients on infertile ovules.
  • Gnetophyte double fertilization (some species) gives TWO embryos, not embryo plus endosperm.
  • Angiosperm history: originated about 140 million years ago (early Cretaceous); oldest angiosperm pollen 130 million years; Archaefructus and Leefructus 125 million years (China); dominant by 100 million years ago. Darwin called their origin an "abominable mystery." Common ancestor probably woody, not aquatic. Bennettitales may be the sister group.
  • Archaefructus sinensis: 125-million-year-old aquatic flowering plant fossil.
  • Clade sizes (lower priority): basal angiosperms about 100 species; magnoliids about 8,500 (spiral floral organs); monocots about 72,000 (one quarter of angiosperms); eudicots about 210,000 (over two thirds).
30.4

Human welfare depends on seed plants

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Atropine source and threats to plant diversityWhich plant does atropine come from, and what is it used for? Then describe the main threats to plant diversity and what is lost.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Atropine (Q22): from the belladonna plant
  • dilates the pupil of the eye. Threats (Q23): the growing human population and its demand for space and resources destroys habitat, especially by clear-cutting tropical forests (worst in the tropics
  • nearly half already cut). Results: plant extinctions plus the insects and animals that depend on them
  • less CO2 absorbed by photosynthesis (possible global warming)
  • loss of unexplored foods and medicines. Plants are renewable, but plant diversity is nonrenewable: an extinct species never returns.

Common mistake: Saying atropine constricts the pupil. Atropine, from belladonna, dilates the pupil.

Full notes for 30.4
★ She said this is on the test

Big idea
Seed plants supply food, wood, fuel and medicines. Atropine comes from the belladonna plant and is used to dilate the pupil of the eye. Human population growth threatens plant diversity, especially through clearing of tropical forests, and lost species cannot return.

Must know

  • Guide Q22: ATROPINE comes from the BELLADONNA plant and is used as an EYE PUPIL DILATOR (Table 30.1).
  • Guide Q23, threats to plant diversity: the growing human population and its demand for space and resources destroys habitat, especially by clear-cutting tropical forests. The threat is worst in the tropics, where over two thirds of humans live and population grows fastest. Nearly half of Earth's tropical forests have already been cut.
  • Forest loss reduces CO2 absorption by photosynthesis, possibly adding to global warming; plant species go extinct, and with them insects and other rain forest animals.
  • What is lost: plant species go extinct along with insects and other animals that depend on them; only a tiny fraction of plants has been explored for food and medicines (our food relies on about two dozen species), so potential foods and medicines disappear. Plants are renewable, but plant DIVERSITY is not: an extinct species never returns.

Terms

Atropine
Compound from the belladonna plant used to dilate the pupil of the eye.
Nonrenewable resource
Plant diversity: once a species is extinct it can never return.
Clear-cutting
Removing all trees from a forest area, as in tropical deforestation.

Examples

  • Belladonna: source of atropine, the eye pupil dilator (Table 30.1).
  • Brazil satellite images 1975 versus 2012 (Fig. 30.18).

Extra detail, lower priority
  • Table 30.1: digitalin (foxglove, heart medication), menthol (eucalyptus tree, throat soother), quinine (cinchona tree, malaria preventive), taxol (Pacific yew, ovarian cancer drug), tubocurarine (curare tree, muscle relaxant), vinblastine (periwinkle, leukemia drug).
  • Willow bark contains salicin; its synthetic derivative acetylsalicylic acid is aspirin. About 25% of U.S. prescription drugs contain a plant ingredient.
  • Six crops (maize, rice, wheat, potatoes, cassava, sweet potatoes) give 80% of human calories; domestication began about 12,000 years ago. Wood (tough-walled xylem) is absent in all living seedless plants.
  • About 63,000 km2 (15 million acres) of tropical rain forest cleared each year; at that rate the remaining 11 million km2 would be gone in 175 years. Nearly half of Earth's tropical forests already cut (Fig. 30.18, Brazil 1975 vs 2012).
  • If current rates continue, 50% or more of Earth's species may go extinct in the next few centuries, a mass extinction rivaling the Permian and Cretaceous.
  • Fewer than 5,000 plant species have been studied for medicines, out of about 325,000 known plant species.
