  • About 63,000 km2 of tropical forest is cleared yearly; at that rate the rest would be gone in about 175 years.
Ch 31

Exam sheet: Chapter 31: Fungi

Memorize this, review it last
  1. Fungi = heterotrophs by ABSORPTION: secrete hydrolytic enzymes outside, absorb small molecules.
  2. Three roles: decomposers, mutualists, parasites/pathogens.
  3. Hypha/hyphae, septum/septa; septate (pores, nuclei stay) vs coenocytic (no septa, multinucleate); walls of CHITIN, not cellulose.
  4. Mycelium maximizes surface-to-volume ratio; hyphae grow in length, not girth; multicellular fungi are not motile.
  5. Arbuscules: through the plant cell WALL, not the plasma MEMBRANE. Ectomycorrhizae: sheath, between cortex cells. Most vascular plants depend on mycorrhizae (fungus gives phosphate and minerals, plant gives organic nutrients).
  6. DRAW the generalized life cycle: mycelium (n); asexual loop spore-producing structures, spores (n); sexual loop plasmogamy, heterokaryotic n+n, karyogamy, zygote 2n, meiosis, spores n.
  7. Generalized lecture cycle: haploid is normal and the diploid zygote is brief; meiosis gives four haploid products. Good conditions favor asexual reproduction; poor conditions favor sexual reproduction and variation. Neurospora adds mitosis to make eight ascospores. Mating types + and -.
  8. Plasmogamy = cytoplasm fuses; karyogamy = nuclei fuse (later). Dikaryotic = heterokaryotic here.
  9. Asexual reproduction: molds make spores by mitosis; yeasts bud without making spores. Deuteromycetes have no known sexual stage.
  10. Ancestor: aquatic, single-celled, flagellated protist. Fungi closer to animals (opisthokonts, Unikonta).
  11. Lineage order: cryptomycetes + microsporidians (basal), chytrids (zoospores), zoopagomycetes, mucoromycetes, ascomycetes + basidiomycetes. Flagella lost with move to land.
  12. DRAW Rhizopus: sporangia (asexual); + / - plasmogamy, young zygosporangium (n+n), karyogamy (2n), dormant zygosporangium (inactive, resists freezing and drying), meiosis, sporangium, haploid spores. Mucoromycota = old Zygomycota.
  13. DRAW Neurospora crassa: conidia (asexual naked spores); plasmogamy, dikaryotic hyphae, ascus, karyogamy, meiosis (4), mitosis (8 ascospores), ascocarp. Genome published 2003.
  14. DRAW the mushroom sexual cycle: dikaryotic mycelium, basidiocarp, gills, basidium, karyogamy, meiosis, 4 basidiospores.
  15. Structure to group: zygosporangium = mucoromycetes; ascus = ascomycetes (sac); basidium = basidiomycetes (club); zoospore = chytrids; harpoon = microsporidians.
  16. Mycorrhizal trend: mucoromycetes, ascomycetes and basidiomycetes include mutualists. Fairy rings expand as underground mycelium grows; chytrid skin infections harm amphibian respiration.
  17. Lichens: fungus (usually ascomycete) + green algae or cyanobacteria; crustose, foliose, fruticose; soredia or fragmentation; pioneers; own binomial names.
  18. Diseases: ergot on rye (Claviceps purpurea), corn smut, tar spot; ringworm and athlete's foot; valley fever (systemic, lungs, water trucks); Candida (opportunistic).
  19. Uses: penicillin from Penicillium; ergot compound lowers blood pressure and stops bleeding after childbirth; S. cerevisiae makes human glycoproteins; diesel-like biofuel; food.
  20. not required: nucleariids, the term conidiophore, glomeromycete details, dikaryotic vs heterokaryotic difference.
31.1

Fungi are heterotrophs that feed by absorption

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Explain how fungi get their foodHow do fungi obtain nutrients? Describe the steps of absorptive nutrition, then name the three ecological roles fungi play and where each one gets its nutrients.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Fungi are heterotrophs that feed by ABSORPTION, their most important shared trait
  • they secrete hydrolytic enzymes to the outside
  • the enzymes break complex molecules into smaller organic molecules
  • the fungus absorbs those small molecules
  • decomposers absorb nutrients from nonliving organic material
  • parasites absorb nutrients from living hosts
  • mutualists absorb nutrients from a host but give something back that helps the host.

Common mistake: Fungi do not ingest food like animals. They digest outside the body with secreted enzymes, then absorb the small molecules.

Describe the fungal body: hyphae, septa, myceliumDescribe a multicellular fungus from hypha to mushroom. Include singular and plural terms, the wall polymer, septate versus coenocytic hyphae, why a mycelium absorbs well, and how hyphae grow and spread.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Most fungi are multicellular filaments, some are single-celled yeasts
  • hypha singular, hyphae plural
  • septum singular, septa plural
  • hyphal walls are strengthened by CHITIN, not cellulose (cellulose is in plants)
  • chitin keeps cells from bursting under osmotic pressure during absorption
  • septate hyphae: septa have pores that let cytoplasm and organelles pass, but nuclei stay in their compartment
  • coenocytic hyphae: no septa, many nuclei in one continuous cytoplasm (multinucleate)
  • mycelium = interwoven mass of hyphae that maximizes surface-to-volume ratio, making absorption very efficient
  • hyphae grow in LENGTH, not girth, by cytoplasmic streaming to the tips
  • multicellular fungi are not motile, they spread by growing
  • a mushroom is the reproductive structure of an underground mycelium, still made of hyphae.

Common mistake: Fungal walls are chitin, not cellulose, and hyphae grow longer, not wider. Fungi spread by growing, not by moving.

Compare specialized hyphae, arbuscules and mycorrhizaeName the specialized hyphae fungi use to trap animals, to take nutrients from plants, and to trade with plant roots. What do mycorrhizal partners exchange, and how far does an arbuscule penetrate?List from memory, then add 2 lines
  • Ring-shaped hyphae act as a noose to trap nematodes (animal feeding)
  • haustoria extract nutrients from living plants
  • arbuscules = branched hyphae of mutualistic fungi that exchange nutrients with plant hosts
  • arbuscules push through the plant cell WALL but not the plasma MEMBRANE, which wraps around the fungus
  • mycorrhizae ("fungus roots") = mutually beneficial fungus and plant root partnerships
  • fungus gives phosphate ions and minerals, plant gives organic nutrients
  • most vascular plants depend on mycorrhizae
  • ectomycorrhizal fungi form a sheath of hyphae over the root and grow between cortex cells
  • arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi go through the root cell wall and push in (invaginate) the plasma membrane.

Common mistake: Arbuscules do not enter the cell itself. They cross the cell wall, but the plant plasma membrane stays intact around them.

Full notes for 31.1
★ She said this is on the test
  • The most important key trait is the way in which they get their nutrition.Fungi are heterotrophs that secrete hydrolytic enzymes and absorb the small molecules released.
  • Now, you need to be aware that most vascular plants are going to depend upon mycorrhizae.Most vascular plants depend on mycorrhizae (fungus gives phosphate and minerals, plant gives organic nutrients).
  • So they're not going to grow in diameter, but in length.Hyphae grow in length, not girth; multicellular fungi are not motile and spread by growing.
  • So it does not penetrate into the cell itself but it does penetrate the cell wall.Arbuscules pass through the plant cell wall but not the plasma membrane.
  • The structure of the mycelium actually will maximize surface to volume ratio, making absorption very efficient.Mycelium of thin hyphae maximizes surface-to-volume ratio for absorption.
  • And hyphae is plural.hypha singular, hyphae plural; septum singular, septa plural.
Big idea
Fungi are a diverse group, but they share one way of getting food: they are heterotrophs that absorb nutrients from outside their bodies. They secrete hydrolytic enzymes that break complex molecules into small organic molecules, then absorb them. Most fungi grow as multicellular filaments called hyphae (a few are single-celled yeasts), and a mass of hyphae, the mycelium, gives a high surface-to-volume ratio for absorption. Some fungi have specialized hyphae for trapping animals or for feeding on or trading nutrients with plants (haustoria, arbuscules, mycorrhizae).

Must know

  • Fungi are heterotrophs that feed by ABSORPTION, their most important shared trait. They secrete hydrolytic enzymes to the outside, the enzymes break complex molecules into smaller organic molecules, and the fungus absorbs them.
  • Three ecological roles: decomposers (absorb nutrients from nonliving organic material), parasites (absorb nutrients from living hosts), mutualists (absorb nutrients from a host but give something back that helps the host).
  • Body forms: multicellular filaments (most common) or single cells called yeasts. Some fungi grow as both at some point in the life cycle.
  • Hyphae (singular hypha) are networks of tiny filaments with tubular cell walls strengthened by CHITIN, not cellulose (cellulose is in plants). Chitin helps keep cells from bursting under the osmotic pressure built up during nutrient absorption.
  • Septate hyphae: cross walls called septa (singular septum) divide hyphae into cells; septa have pores that let cytoplasm and organelles pass, but in her framing the nucleus stays in its compartment. Coenocytic fungi lack septa, so hundreds or thousands of nuclei share one continuous cytoplasm (multinucleate).
  • Mycelium = interwoven mass of hyphae that infiltrates the food source. It maximizes surface-to-volume ratio, making absorption efficient.
  • Hyphae grow in LENGTH, not girth, using cytoplasmic streaming to move materials to the tips. Multicellular fungi are not motile; they colonize new territory by growing hyphae into it.
  • Mushrooms are the reproductive structures of large underground mycelia, and they are still made of hyphae.
  • Specialized hyphae: ring-shaped hyphae that act as a noose to trap nematodes (animal feeding); haustoria that extract nutrients from plants (plant feeding).
  • Mutualistic fungi have specialized branching hyphae called arbuscules that exchange nutrients with plant hosts. Arbuscules push through the plant cell wall but not through the plasma membrane; the membrane wraps around the fungus.
  • Mycorrhizae ("fungus roots") are mutually beneficial fungus and plant root partnerships: the fungus delivers phosphate ions and minerals, the plant supplies organic nutrients. Most vascular plants depend on mycorrhizae.
  • Ectomycorrhizal fungi form sheaths of hyphae over the root surface and grow into the extracellular spaces of the root cortex. Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi go through the root cell wall and form tubes by pushing in (invaginating) the root cell plasma membrane.

Terms

heterotroph
An organism that gets its food from other organisms; fungi do this by absorbing nutrients.
absorption (fungal nutrition)
Fungi take nutrients into the body from outside after digesting them externally.
hydrolytic enzymes
Enzymes fungi secrete to the outside that break complex molecules into smaller organic molecules that can be absorbed.
decomposer
A fungus that breaks down and absorbs nutrients from nonliving organic material.
parasitic fungus
A fungus that absorbs nutrients from a living host.
mutualistic fungus
A fungus that absorbs nutrients from a host but gives back actions or products that benefit the host.
yeast
A single-celled fungus; yeasts live in moist environments rich in soluble nutrients like sugars and amino acids.
hypha (plural hyphae)
A tiny tubular filament that makes up the fungal body.
chitin
The structural polymer in fungal cell walls (not cellulose) that strengthens hyphae and helps keep cells from bursting from osmotic pressure.
septum (plural septa)
A cross wall that divides a hypha into cells; it has pores for cytoplasm and organelles to pass, but nuclei stay in place.
septate hyphae
Hyphae divided into cells by septa.
coenocytic fungi
Fungi with no septa, so many nuclei share one continuous cytoplasm (multinucleate).
mycelium (plural mycelia)
An interwoven mass of hyphae that infiltrates the food source and maximizes surface-to-volume ratio.
cytoplasmic streaming
Movement of cytoplasm that carries materials to the growing tips of hyphae.
haustoria
Specialized hyphae that let a fungus extract nutrients from plant cells.
arbuscule
A specialized branching hypha of a mutualistic fungus that penetrates the plant cell wall (not the plasma membrane) to exchange nutrients.
mycorrhizae
Mutually beneficial relationships between fungi and plant roots ("fungus roots").
ectomycorrhizal fungi
Mycorrhizal fungi that form a sheath of hyphae over the root and grow between cells of the root cortex, without entering the cells.
arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi
Mycorrhizal fungi whose arbuscules pass through root cell walls and push in the plasma membrane to form tubes.

Examples

  • Mushrooms on the forest floor: the small aboveground extension (reproductive structure) of a vast network of hyphae below ground.
  • Armillaria bulbosa (animation): mycelia reported to spread over hundreds of acres, showing how huge mycelia can be.
  • Nematode-trapping fungus: ring-like hyphae act as a noose that tightens around a nematode so the fungus can feed on it.
  • Mold on bread or leaves (animation): mycelia release digestive enzymes into decaying matter, liberating sugars and amino acids.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "The most important key trait is the way in which they get their nutrition." Fungi absorb nutrients after secreting enzymes.
  • Hyphae is plural, hypha is singular; septa is plural, septum is singular.
  • Chitin, not cellulose, is in fungal walls: "chitin is different from cellulose... cellulose we're going to see in plants."
  • The mycelium "will maximize surface to volume ratio, making absorption very efficient."
  • Arbuscules go through the plant cell wall but "does not penetrate into the cell itself." The plasma membrane stays outside the fungus.
  • "You need to be aware that most vascular plants are going to depend upon mycorrhizae." Mycorrhizae come back in the plant chapters.
  • Hyphae grow in length, not in diameter, and multicellular fungi are not motile.
Memory hooks
  • Fungi Digest Outside, Absorb InsideFungi secrete hydrolytic enzymes to digest food outside the body, then absorb the small molecules.
  • Dead, Parasite, PartnerThe three fungal roles: decomposers eat the dead, parasites take from a living host, mutualists give back to the host.
  • Chitin for Champignons, cellulose for cabbageFungal walls are made of chitin; cellulose is the plant wall polymer.
  • Septa = separated rooms; Coeno = one big common roomSeptate hyphae are divided into cells by septa with pores; coenocytic hyphae have no septa and many nuclei in one cytoplasm.
  • Grow long, not wideHyphae grow in length, not girth, so the mycelium has a huge surface-to-volume ratio for absorption.
  • Ecto = exterior sleeve; Arbuscular = breaks the wall, not the membraneEctomycorrhizae sheath the root and grow between cortex cells; arbuscules pass through the cell wall but only push in the plasma membrane.

  • Concept page figure "How do structure and function in fungi relate to their role in ecosystems?": a mushroom over soil with branching hyphae. Extra detail, lower priority
  • Enzymes can digest compounds from living or dead sources.
  • Extracellular digestion animation: enzymes break cellulose, RNA, proteins and fats into glucose, nucleotides, amino acids and fatty acids, which are absorbed for energy or as building blocks. (The animation narration says "nucleic acids" for the RNA product, a source error: RNA already is a nucleic acid; its absorbable subunits are nucleotides.)
  • Yeasts live in moist places with plentiful soluble nutrients such as sugars or amino acids. Some fungi grow as both filaments and yeasts at some point in the life cycle.
  • Mycorrhizal fungi colonize soils by dispersing haploid cells called spores.

31.2

Fungi produce spores through sexual or asexual life cycles

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Draw the generalized fungal life cycle (Figure 31.5)Draw the generalized fungal life cycle starting from a haploid mycelium. Show the asexual loop and the sexual loop, name each fusion event, and label every stage n, n+n or 2n.Draw and label on paper
  • Start: mycelium (n). Asexual loop: mycelium makes spore-producing structures
  • they release haploid spores (n) made by mitosis
  • spores germinate into a new mycelium. Sexual loop: hyphae of + and - mating types meet
  • plasmogamy (cytoplasm fuses)
  • heterokaryotic (dikaryotic) stage (n+n), nuclei not yet fused
  • karyogamy (nuclei fuse, later)
  • diploid zygote (2n), the only diploid cell
  • meiosis
  • haploid spores (n), 4 per diploid cell
  • spores germinate into mycelium (n). Both loops end in haploid spores.

Common mistake: n+n is not 2n. After plasmogamy the two nuclei stay separate; only karyogamy makes the diploid zygote.

Explain when fungi reproduce sexually versus asexuallyWhat is the normal ploidy of fungi? Which conditions trigger asexual versus sexual reproduction, which one gives genetic variation, and how do fungal mating types differ from male and female?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Haploid is the normal fungal state
  • the diploid zygote is short-lived (transient) and occurs only in the sexual cycle
  • good environmental conditions = asexual reproduction, genetically identical spores
  • unfavorable conditions = sexual reproduction
  • asexual reproduction gives no genetic variation
  • karyogamy plus meiosis give variation in sexual reproduction
  • mating types are plus (+) and minus (-), no males and females
  • pheromones signal mating type
  • plasmogamy = cytoplasm fuses first
  • karyogamy = nuclei fuse hours, days or centuries later
  • dikaryotic and heterokaryotic are used interchangeably here (the difference is not required).

Common mistake: Students reverse the conditions. Good conditions bring asexual reproduction; poor conditions bring sexual reproduction.

Name asexual reproduction in molds, yeasts and deuteromycetesHow do molds and single-celled yeasts reproduce asexually, and which one makes no spores? What is a deuteromycete, and what happens when its sexual stage is found?Write 3 lines on paper
  • Molds make haploid spores asexually by mitosis and form visible, furry mycelia (mold on an orange reproduces only asexually)
  • single-celled yeasts reproduce asexually WITHOUT spores, by simple cell division or budding: a small bud cell pinches off the parent
  • deuteromycetes = yeasts and filamentous fungi with no known sexual stage
  • they are reclassified when a sexual stage is found
  • deuteromycete is now only a descriptive term, and genomic techniques are used to classify fungi.

Common mistake: Yeasts do not make spores when they reproduce asexually; they bud. Molds are the ones making asexual spores by mitosis.

Full notes for 31.2
★ She said this is on the test
  • Here we have a generalized lifecycle of fungi. Make sure that you know this particular figure.Draw and label the generalized fungal life cycle: asexual and sexual loops with n, n+n, 2n.
  • Fungi are haploid, so that is a fact that you need to remember.Haploid is the normal fungal state; the diploid zygote is short-lived.
  • If good environmental conditions are present, asexual reproduction will occur. If environmental conditions are not favorable, then sexual reproduction will happen.Good conditions = asexual (identical spores); unfavorable conditions = sexual.
  • Asexual reproduction will not give us any genetic variation.For the lecture cycle, asexual reproduction produces genetically identical offspring; karyogamy plus meiosis produces variation in sexual reproduction.
  • There's no males and females. We recognize them as plus and minus.Fungal mating types are + and -.
  • I'm not going to, um, ask you guys to know the difference between, uh, [dikaryotic] and [heterokaryotic].not required: the dikaryotic vs heterokaryotic distinction; both mean n+n here.
Big idea
Fungi make huge numbers of spores, by sexual or asexual life cycles, that are carried by wind or water and germinate into new mycelia when they land in a moist place with food. The normal state of fungal nuclei is haploid. When conditions are good, fungi reproduce asexually and make genetically identical spores; when conditions are poor, hyphae of different mating types fuse (plasmogamy), form an n+n heterokaryotic (dikaryotic) stage, then fuse nuclei (karyogamy) to make a short-lived diploid zygote that undergoes meiosis to make haploid spores. Molds reproduce asexually by mitosis, yeasts by budding, and fungi with no known sexual stage are called deuteromycetes.

Must know

  • Fungi make vast numbers of spores, by sexual or asexual life cycles. Wind or water carries spores long distances; in a moist place with food they germinate into new mycelia.
  • In the generalized fungal cycle taught here, fungal nuclei and spores are HAPLOID; haploid is the normal fungal state ("a fact that you need to remember"). Diploid nuclei are TRANSIENT and occur only in the sexual cycle.
  • Asexual cycle: haploid mycelium (n) makes spore-producing structures that release haploid spores; spores germinate into a new mycelium. In the generalized cycle everything here is haploid, and offspring are genetically identical.
  • Good environmental conditions = asexual reproduction keeps going. Unfavorable conditions = sexual reproduction.
  • Sexual reproduction requires fusion of hyphae from DIFFERENT MATING TYPES, plus (+) and minus (-), not male and female. Many fungi signal their mating type with pheromones.
  • Plasmogamy = union (fusion) of the cytoplasm of two parent mycelia.
  • In most fungi the nuclei do not fuse right away. The cell holds two genetically different haploid nuclei: the heterokaryotic stage (n+n). If the nuclei pair off one from each parent per cell, the cell is dikaryotic. For this class the terms are used interchangeably.
  • Karyogamy = fusion of the nuclei, hours, days or even centuries after plasmogamy. It makes a diploid (2n) zygote.
  • The short-lived zygote undergoes meiosis, giving haploid spores. Each diploid cell gives 4 spores.
  • In the lecture cycle, karyogamy and meiosis together produce genetic variation in fungi; her wording: "Asexual reproduction will not give us any genetic variation."
  • Molds make haploid spores asexually by mitosis (mold on an orange reproduces only asexually). Single-celled yeasts reproduce asexually WITHOUT spores by budding: a small bud cell pinches off the parent.
  • Deuteromycetes = yeasts and filamentous fungi with no known sexual stage. They are reclassified when a sexual stage is found. Deuteromycete is now only a descriptive term; genomic techniques are used to classify fungi.

Terms

spore
A haploid reproductive cell, made sexually or asexually, carried by wind or water.
germination
Growth of a spore into hyphae that form a new mycelium.
spore-producing structure (sporangium)
The structure on the mycelium that makes and releases spores.
mating types (+ and -)
The two compatible strains of a fungus whose hyphae must fuse for sexual reproduction; fungi have no males or females.
pheromones
Signaling molecules fungi release to communicate their mating type to the opposite mating type.
plasmogamy
Fusion of the cytoplasm of two parent mycelia of different mating types.
heterokaryon / heterokaryotic stage
A cell or mycelium with coexisting, genetically different haploid nuclei that have not fused (n+n).
dikaryotic
Having two haploid nuclei per cell, one from each parent; used interchangeably with heterokaryotic in this class.
n+n
Notation for the heterokaryotic or dikaryotic condition: two separate haploid nuclei in one cell.
karyogamy
Fusion of the two haploid nuclei to form a diploid nucleus.
zygote (fungal)
The short-lived diploid cell made by karyogamy; it undergoes meiosis right away.
meiosis (fungal)
Division of the diploid zygote that gives haploid spores, four per diploid cell in the generalized lecture cycle.
mold
A fungus that produces haploid spores asexually by mitosis.
budding
Asexual reproduction of yeast by simple cell division, where a small cell pinches off the parent; no spores are made.
deuteromycetes
Yeasts and filamentous fungi with no known sexual stage; now a descriptive term, not a formal group.

Examples

  • Mold on an orange: a mold that reproduces asexually by mitosis.
  • Yeast cell with a bud: asexual reproduction by budding, no spores.
  • Baker's or brewer's yeast, Saccharomyces cerevisiae (Ascomycota video): reproduces asexually by budding in rich conditions and sexually when mating types a and alpha meet; yeast conjugation skips the dikaryotic stage.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "Here we have a generalized lifecycle of fungi. Make sure that you know this particular figure." It comes back in lab.
  • "Fungi are haploid, so that is a fact that you need to remember." The diploid stage is transient.
  • Good conditions = asexual; unfavorable conditions = sexual.
  • Mating types are plus and minus: "There's no males and females."
  • "I'm not going to, um, ask you guys to know the difference between, uh, [dikaryotic] and [heterokaryotic]. Um, for our purposes within this class, we're going to use the terms interchangeably."
  • "Asexual reproduction will not give us any genetic variation." In her lecture cycle, variation comes from karyogamy plus meiosis in the sexual cycle.
Memory hooks
  • Good times = clones; bad times = sexGood conditions bring asexual reproduction (identical spores); unfavorable conditions bring sexual reproduction.
  • First the Party, then the KissPlasmogamy (cytoplasm fuses) happens first; karyogamy (nuclei fuse) comes later, hours to centuries later.
  • Plasma = cytoplasm; Karyo = nucleus (kernel)Plasmogamy fuses cytoplasm; karyogamy fuses nuclei.
  • n → n+n → 2n → nHaploid mycelium, heterokaryotic (dikaryotic) stage after plasmogamy, diploid zygote after karyogamy, meiosis back to haploid spores.
  • Fungi live single (haploid)In the generalized fungal cycle taught here, haploid is normal and the diploid stage is brief; the supplementary yeast cycle includes diploid budding.
  • Plus meets minus, no he or sheFungal mating types are + and -, not male and female; pheromones signal mating type.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • CANONICAL: lecture slide Figure 31.5, generalized life cycle ("Make sure that you know this particular figure"). LEFT loop = asexual: mycelium (n) to spore-producing structures (n) to spores (n), germination back to mycelium. RIGHT loop = sexual: mycelium (n), PLASMOGAMY (fusion of cytoplasm) to heterokaryotic stage (n+n), KARYOGAMY (fusion of nuclei) to zygote (2n), MEIOSIS to spores (n), germination back to mycelium. Draw both loops sharing the mycelium and color each ploidy differently (haploid, heterokaryotic in purple, diploid); a second arrow shows the other mating type joining at plasmogamy.

  • Photos: mold on an orange (asexual molds) and a yeast cell producing a bud.
Extra detail, lower priority

On her exam use the generalized haploid cycle.

31.3-31.4

The ancestor of fungi was an aquatic, single-celled, flagellated protist; Fungi have radiated into a diverse set of lineages

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Place fungi on the tree and order the lineagesDescribe the ancestor of fungi and which group fungi are closest to. List the fungal lineages in branching order with one feature each, and explain what changed with the move to land.List from memory on paper
  • Ancestor: aquatic, single-celled, flagellated protist
  • fungi are closer to ANIMALS than to plants
  • fungi, animals and protist relatives = opisthokonts, supergroup Unikonta (nucleariids not required)
  • about 145,000 known species, maybe 2.2 to 3.8 million
  • order: cryptomycetes (flagellated spores) and microsporidians (harpoon-like organelle) as basal sister groups
  • chytrids (flagellated zoospores)
  • zoopagomycetes (nonflagellated spores, zygosporangium)
  • mucoromycetes (Rhizopus, Pilobolus
  • old name Zygomycota, used in lab
  • glomeromycetes sit here, details not required)
  • ascomycetes (sac, ascus) and basidiomycetes (club, basidium) as sister groups
  • flagella lost with the move from water to land (wind-dispersed spores)
  • trend: mucoromycetes, ascomycetes and basidiomycetes all include mycorrhizal mutualists.

Common mistake: Fungi are not close relatives of plants, even though botany once studied them. Molecular data place them with animals as opisthokonts.

Draw the Rhizopus life cycle (Figure 31.15)Draw the Rhizopus stolonifer life cycle. Show the asexual sporangium loop and the sexual loop from + and - hyphae to haploid spores, labeling n, n+n and 2n and when meiosis occurs.Draw and label on paper
  • Mucoromycete (old Zygomycota)
  • coenocytic hyphae (n) penetrate and absorb from rotting food. Asexual (good conditions): sporangia at the tips of upright hyphae release haploid spores (n) that germinate into mycelium. Sexual (deteriorating conditions): + and - hyphae meet
  • gametangia undergo plasmogamy
  • young zygosporangium, heterokaryotic (n+n)
  • karyogamy gives diploid nuclei (2n)
  • the zygosporangium is metabolically INACTIVE (dormant) and resists freezing and drying
  • when conditions improve, meiosis occurs
  • the zygosporangium germinates into a sporangium
  • it releases genetically diverse haploid spores (n). The normal condition is haploid: both loops end in haploid spores.

Common mistake: The zygosporangium is metabolically inactive and dormant (follow the lecture, not the outline). Meiosis waits until conditions improve.

Draw the Neurospora crassa life cycle (Figure 31.18)Draw the Neurospora crassa life cycle. Show the asexual spores, then the sexual path to the ascus, naming each fusion and division and the number of ascospores. When was its genome published?Draw and label on paper
  • Ascomycete (sac fungus), bread mold and model research organism
  • entire genome published in 2003. Asexual: specialized hyphae release haploid conidia, "naked spores" not enclosed in a sporangium (the term conidiophore is not required). Sexual: a conidium fuses with hyphae of the other mating type (plasmogamy)
  • dikaryotic hyphae (n+n) form
  • asci form at their tips inside an ascocarp
  • karyogamy in the ascus gives a diploid nucleus (2n)
  • meiosis gives 4 haploid nuclei
  • one mitosis gives 8 ascospores (n)
  • ascospores are discharged from the ascocarp one at a time and germinate into new mycelia.

Common mistake: Meiosis gives 4 nuclei, then one mitosis makes 8 ascospores. Conidia are asexual spores; ascospores are sexual spores.

Draw the mushroom (basidiomycete) sexual life cycle (Figure 31.20)Draw the sexual life cycle of a mushroom-forming basidiomycete from two haploid mycelia to spores. Label the dikaryotic stage, the basidiocarp parts, where karyogamy and meiosis happen, and the spore number.Draw and label on paper
  • Basidiomycetes = club fungi (club-shaped basidium): mushrooms, puffballs, shelf fungi
  • rusts and smuts parasitize plants
  • key wood decomposers that break down lignin. Sexual cycle only: + and - haploid mycelia (n) fuse (plasmogamy)
  • long-lived dikaryotic mycelium (n+n)
  • it forms a basidiocarp (the mushroom, e.g. a white supermarket mushroom) of tightly packed dikaryotic hyphae
  • gills under the cap are lined with basidia
  • karyogamy in each basidium (2n), followed right away by meiosis
  • 4 haploid basidiospores (n), dispersed by wind, germinate into haploid mycelia. Fairy rings: the mycelium is underground and grows outward about 30 cm per year, so the ring widens.

Common mistake: A basidium makes 4 basidiospores by meiosis only, with no extra mitosis. The 8-spore ascus belongs to Neurospora.

  • This used to be referred to as [Zygomycota]. And we will refer to it as [Zygomycota] um, in lab as well.Mucoromycota = old Zygomycota.
  • kind of seeing a trend here with our last three groupingsMucoromycetes, ascomycetes and basidiomycetes all include mycorrhizal mutualists.
  • the [nucleariids], which we're not going to talk aboutnot required: nucleariids (even though the opisthokont slide prints them).
  • I'm not going to hold you responsible for that term. [Conidiophore].not required: the term conidiophore. Know conidia.
  • the [glomeromycetes] is not something that we're going to talk about in herenot required beyond placement: glomeromycetes sit in Mucoromycota.
Big idea
Molecular data show fungi and animals are more closely related to each other than either is to plants; both are opisthokonts that evolved from a unicellular, flagellated ancestor. Fungi radiated into lineages that include the basal cryptomycetes and microsporidians, the chytrids (flagellated zoospores), zoopagomycetes, mucoromycetes (Rhizopus, Pilobolus, glomeromycetes), ascomycetes (sac fungi with asci) and basidiomycetes (club fungi with basidia). The instructor wants the life cycles of Rhizopus, Neurospora crassa and a mushroom (basidiomycete). Losing flagella and making wind-dispersed spores probably went with the move from water to land.

Must know

  • The ancestor of fungi was aquatic, single-celled and a flagellated protist. Molecular data: fungi and animals are more closely related to each other than either is to plants (fungi were once studied in botany). Fungi, animals and their protist relatives form the opisthokonts, in supergroup Unikonta.
  • About 145,000 known species of fungi; the real number may be 2.2 to 3.8 million.
  • Basal lineage (sister groups, dotted lines on the tree): cryptomycetes (unicellular, flagellated spores, many parasitize protists or other fungi) and microsporidians (unicellular parasites of protists and animals including humans, highly reduced mitochondria, spores inject a harpoon-like organelle; a honeybee parasite may contribute to Colony Collapse Disorder).
  • Chytrids: nearly all have flagellated spores called zoospores; decomposers, parasites or mutualists. Zoopagomycetes: filamentous, nonflagellated spores, durable zygosporangium in sex; some change the behavior of insects they parasitize. Losing flagella (wind-dispersed spores) probably went with the move from water to land.
  • Mucoromycetes (Mucoromycota) used to be called Zygomycota, and lab still calls them Zygomycota. Includes Rhizopus (bread mold) and Pilobolus, which aims and shoots its sporangium toward bright light. Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi sit here; about 85% of all plant species partner with arbuscular mycorrhizae.
  • Rhizopus life cycle (KNOW IT): coenocytic hyphae penetrate and absorb from rotting food. Asexual (good conditions): sporangia at the tips of upright hyphae release haploid spores. Sexual (deteriorating conditions): + and - hyphae fuse (plasmogamy) into a young heterokaryotic zygosporangium; karyogamy gives diploid nuclei; the zygosporangium is metabolically INACTIVE and resists freezing and drying; when conditions improve, meiosis occurs and it germinates into a sporangium that releases haploid spores. Both loops end in haploid spores.
  • Ascomycetes (Ascomycota) are the SAC FUNGI: sexual spores form in a sac-like ascus, and asci are made in fruiting bodies called ascocarps. They range from unicellular yeasts to cup fungi and morels. About 25% of ascomycete species form lichens; some form mycorrhizae; others live in leaves and make compounds toxic to insects.
  • Ascomycete asexual spores are conidia, "naked spores" not enclosed in a sporangium, made at the tips of specialized hyphae. Sexual: a conidium fuses with hyphae of the other mating type, dikaryotic hyphae form, and asci form at their tips; karyogamy and meiosis happen in the ascus, then one mitosis gives 8 ascospores (Neurospora), discharged one at a time.
  • Neurospora crassa (KNOW ITS LIFE CYCLE): a bread mold and model research organism; its entire genome was published in 2003.
  • Basidiomycetes (Basidiomycota) are the CLUB FUNGI, named for the club-shaped basidium: mushrooms, puffballs and shelf fungi; rusts and smuts are plant parasites. They are important decomposers of wood and very good at breaking down lignin. Her "trend": mucoromycetes, ascomycetes and basidiomycetes all include mycorrhizal mutualists.
  • Mushroom life cycle (KNOW IT, sexual only): + and - haploid mycelia fuse (plasmogamy) into a long-lived dikaryotic (n+n) mycelium; the basidiocarp (mushroom, e.g. white supermarket mushroom) is tightly packed dikaryotic hyphae; gills under the cap are lined with basidia; karyogamy in each basidium is followed right away by meiosis, giving 4 haploid basidiospores dispersed by wind.
  • Fairy rings: some basidiomycete mushrooms appear in a ring, sometimes overnight; the mycelium underground expands about 30 cm per year, so the ring gets bigger each year.

Terms

opisthokonts
The clade made of fungi, animals and their protist relatives, which evolved from a unicellular, flagellated ancestor.
basal fungal lineage
The earliest-branching fungi on the tree: cryptomycetes and microsporidians, which form sister groups.
Cryptomycota (cryptomycetes)
Basal phylum of unicellular fungi with flagellated spores and chitin-rich walls; many parasitize protists or other fungi.
Microsporidia (microsporidians)
Basal phylum of unicellular parasites of protists and animals with highly reduced mitochondria and spores that inject a harpoon-like organelle.
Colony Collapse Disorder
Loss of honeybee colonies that a microsporidian parasite of honeybees may contribute to.
Chytridiomycota (chytrids)
Phylum of mostly aquatic fungi with flagellated spores (zoospores), chitin walls, and unicellular or colonial bodies.
zoospore
A flagellated spore, found in nearly all chytrids.
Zoopagomycota (zoopagomycetes)
Phylum of filamentous fungi that parasitize or live with animals, fungi or protists; nonflagellated spores and a zygosporangium in sex.
zygosporangium
Durable, thick-walled structure formed after plasmogamy that houses and protects the zygote; karyogamy and meiosis occur inside it; resists freezing and drying.
Mucoromycota (mucoromycetes)
Phylum of molds and mycorrhizal fungi, formerly Zygomycota, that includes Rhizopus, Pilobolus and the glomeromycetes.
sporangium (plural sporangia)
A sac at the tip of an upright hypha that releases hundreds of air-dispersed haploid spores.
glomeromycetes
The arbuscular mycorrhizae-forming clade within Mucoromycota; know only its placement.
Ascomycota (ascomycetes)
The sac fungi, from yeasts to cup fungi and morels, making sexual spores in asci.
ascus (plural asci)
The sac-like cell where karyogamy and meiosis occur; in Neurospora one mitosis after meiosis gives 8 ascospores (yeast asci hold 4).
ascocarp
The fruiting body of an ascomycete, on whose surface the asci form.
ascospore
A sexual spore of an ascomycete; 8 per ascus in Neurospora (meiosis plus one mitosis).
conidia
Asexual "naked" spores of ascomycetes made at the tips of specialized hyphae, not inside a sporangium.
Basidiomycota (basidiomycetes)
The club fungi; mushrooms, puffballs, shelf fungi, rusts and smuts, named for the basidium.
basidium (plural basidia)
The club-shaped cell on the gills where karyogamy and meiosis occur, producing 4 basidiospores.
basidiocarp
The fruiting body (mushroom) of a basidiomycete, made of tightly packed dikaryotic hyphae.
gills
Thin plates under the mushroom cap that are lined with basidia.
basidiospore
A haploid sexual spore made on a basidium and dispersed by wind.
lignin
A tough component of wood that basidiomycetes are very good at decomposing.
rusts and smuts
Basidiomycete plant parasites.
fairy ring
A ring of mushrooms over an underground mycelium that widens as the mycelium grows outward.

Examples

  • Microsporidian parasite of honeybees: may contribute to Colony Collapse Disorder.
  • Chytrids at hydrothermal vents: shows how widespread chytrids are.
  • Zoopagomycetes that change insect behavior: parasites that alter the behavior of the insects they infect.
  • Rhizopus (bread mold), Rhizopus stolonifer in the Zygomycota video: decomposer on bread and strawberries; its life cycle is the model mucoromycete cycle; an opportunistic human pathogen that rarely causes disease.
  • Pilobolus: aims and shoots its sporangium toward bright light.
  • Glomeromycetes: form arbuscular mycorrhizae with about 85% of plant species.
  • Cup fungi and morels: elaborate ascomycete fruiting bodies (ascocarps). Peziza is the cup fungus example in the Ascomycota video.
  • Neurospora crassa: bread mold, model research organism, genome published in 2003; life cycle to know.
  • Ascomycota video: Penicillium on citrus rind (makes penicillin); Saccharomyces cerevisiae (single-celled yeast for beer, wine, bread); Candida albicans (thrush, dimorphic); Aspergillus (used to ferment rice for sake).
  • Ascomycetes inside plant leaves: make compounds toxic to insects that protect the plant.
  • Puffballs, shelf fungi on rotting logs, maiden veil fungus: basidiomycetes; shelf fungi decompose wood and lignin.
  • Common white supermarket mushroom: a basidiocarp.
  • Fairy ring around a tree: underground basidiomycete mycelium expanding about 30 cm per year.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Fungi are more closely related to animals than to plants, even though fungi were "traditionally studied in botany classes."
  • Species numbers: about 145,000 known; possibly 2.2 to 3.8 million total ("which is just crazy").
  • Rhizopus: "you will be held responsible for learning this one... this is the figure that I want you guys to know."
  • Neurospora crassa: "This is something that I want you guys to know," and "its entire genome was published in 2003."
  • Mushroom (basidiomycete) life cycle: "another life cycle that I want you guys to know," a sexual-only cycle in the figure.
  • Conidiophore: "I'm not going to hold you responsible for that term."
  • Glomeromycetes: "not something that we're going to talk about in here," just know they are in Mucoromycota.
  • Mucoromycota "used to be referred to as Zygomycota," and lab will call it Zygomycota.
  • Trend: Mucoromycetes, ascomycetes and basidiomycetes all include mycorrhizal mutualists.
  • Use the end-of-chapter summary table of fungal phyla and distinguishing characteristics to study.
Memory hooks
  • Can My Cat Zap Mice And BatsFungal lineages from the base: Cryptomycetes, Microsporidians, Chytrids, Zoopagomycetes, Mucoromycetes, Ascomycetes, Basidiomycetes.
  • Chytrids swimChytrids have flagellated spores (zoospores); later lineages lost flagella as fungi moved onto land and used wind.
  • Ascus = sac of 8; Basidium = club of 4In Neurospora each ascus holds 8 ascospores (meiosis plus one mitosis); a basidium makes 4 basidiospores (meiosis only).
  • Zygosporangium = Zzz bunkerThe zygosporangium is a durable, metabolically inactive structure that resists freezing and drying until conditions improve.
  • Neuro in 2003Neurospora crassa, a bread mold and model organism, had its whole genome published in 2003.
  • Conidia = CoverlessConidia are the asexual naked spores of ascomycetes, not enclosed in a sporangium.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Opisthokont tree: unicellular flagellated ancestor at the base; one branch to animals (and their protist relatives), a separate branch to fungi.
  • Fungal phylogeny: ancestral protist at the root; cryptomycetes and microsporidians on dotted lines at the base; then chytrids, zoopagomycetes, mucoromycetes, ascomycetes, basidiomycetes. Traditional groups are mucoromycetes, ascomycetes, basidiomycetes; the others are newer.
  • Rhizopus life cycle: draw the mycelium on bread; ASEXUAL loop: upright hyphae with black sporangia releasing haploid spores that germinate. SEXUAL loop: + and - hyphae meet, plasmogamy, young zygosporangium (heterokaryotic, n+n), karyogamy (diploid nuclei), dormant thick-walled zygosporangium, meiosis, zygosporangium germinates into a sporangium that releases haploid spores. Label haploid, heterokaryotic and diploid.
  • Neurospora crassa life cycle: ASEXUAL: hyphae make conidia that disperse and germinate. SEXUAL: conidium of one mating type fuses with mycelium of the other (plasmogamy), dikaryotic hyphae, ascus forms at the hyphal tip, karyogamy (diploid zygote in ascus), meiosis gives 4 nuclei, one mitosis gives 8 ascospores, released one at a time from the ascocarp, germinate into new mycelium.
  • Mushroom life cycle (animation "Life Cycle of a Mushroom" and lecture): haploid spore germinates by mitosis into a hypha, mycelium; hyphae of different mating types fuse (plasmogamy) into a dikaryotic n+n cell and mycelium; mycelium grows into a fruiting body (mushroom) when conditions are right; cap with gills underneath; gills lined with dikaryotic basidia; karyogamy makes a diploid nucleus; meiosis makes 4 haploid nuclei; 4 basidiospores form on extensions and break off. Label cap, gills, basidium, basidiospores, n, n+n, 2n.
  • Microsporidian spore with a harpoon-like organelle injecting into a host cell; cryptomycete spores inside a host fungus; chytrid with flagellated zoospores.
  • Photos: ascocarps (cup-shaped fruiting bodies), puffballs, shelf fungi, maiden veil fungus, fairy ring around a tree.
  • End-of-chapter summary table: fungal phyla, distinguishing characteristics, and small pictures; the instructor recommends it for exam study.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Molecular analyses and metagenomic studies (DNA from environmental samples) have found entirely new fungal groups.
  • Known species per lineage (stated, not stressed): cryptomycetes about 30 (genetic data suggest many more), microsporidians about 1,300, chytrids about 1,000, zoopagomycetes about 900, mucoromycetes about 750, ascomycetes about 90,000, basidiomycetes about 50,000.
  • Cryptomycetes live in soils, marine and freshwater, aerobic and anaerobic, with chitin-rich walls. Microsporidians have very small genomes (as few as 2,000 genes).
  • Chytrids live in lakes, soil and marine habitats including hydrothermal vents; chitin walls; unicellular or colonial.
  • Zoopagomycetes are parasites or commensal symbionts (living with a host without harming or helping it) of animals, or parasites of other fungi or protists.
  • Glomeromycetes: the arbuscular mycorrhizae-forming clade in Mucoromycota. She said "not something that we're going to talk about"; only know they sit in Mucoromycota.
  • Ascomycetes live in marine, freshwater and terrestrial habitats; elaborate fruiting bodies include the morel Morchella esculenta and the truffle Tuber melanosporum.
  • Neurospora asci hold their ascospores in order, so they are easy to track.
  • Supplementary video vocabulary (YouTube speakers, not stressed by the instructor): gametangia (hyphal extensions holding a haploid nucleus that meet at plasmogamy); sporangiophore (upright hypha bearing a sporangium); sporangiospores (spores made in the sporangium); rhizoid hyphae (hyphae that bury into bread to absorb); zygospore; budding vs fission (yeast cell divides into equal halves); conjugation (yeast mating, no dikaryotic stage); dimorphic fungus (switches between multicellular and single-celled yeast forms, e.g. Candida albicans, Aspergillus, many pathogenic basidiomycetes).
  • Supplementary Ascomycota video chromosome numbers: Saccharomyces cerevisiae 16, Penicillium 4, Peziza 8 to 16 (haploid sets). Peziza is the cup fungus example; Aspergillus ferments rice for sake; yeast asci hold 4 ascospores, not 8.
  • Supplementary Basidiomycota video: some basidiomycetes also reproduce asexually by fragmented hyphae; the lecture mushroom figure shows only the sexual cycle.
  • Conidiophore (the stalk bearing conidia) appears on the slide, but she said "I'm not going to hold you responsible for that term."
  • Numbers stated in lecture (secondary, not stressed as exam priorities): about 145,000 known species, possibly 2.2 to 3.8 million; about 85% of plant species partner with arbuscular mycorrhizae; about 25% of ascomycete species form lichens; fairy ring mycelium expands about 30 cm per year.
31.5

Fungi play key roles in nutrient cycling, ecological interactions, and human welfare

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Explain fungi as decomposers and mutualistsName the three ways fungi interact with other organisms. Why do decomposers matter? Give examples of fungal mutualisms with plants and with animals.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Three roles: decomposers, mutualists, pathogens (parasites)
  • fungi and bacteria decompose cellulose and lignin and recycle chemical elements between the living and nonliving world
  • without them, life as we know it would cease to exist
  • endophytes live inside leaves or other plant parts without causing harm, mostly ascomycetes
  • some make toxins that defend the plant, others help it tolerate heat, drought and heavy metals
  • mycorrhizae trade minerals for organic nutrients
  • cattle and other grazing mammals: gut fungi break down plant material
  • leafcutter ants bring leaves to feed fungi in their nest and eat the nutrient-rich tips of the hyphae.

Common mistake: Endophytes are not parasites. They live inside plants without causing harm, and many help the plant.

Describe lichens: partners, forms, reproduction and roleWhat two partners make up a lichen and what does each contribute? Name and describe the three lichen growth forms, explain how lichens reproduce asexually, and give their ecological role.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Lichen = fungus (usually an ascomycete) + photosynthetic microorganisms (green algae or cyanobacteria)
  • the fungus gives shape, structure, most of the mass and a suitable environment
  • algae or cyanobacteria sit in a layer just below the surface and give carbon compounds
  • cyanobacteria also fix nitrogen
  • the symbiosis is so complete that lichens get their own scientific names
  • forms: crustose (crusty), foliose (leaf-like), fruticose (shrub-like)
  • asexual reproduction by fragmentation or soredia, small clusters of hyphae with embedded algae, so each carries BOTH partners
  • the fungus can also reproduce sexually
  • pioneers on new rock and soil (volcanic flows, burned forests) that break rock down chemically
  • probably helped early plants move onto land.

Common mistake: Soredia carry both partners, not just the fungus. Fruticose is shrub-like; foliose is leaf-like.

Give examples of fungal diseases and human usesGive fungal disease examples in plants and in animals, including the three kinds of mycoses and the amphibian decline. Then name four ways people use fungi.List from memory on paper
  • Plant diseases: corn smut, tar spot on maple, ergots on rye (Claviceps purpurea) causing ergotism (gangrene, spasms, hallucinations)
  • chytrids: deadly skin infections that caused a large decline in amphibians, because amphibians breathe through moist skin
  • mycosis = fungal infection in animals
  • skin: ringworm, athlete's foot
  • systemic: valley fever (coccidioidomycosis), soil spores infect the lungs
  • opportunistic: Candida albicans yeast infection
  • uses: food (cheese, bread, alcoholic beverages), penicillin from Penicillium, an ergot compound that lowers blood pressure and stops bleeding after childbirth, engineered S. cerevisiae that can produce human glycoproteins, diesel-like biofuel
  • optional numbers (lower priority): about 30% of fungi are parasites or pathogens
  • about 500 amphibian species declined
  • about 40,000 died of ergotism in France in 944 C.E.

Common mistake: Valley fever is systemic (lungs), not a skin mycosis. Chytrids kill amphibians through the skin they breathe through.

Match each fungal lineage to its defining featureFrom memory, write the seven fungal lineages and one distinguishing feature for each, as in the end-of-chapter summary chart.Make a two-column table on paper
  • Cryptomycetes: unicellular parasites with flagellated spores
  • microsporidians: unicellular parasites with resistant spores that infect through a harpoon-like organelle
  • chytrids: flagellated spores (zoospores)
  • zoopagomycetes: resistant zygosporangium in sexual reproduction
  • mucoromycetes: zygosporangium (Rhizopus), include arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi
  • ascomycetes (sac fungi): sexual ascospores in saclike asci inside ascocarps, plus asexual conidia
  • basidiomycetes (club fungi): basidiocarp with club-shaped basidia making basidiospores.

Common mistake: Flagellated spores belong only to chytrids and cryptomycetes. Microsporidians use a harpoon-like organelle, not flagella.

Lecture video 31.5 (opens online)
Full notes for 31.5
★ She said this is on the test
  • So this might be a very helpful chart for you to study for the exam.The end-of-chapter summary of fungal lineages: one defining feature per phylum.
  • Here we've got the three different forms of lichens, and we will look at these three forms in lab. [Crustose], I like to remember this because it looks crusty.Lichen forms: crustose (crusty), foliose (leaf-like), fruticose (shrub-like).
  • Life as we know it would really cease to exist.Fungi and bacteria are the decomposers that recycle chemical elements.
Big idea
Fungi interact with other organisms as decomposers, mutualists and pathogens. As decomposers of cellulose and lignin, fungi and bacteria recycle chemical elements between the living and nonliving world. Mutualistic fungi live with plants (endophytes, mycorrhizae), animals (cattle guts, leafcutter ants) and photosynthetic partners (lichens). About 30% of fungi are parasites or pathogens, mostly of plants, and fungi cause animal diseases (mycoses) such as chytrid infections of amphibians, ringworm, athlete's foot, valley fever and yeast infections. People use fungi for food, antibiotics, biotechnology and biofuels.

Must know

  • Fungi interact with other organisms as decomposers, mutualists and pathogens. As decomposers of cellulose and lignin, fungi and bacteria recycle chemical elements between the living and nonliving world; without them, life as we know it would cease.
  • Endophytes: all plant species studied harbor symbiotic fungi or bacteria that live inside leaves or other plant parts without causing harm. Most endophytes are ascomycetes. Some make toxins that defend the plant; others help the plant tolerate heat, drought and heavy metals.
  • Fungus-animal mutualisms: fungi share digestive services, such as breaking down plant material in the guts of cattle and other grazing mammals. Leafcutter ants farm fungi: ants bring leaves to feed the fungi in their nest and eat the nutrient-rich tips of the fungal hyphae.
  • Lichens: symbiotic associations of a fungus (usually an ascomycete) with photosynthetic microorganisms (unicellular or filamentous green algae or cyanobacteria). The symbiosis is so complete that lichens get their own scientific names (binomials).
  • Three lichen forms (seen in lab): crustose (crusty), foliose (leaf-like), fruticose (shrub-like).
  • The fungus gives the lichen its shape, structure and most of its mass, and gives the partner a suitable environment for growth. The algae or cyanobacteria sit in a layer just below the surface and give carbon compounds; cyanobacteria also fix nitrogen.
  • Lichens reproduce asexually by fragmentation or by soredia: small clusters of fungal hyphae with embedded algae, so each soredium carries BOTH partners. The fungal partner can also reproduce sexually.
  • Lichens are pioneers on new rock and soil surfaces (volcanic flows, burned forests) and help break rock down chemically. Early lichens probably modified the land and helped early plants move from water to land.
  • About 30% of known fungal species are parasites or pathogens, mostly of plants; about 10 to 50% of the world's fruit harvest is lost to fungi each year. Examples: corn smut, tar spot on maple, ergots on rye.
  • Ergots on rye are made by the ascomycete Claviceps purpurea. Eating infected grain causes ergotism: gangrene, nervous spasms, burning sensations, hallucinations and temporary insanity; an outbreak around 944 C.E. killed about 40,000 people in France. Yet an ergot compound lowers high blood pressure and stops internal bleeding after childbirth.
  • Animals are less susceptible to parasitic fungi than plants, but two chytrid species are implicated in the decline or extinction of about 500 amphibian species worldwide (especially frogs). They cause severe skin infections, deadly because amphibians breathe through moist skin.
  • Mycosis = fungal infection in animals. Skin: ringworm (athlete's foot is a type). Systemic: coccidioidomycosis (valley fever), soil spores infect the lungs; in the San Fernando Valley builders spray water on disturbed soil. Opportunistic: Candida albicans yeast infections when the body is vulnerable.

Terms

mutualism (fungal)
A relationship where a fungus absorbs nutrients from a host and gives benefits back to the host.
endophyte
A symbiotic fungus or bacterium that lives inside leaves or other plant parts without harming the plant; most fungal endophytes are ascomycetes.
lichen
A symbiotic association between a fungus and photosynthetic microorganisms (green algae or cyanobacteria).
crustose lichen
A crusty lichen form, often on rock.
foliose lichen
A leaf-like lichen form.
fruticose lichen
A shrub-like lichen form.
soredia
Small clusters of fungal hyphae with embedded algae that lichens release for asexual reproduction; each carries both partners.
fragmentation
Asexual reproduction of a lichen by breaking off pieces.
pioneer (lichen)
An early colonizer of new rock or soil surfaces that helps break the surface down.
pathogen
An organism that causes disease; fungal parasites and pathogens attack mostly plants.
ergot
A dark fungal growth on rye made by the ascomycete Claviceps purpurea; its toxins cause ergotism.
ergotism
Poisoning from eating ergot-infected grain: gangrene, spasms, burning sensations, hallucinations and temporary insanity.
mycosis (plural mycoses)
The general term for a fungal infection in animals.
ringworm
A fungal skin infection spread by sharing towels; athlete's foot is a type of ringworm.
systemic mycosis
A fungal infection that spreads through the body and causes serious illness.
coccidioidomycosis (valley fever)
A systemic mycosis from soil spores that infects the lungs with tuberculosis-like symptoms.
opportunistic mycosis
A fungal infection that happens only when the body is vulnerable, such as a Candida albicans yeast infection.
fungicide
A treatment, such as a lotion or powder, that kills fungi.
penicillin
An antibiotic made by the fungus Penicillium.
human glycoproteins
Human proteins such as insulin-like growth factor that genetically modified Saccharomyces cerevisiae is used to make.
biofuel
Fuel from living things; a fungus makes hydrocarbons similar to diesel fuel.

Examples

  • Cattle and other grazing mammals: gut fungi break down plant material (digestive mutualism).
  • Leafcutter ants: farm fungi on leaves in their nests and eat the nutrient-rich hyphal tips.
  • Crustose lichen on a rock, foliose lichen on wood, fruticose lichen on the side of a tree.
  • Lichens on volcanic flows and burned forests: pioneer species that start breaking down rock.
  • Corn smut on an ear of corn; tar spot fungus on maple leaves; ergots on rye.
  • Ergotism outbreak in France, 944 C.E.: about 40,000 deaths.
  • Two chytrid species: decline or extinction of about 500 amphibian species, mostly frogs.
  • Athlete's foot and ringworm; coccidioidomycosis (valley fever) in the San Fernando Valley, where water trucks keep construction soil from blowing.
  • Candida albicans: normal yeast that can cause an opportunistic yeast infection (and thrush in the mouth, per the Ascomycota video).
  • Penicillium: makes penicillin.
  • Ergot compound: lowers high blood pressure and stops internal bleeding after childbirth.
  • Saccharomyces cerevisiae: modified to make human glycoproteins such as insulin-like growth factor.
  • A fungus making diesel-like hydrocarbons: biofuel research.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Three roles to know: decomposers, mutualists, pathogens.
  • Without fungi and bacteria as decomposers, "life as we know it would really cease to exist."
  • Lichens come up in both lecture and lab; know the three forms. Crustose: "I like to remember this because it looks crusty."
  • "The symbiosis are so complete that lichens are even given their own scientific names."
  • Lichens probably came onto land first and helped plants make the transition to land.
  • Numbers she stated (secondary checklist, not stressed as must-memorize): 30% of fungi are parasites or pathogens; 10 to 50% of fruit harvest lost yearly; 2 chytrid species, about 500 amphibian species; ergotism killed about 40,000 in France (944 C.E.).
  • Local example: valley fever and the water trucks in the San Fernando Valley.
  • "At the end of your chapter, you have this very nice summary of key concepts with our fungal lineages... this might be a very helpful chart for you to study for the exam."
Memory hooks
  • Decompose, Mutualize, ParasitizeThe three ways fungi interact: decomposers, mutualists, pathogens.
  • Crusty, Leafy, ShrubbyLichen forms: crustose (crusty), foliose (leaf-like), fruticose (shrub-like).
  • Fungus builds the house, alga cooks the foodIn a lichen the fungus gives shape and a place to grow; the alga or cyanobacterium gives carbon compounds (cyanobacteria also give nitrogen).
  • 30, 10 to 50, 2 and 50030% of fungi are parasites or pathogens; 10 to 50% of fruit harvest lost yearly; 2 chytrid species hit about 500 amphibian species.
  • Valley fever loves dustCoccidioidomycosis spores rise from disturbed soil into the lungs, so builders in the San Fernando Valley water the soil.
  • Penicillium Protects, Saccharomyces SynthesizesPenicillium makes penicillin; Saccharomyces cerevisiae is engineered to make human glycoproteins like insulin-like growth factor.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Leafcutter ants carrying leaves to the fungus garden in their nest.
  • Three lichen photos: crustose on rock, foliose (leaf-like), fruticose (shrub-like) on a tree.
  • Lichen anatomy cross section and micrograph: fungal hyphae make up most of the body; a layer of algal cells lies just below the upper surface. Draw: upper surface of packed hyphae, algal layer under it, loose hyphae below; label the fungal hyphae and algal cells, and show a soredium (hyphae wrapped around algae) breaking off.
  • Plant pathogen photos: corn smut on an ear of corn, tar spot on maple leaves, black ergots on rye.
  • Dead frogs killed by chytrid infection.
  • End-of-chapter summary table of fungal lineages and their distinguishing characteristics.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Lichens grow on rocks, rotting logs, trees and roofs; millions of photosynthetic cells are held in a mass of fungal hyphae.
  • Some lichens also hold a basidiomycete yeast as a second fungal partner; its role is not known.
  • Grain crops also suffer major losses to fungi.
  • Animals are less susceptible to parasitic fungi than plants.
  • Ringworm and athlete's foot fungicide treatment takes several weeks.
  • Biofuel fungus on the slide: Gliocladium roseum, whose genome is being sequenced.
  • Chytrid map slide: infection spreading through Sixty Lake Basin, California, 2004 to 2008 (dates and place were not stressed).
  • Candida albicans also causes thrush in the mouth (Ascomycota video).
  • Supplementary visual context: Introduction to Fungi shows examples involving food, lichens, decay and disease. Review the explicit lecture priorities first.
  • Numbers stated in lecture (secondary, not stressed as exam priorities): about 30% of known fungi are parasites or pathogens; 10 to 50% of the world's fruit harvest lost to fungi yearly; two chytrid species linked to about 500 amphibian species; ergotism outbreak around 944 C.E. killed about 40,000 in France.
  • Secondary checklist, details and practical uses: ringworm spreads by sharing towels and is treated with fungicide; valley fever gives tuberculosis-like symptoms. Uses: food (cheese, alcoholic beverages, bread), Penicillium makes penicillin, Saccharomyces cerevisiae is engineered to make human glycoproteins such as insulin-like growth factor, and a fungus makes diesel-like hydrocarbons (biofuel).
Ch 35

Exam sheet: Chapter 35: Vascular Plant Structure, Growth, and Development

Memorize this, review it last
  1. Plant body figure labels: shoot system, root system, apical bud, axillary bud, node, internode, blade, petiole, stem, vegetative shoot, reproductive shoot (flower), taproot, lateral roots.
  2. Roots: anchor, absorb, store. Rhizome, stolon, potato tuber = stems. Onion layers, the leaf tendrils shown in lecture, cactus spines = leaves. Pneumatophore, prop, buttress, storage, strangling aerial = roots.
  3. Monocot parallel veins, eudicot branching. Compound leaf: one petiole, one axillary bud.
  4. Tissue colors: blue dermal, purple vascular, yellow ground. Pith inside, cortex outside. Systems continuous through the plant.
  5. Parenchyma: divides and differentiates. Collenchyma: alive, uneven primary walls, does not restrain growth. Sclerenchyma: lignin, dead (sclereids in pears, fibers).
  6. Tracheid = long, tapered, pits. Vessel element = wide, short, perforation plates. Both dead.
  7. Sieve-tube element: alive, no nucleus, ribosomes, vacuole; sieve plates; companion cell. Sieve cells in ferns and gymnosperms.
  8. Xylem one way up; phloem multidirectional.
  9. Primary growth = length (apical meristems). Secondary = thickness (vascular cambium + cork cambium). Plants: indeterminate growth.
  10. Protoderm to dermal, ground meristem to ground, procambium to vascular.
  11. Vascular cambium: X inside, P outside, more X. Cork cambium: outward only. Youngest cells next to the cambium.
  12. Terminal bud-scale scar rings mark past positions of the apical bud; annual stem segments show years of growth. Annual: a year or less; biennial: two growing seasons; perennial: many years.
  13. Root tip: root cap, division, elongation, differentiation (= maturation, root hairs).
  14. Root layers: epidermis, cortex, endodermis, pericycle, vascular cylinder. Eudicot X xylem; monocot xylem/phloem ring around parenchyma.
  15. Lateral roots from the pericycle (xylem and phloem stay continuous); lateral shoots from axillary buds (no damage).
  16. Shoot tip: shoot apical meristem, leaf primordia, young leaves, axillary buds. Apical dominance: closest bud most inhibited; snip the tip, get a bush.
  17. Stems: eudicot ring of bundles (xylem in, phloem out); monocot scattered. Leaf: palisade top, spongy air spaces, bundle sheath, guard cells (osmotic).
  18. Periderm = cork cambium + cork. Bark = everything outside the vascular cambium. Lenticels = gas exchange through periderm. Suberin seals cork.
  19. Early wood spring thin walls, late wood summer thick; thick ring warm/wet, thin cold/dry. Heartwood is older inner xylem that no longer conducts; sapwood is outer xylem that still conducts. The water-conducting cells are dead in both.
  20. Developmental plasticity = environment's effect on development. Arabidopsis: mustard family, first plant genome, about 27,000 genes, 5 chromosome pairs.
35.1

Plants have a hierarchical organization consisting of organs, tissues, and cells

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Label the whole plant bodySketch a flowering plant and label the shoot system, root system, and every bud, stem, leaf, and root part from the lecture figure.Draw and label on paper
  • Shoot system = stems + leaves (+ flowers)
  • root system = roots
  • apical bud at the shoot tip
  • axillary bud in the angle where a leaf meets the stem
  • node = where leaves attach
  • internode = stem between nodes
  • leaf = blade + petiole
  • stem
  • vegetative shoot (leaves, no flower)
  • reproductive shoot = flower
  • taproot = main vertical root
  • lateral roots branch from it. Roots need sugar from the shoot
  • the shoot needs water and minerals from the roots.

Common mistake: Mixing node and internode: the node is where the leaf attaches; the internode is the bare stem between two nodes.

Sort roots, stems, and leaves and their modified formsList the three jobs of roots, compare taproot and fibrous systems, then classify: rhizome, stolon, potato tuber, onion layers, tendril, cactus spine, prop root, pneumatophore, buttress root.List from memory
  • Roots anchor, absorb water and minerals, store carbohydrates
  • root hairs (epidermal cell extensions) and mycorrhizae increase absorption
  • taproot system: taproot anchors, lateral root tips absorb
  • fibrous system (grasses): adventitious roots arise from the stem, hold topsoil
  • STEMS: rhizome (underground), stolon (along the surface, strawberry), potato tuber
  • LEAVES: onion layers (storage), the leaf tendril shown in lecture, cactus spines, reproductive leaves
  • ROOTS: prop, buttress, storage (beet), pneumatophores, strangling aerial roots
  • monocot leaves have parallel veins, eudicot leaves branching veins
  • compound leaf = many leaflets, one petiole, one axillary bud.

Common mistake: Calling a potato a root: tubers, rhizomes, and stolons are all modified stems.

Name the tissue systems and the three ground cell typesName the three tissue systems with their figure colors and jobs. Then give the wall type, living or dead, and key job for parenchyma, collenchyma, and sclerenchyma.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Dermal (blue): epidermis with waxy cuticle, guard cells, trichomes
  • replaced by periderm in woody parts
  • vascular (purple): xylem and phloem, transport and support
  • ground (yellow): filler for storage, photosynthesis, support
  • pith = ground tissue inside the vascular tissue, cortex = outside it
  • all three are in every organ and continuous through the plant
  • parenchyma: thin flexible primary walls, large central vacuole, most metabolism, keeps the ability to divide and differentiate
  • collenchyma: strands, unevenly thickened primary walls, alive at maturity, flexible support that does not restrain growth
  • sclerenchyma: rigid lignin secondary walls, dead at maturity but walls remain for support
  • sclereids (pear grit) and fibers.

Common mistake: Saying collenchyma is dead: collenchyma is alive at maturity; sclerenchyma is the dead, lignified support cell.

Compare xylem and phloem conducting cellsCompare tracheids with vessel elements, then describe a sieve-tube element and its companion cell. Which way does each tissue move material?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Xylem cells are dead at maturity with lignified walls
  • tracheid: long, thin, tapered, water passes through pits
  • vessel element: wider, shorter, thinner walled, joined end to end through perforation plates
  • sieve-tube element: alive but no nucleus, ribosomes, or vacuole
  • joined end to end by sieve plates
  • companion cell keeps its own nucleus and ribosomes and supports it through plasmodesmata
  • sieve cells in seedless vascular plants (ferns) and gymnosperms
  • xylem moves water and minerals one way, roots to shoots
  • phloem moves sugar in many directions, from where it is made to growing parts or storage.

Common mistake: Thinking phloem cells are dead like xylem: sieve-tube elements are alive, just missing nucleus, ribosomes, and vacuole.
