Full notes for 35.1
★ She said this is on the test
  • Here's the figure that I want you guys to know. Spend some time with this figure.Label the whole plant body unaided: shoot and root systems, apical and axillary buds, node, internode, blade, petiole, vegetative and reproductive shoot, taproot, lateral roots.
  • You should know the structure of these.Tracheid (long, tapered, pits) vs vessel element (wider, shorter, perforation plates).
  • a lot of important, uh, definitions are in this particular concept. Make sure you take good notes on it.Definitions of organs, tissue systems, and the five cell types.
  • most importantly for our parenchyma cells is that they retain the ability to divide and differentiate.Parenchyma keeps the ability to divide and differentiate.
  • they are living at maturity. And that's important... They do not restrain growth. They do not restrain growth.Collenchyma: alive at maturity, flexible support that does not restrain growth.
  • keep in mind that our tissue system is continuous throughout the plantDermal, vascular, and ground tissue systems run continuously through shoot and root.
  • again modified stem not a rootPotato tuber (and rhizome, stolon) is a modified stem, not a root.
  • I want you to be aware of those as well.Adventitious roots arise from the stem (fibrous root systems).
Big idea
Plant organs are made of tissues, and tissues are made of cells; emergent properties appear at each level. The three basic organs are roots, stems and leaves, organized into a root system and a shoot system (stems and leaves). Every organ is built from three tissue systems (dermal, vascular, ground) that are continuous through the plant. The major cell types are parenchyma, collenchyma, sclerenchyma, the water-conducting cells of the xylem and the sugar-conducting cells of the phloem.

Must know

  • Hierarchy: cells form tissues, tissues form organs, with emergent properties at each level. 3 organs (roots, stems, leaves) in 2 systems: shoot system (stems + leaves) and root system (roots). Roots rely on sugar made in the shoot; the shoot relies on water and minerals absorbed by the roots.
  • Plant body figure, know every label: shoot system, root system, apical bud, axillary bud, node, internode, leaf (blade + petiole), stem, vegetative shoot, reproductive shoot (flower), taproot, lateral roots.
  • Roots anchor, absorb water and minerals, and store carbohydrates. Taproot system (tall plants): taproot from the primary root mainly anchors; absorption occurs mostly at lateral root tips. Fibrous system (grasses): primary root dies, adventitious roots arise from the stem, the mat holds topsoil and prevents erosion.
  • Root hairs: finger-like extensions of epidermal cells (one per epidermal cell) that increase absorptive surface. Mycorrhizal associations with soil fungi greatly increase mineral absorption.
  • Modified roots and their jobs: prop roots (adventitious roots that support), storage roots (beet), pneumatophores (roots growing up out of the ground), strangling aerial roots (grow up over the ground and wrap a host), buttress roots (triangular supports at a tree base).
  • Stem: alternating nodes (leaves attach) and internodes; apical bud elongates the shoot; axillary bud can become a branch, a thorn, or a flower. Modified stems: rhizome (underground), stolon (along the surface, strawberry plantlets), tuber (potato). All are stems, not roots.
  • Leaf: main photosynthetic organ; flattened blade + petiole; monocots have parallel veins, eudicots branching veins; compound leaf has many leaflets but one petiole and one axillary bud. Modified leaves: tendrils (her example is a leaf tendril), spines (cactus), storage leaves (onion layers), reproductive leaves (drop plantlets).
  • Three tissue systems (dermal, vascular, ground) are in every organ and continuous through the whole plant. Figure colors: blue = dermal, purple = vascular, yellow = ground. Dermal: epidermis with waxy cuticle; periderm replaces it in woody parts; guard cells for gas exchange; trichomes reduce water loss, reflect light, defend against insects.
  • Xylem moves water and minerals one way, roots to shoots. Phloem moves sugar in many directions, from where it is made to growing parts or storage. Stele = all vascular tissue of a root or stem: in a root it is one central vascular cylinder (eudicot: lobed xylem core with phloem between the lobes; monocot: a ring of alternating xylem and phloem around a parenchyma core); in stems and leaves it is split into vascular bundles.
  • Ground tissue is the filler: pith is ground tissue inside the vascular tissue, cortex is ground tissue outside it. Functions: storage, photosynthesis, support, short-distance transport.
  • Parenchyma: thin flexible primary walls, large central vacuole, most metabolism, keeps the ability to divide and differentiate. Collenchyma: strands, unevenly thickened primary walls, alive at maturity, flexible support that does not restrain growth. Sclerenchyma: rigid lignified secondary walls, many dead at maturity; sclereids (pear grit) and fibers.
  • Xylem conducting cells are dead with lignified walls: tracheids (long, thin, tapered, water passes through pits) and vessel elements (wider, shorter, thinner walled, end to end through perforation plates). Phloem: sieve-tube elements are alive but lack nucleus, ribosomes and vacuole, join through sieve plates, and each has a companion cell that keeps its own nucleus and ribosomes and supports it through plasmodesmata. Sieve cells: seedless vascular plants and gymnosperms.

Terms

tissue
A group of cells of one or more types that work together to perform a specialized function.
organ
Several types of tissues put together to carry out particular functions.
shoot system
The stems and leaves of a plant.
root system
The roots of a plant.
primary root
The first root to emerge from the seed.
lateral root
A branch root coming off the primary root; improves anchorage and water absorption.
taproot
Main root that develops from the primary root and mainly anchors tall plants.
fibrous root system
A thick mat of roots below the soil surface in which the primary root dies without forming a taproot.
adventitious root
A root that arises from the stem rather than from another root.
root hair
Finger-like extension of a root epidermal cell near the root tip that increases absorptive surface.
mycorrhizal association
Symbiosis between roots and soil fungi that greatly increases mineral absorption.
pneumatophore
A modified root that grows up out of the ground.
buttress root
Triangular root structure at the base of a tree that helps support it.
node
Point on a stem where leaves are attached.
internode
Stem segment between two nodes.
apical bud
Bud at the growing tip of a stem that elongates the young shoot.
axillary bud
Bud where a leaf (petiole) joins the stem; can become a lateral branch, thorn or flower.
rhizome
Modified stem that runs underground, roughly parallel to the surface.
stolon
Modified stem growing along the soil surface that forms new plantlets (strawberry).
tuber
Swollen underground modified stem, such as a potato.
blade
The flattened part of a leaf.
petiole
The stalk that joins the leaf blade to the stem.
compound leaf
A leaf divided into multiple leaflets that still has one petiole and one axillary bud.
tendril
In her lecture example, a modified leaf that wraps around structures to stabilize the plant.
epidermis
Single protective outer tissue layer of nonwoody plants.
cuticle
Waxy coating on the epidermis that protects against water loss.
guard cells
Specialized dermal cells that facilitate gas exchange in the shoot.
trichome
Hairlike outgrowth of epidermal cells that reduces water loss, reflects light and defends against insects.
xylem
Vascular tissue that conducts water and dissolved minerals upward from roots to shoots.
phloem
Vascular tissue that transports sugars from where they are made to growing parts or storage.
stele
All the vascular tissue of a root or stem; one central vascular cylinder in a root, separate vascular bundles in stems and leaves.
vascular bundle
Separate strand of xylem and phloem in stems and leaves.
pith
Ground tissue internal to the vascular tissue.
cortex
Ground tissue external to the vascular tissue.
parenchyma
Thin-walled living cells with a large central vacuole that do most metabolism and can still divide and differentiate.
collenchyma
Living cells in strands with unevenly thickened primary walls giving flexible support to young shoots.
sclerenchyma
Rigid support cells with lignified secondary walls, many dead at maturity.
lignin
Strengthening polymer in secondary cell walls.
sclereid
Boxy, irregular sclerenchyma cell with very thick lignified walls (gritty texture of pears).
fiber
Long, slender, tapered sclerenchyma cell grouped in strands.
tracheid
Long, thin, tapered water-conducting xylem cell; water passes between tracheids through pits.
pit
Thin region of the wall lacking secondary wall, where water moves between cells.
vessel element
Wider, shorter, thinner-walled water-conducting cell that lines up end to end to form a vessel.
perforation plate
End wall of a vessel element that lets water flow freely along the vessel.
sieve cell
Sugar-conducting phloem cell of seedless vascular plants and gymnosperms.
sieve-tube element
Living sugar-conducting cell of angiosperms; chains of them form sieve tubes.
sieve plate
Porous end wall between sieve-tube elements (looks like a shower drain).
companion cell
Living cell beside a sieve-tube element that keeps its own nucleus and ribosomes and supports the sieve-tube element through plasmodesmata.

Examples

  • Corn seed: primary root covered with root hairs.
  • Grasses: fibrous root systems that hold topsoil and prevent erosion.
  • Beet: storage root.
  • Strawberry: stolons with baby plants at their ends (asexual reproduction).
  • Potato: tuber, a modified stem found underground, not a root.
  • Onion: layers are storage leaves; the stem is at the bottom with roots below.
  • Cactus: spines are modified leaves.
  • Viney plants: tendrils.
  • Pears: sclereids give the gritty texture.
  • Ash tree: cross section of fiber cells.
  • Ferns: seedless vascular plants that use sieve cells and have some vessel elements.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "This is kind of a long concept, and a lot of important definitions are in this particular concept."
  • Plant body figure (shoot system, root system, taproot, lateral roots, blade, petiole, axillary bud, apical bud, node, internode, vegetative and reproductive shoot): "Here's the figure that I want you guys to know."
  • Rhizomes and tubers are stems, "not a root."
  • Collenchyma "are living at maturity. And that's important"; they "do not restrain growth."
  • Sclerenchyma are dead at maturity but their walls remain for support.
  • Tracheids vs vessel elements: "You should know the structure of these."
  • Phloem cells are alive but lack nucleus, ribosomes, vacuole, some cytoskeleton: "they don't have a whole lot left." The companion cell keeps its own nucleus and ribosomes and supports the sieve-tube element.
  • Tissue colors on figures: blue dermal, purple vascular, yellow ground.
  • Mycorrhizae: "That's one of the reasons that these mycorrhizal associations are so important." (explanation, not an exam signal)
Memory hooks
  • Anchor, Absorb, StoreThe three root functions: anchor the plant, absorb water and minerals, store carbohydrates.
  • Rhizome, Stolon, Tuber are all StemsThese modified structures are stems, not roots (potato is a stem).
  • Monocot Parallel, Eudicot Branching (MP, EB)Monocot leaves have parallel veins; eudicots have branching veins.
  • Pith Inside, Cortex OutsideGround tissue inside the vascular tissue is pith; outside it is cortex.
  • Collenchyma Can flex and is alive, Sclerenchyma is Stiff and deadCollenchyma gives flexible living support; sclerenchyma has rigid lignified walls and is usually dead at maturity.
  • Xylem eXpired, Phloem PulseXylem conducting cells are dead at maturity; phloem sieve cells are alive (but lack nucleus, ribosomes, vacuole).

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Structure fits function figure: organ level (leaves provide surface area, stems support and elevate, roots anchor and absorb); tissue level in a leaf cross section (dermal tissue protects, vascular tissue supports and transports, ground tissue carries out photosynthesis); cellular level (photosynthetic cells contain chloroplasts, tube-shaped cells transport resources, cells with root hairs increase surface area).
  • Plant body: label shoot system (apical bud, node, internode, leaf with blade and petiole, axillary bud, vegetative shoot, reproductive shoot = flower) and root system (taproot with lateral roots).
  • Tracheid vs vessel element drawing: tracheids long and tapered with pits; vessel elements shorter and wider with perforation plates at the ends.
  • Sieve-tube element with a sieve plate at its end and a companion cell beside it (the companion cell has its own nucleus and ribosomes) connected by plasmodesmata.
  • Plant cell video: cellulose cell wall, large central vacuole, chloroplasts, mitochondria making ATP.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Cell = fundamental unit of life; tissue = group of cells of one or more types doing a specialized function; organ = several tissues carrying out functions.
  • Plants take up water and minerals from below ground and CO2 and light from above ground.
  • Primary root is the first root to emerge from the seed; lateral roots branch off it for better anchorage and water absorption.
  • Main stem function: elongate and orient the shoot to maximize photosynthesis.
  • Leaf jobs: intercept light, exchange gases, dissipate heat, defend against herbivores and pathogens.
  • Plant identification uses leaf shape, vein arrangement, and spatial pattern of leaves.
  • Vascular tissue also gives mechanical support.
  • Vessel elements occur in most angiosperms, a few gymnosperms, and some seedless vascular plants (ferns).
  • Sclereids are boxy and irregular; fibers are long, slender, tapered, in strands (ash tree cross section).
  • Phloem cells also lose some cytoskeletal elements: "they don't have a whole lot left."
  • Plant cell video: cellulose cell wall, large central vacuole, chloroplasts, mitochondria.
35.2

Different meristems generate new cells for primary and secondary growth

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Contrast primary and secondary growth and their meristemsDefine indeterminate growth. Which meristems cause primary growth and which cause secondary growth? What does each primary meristem become?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Indeterminate growth: plants grow their entire life because of meristems (undifferentiated dividing cells, mitosis)
  • leaves and most animals are determinate
  • initials stay behind, so the meristem remains the meristem
  • primary growth = length, from apical meristems at root tips, shoot tips, axillary buds
  • adding elongated, differentiated cells lengthens the stem or root
  • secondary growth = thickness, from 2 lateral meristems: vascular cambium (secondary xylem = wood, secondary phloem) and cork cambium (periderm replaces epidermis)
  • protoderm to dermal, ground meristem to ground, procambium to vascular
  • both kinds of growth happen at once in different parts of one plant
  • no secondary growth in leaves or most monocots.

Common mistake: Saying apical meristems thicken stems: apical = length (primary); only the lateral meristems (vascular and cork cambium) add thickness.

Track cells added by the vascular and cork cambiumDraw a vascular cambium cell dividing over time, then a cork cambium cell. Mark where xylem, phloem, and cork go and label the youngest and oldest cells.Draw and label on paper
  • Vascular cambium divides
  • one daughter stays cambium, the other becomes xylem (inside) or phloem (outside)
  • more cells go inside, so secondary xylem (wood) is much thicker than bark
  • cork cambium adds cork cells only to the outside (her exam answer)
  • youngest xylem, phloem, and cork cells sit next to their cambium
  • oldest are farthest away
  • sequence: X1, then X1 P1, then X1 X2 P1, then X1 X2 P2 P1 with the cambium in the middle.

Common mistake: Putting the youngest xylem at the center: the youngest cells are always next to the cambium; the oldest are pushed farthest away.

Read a twig and plant life spansOn a winter twig, what do the terminal bud-scale scar rings and leaf scars tell you? Define annual, biennial, and perennial.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Terminal bud-scale scar rings mark past positions of the apical bud
  • the segments between them show each year of growth, so counting segments gives years
  • leaf scars mark where leaves attached and sit under axillary buds
  • annual: life cycle in a year or less
  • biennial: two growing seasons
  • perennial: lives many years.

Common mistake: Reading leaf scars as yearly marks: the terminal bud-scale scar rings, not leaf scars, separate the yearly segments.

Lecture video 35.2 (opens online)
Animation - Primary and secondary growth (from her concept page)
Animation - Primary and secondary growth (opens online)
Full notes for 35.2
★ She said this is on the test
  • (outline) Figure 35.11 Visualizing primary and secondary growth (KNOW THESE)Primary growth (apical meristems, length) and secondary growth (lateral meristems, thickness) figures, with labels.
  • Spend some time with this animation. It will help you understand the process and you just might see this again.Secondary growth cell addition: vascular cambium X inward and P outward (more X), cork only outward, youngest next to the cambium.
  • So more xylem cells, secondary xylem cells will be formed on the inside versus our secondary phloem cells being formed on the outsideVascular cambium makes more xylem (inside) than phloem (outside).
  • the youngest cork cell is going to be closer to our cork cambiumYoungest cells are adjacent to the cambium that made them.
  • Keep in mind that a plant can grow throughout its entire life. You guys know this. It is called [indeterminate] growth.Plants show indeterminate growth because of meristems.
  • keep in mind the addition of elongated, differentiated cells is what is actually going to lengthen the stem or the [root]Primary growth lengthens by adding elongated differentiated cells behind the apical meristem.
Big idea
Plants show indeterminate growth because of meristems, undifferentiated tissues of dividing cells where mitosis occurs. Apical meristems at root and shoot tips drive primary growth (length); lateral meristems (vascular cambium and cork cambium) drive secondary growth (thickness). Initials stay in the meristem while other new cells differentiate; primary meristems (protoderm, ground meristem, procambium) produce the three tissue systems. Flowering plants are annuals, biennials or perennials by life-cycle length.

Must know

  • Indeterminate growth: plants grow throughout their entire life because of meristems. Determinate growth: most animals and some plant organs (such as leaves) stop at a set size.
  • Meristem: undifferentiated tissue of dividing cells (mitosis). Initials (stem cells) stay in the meristem, so "the meristem remains the meristem"; the other new cells differentiate into tissues.
  • Primary growth = growth in length, from apical meristems at the tips of roots, shoots, and axillary buds. Adding elongated, differentiated cells lengthens the stem or root; the youngest differentiated cells are nearest the tip.
  • Secondary growth = growth in thickness, from 2 lateral meristems: vascular cambium (secondary xylem = wood, and secondary phloem) and cork cambium (replaces the epidermis with tough periderm).
  • Vascular cambium adds cells both ways: xylem to the inside, phloem to the outside, with more going inside. That is why wood is much thicker than bark.
  • Cork cambium adds cork cells only to the outside (her exam answer).
  • Youngest xylem, phloem, and cork cells sit next to their cambium; the oldest are farthest away.
  • Primary meristems: protoderm makes dermal tissue, ground meristem makes ground tissue, procambium makes vascular tissue.
  • No lateral meristems in leaves, so no secondary growth in leaves (her model). Secondary growth is also absent in most monocots (palms, grasses) and many short-lived eudicots.
  • Primary and secondary growth happen at the same time in different parts of the same plant.
  • Twig: terminal bud-scale scar rings mark past positions of the apical bud and delimit annual growth segments, so the segments show years of growth; leaf scars (where leaves attached) sit under axillary buds.
  • Annuals complete the life cycle in a year or less; biennials need two growing seasons; perennials live for many years.
  • The vascular cambium is not used up: after each division one daughter cell stays a cambium (meristem) cell and the other becomes xylem (inward) or phloem (outward). More divisions make xylem than phloem, so wood gets much thicker than bark (Primary and secondary growth animation).

Terms

meristem
Undifferentiated tissue of dividing cells where mitosis occurs.
indeterminate growth
Growth that continues throughout the plant's life.
determinate growth
Growth that stops once a predetermined size is reached (most animals, some plant organs).
apical meristem
Meristem at the tips of roots and shoots that produces primary growth.
primary growth
Growth in length of roots and shoots.
lateral meristem
Meristem that increases the circumference of stems and roots of woody plants.
secondary growth
Growth in thickness produced by lateral meristems.
vascular cambium
Lateral meristem that adds secondary xylem (wood) inward and secondary phloem outward.
cork cambium
Lateral meristem that produces cork to the outside and replaces the epidermis with periderm.
initials
Stem cells that remain in the meristem and keep producing new cells.
protoderm
Primary meristem that produces dermal tissue.
ground meristem
Primary meristem that produces ground tissue.
procambium
Primary meristem that produces vascular tissue.
leaf primordia
Young structures at the shoot tip that become leaves.
bud scar
Ring of terminal bud-scale scars on a twig marking where the apical bud sat at the end of a past growing season.
leaf scar
Mark under an axillary bud where a leaf used to be attached.
annual
Flowering plant that completes its life cycle in a year or less.
biennial
Flowering plant that needs two growing seasons.
perennial
Flowering plant that lives for many years.

Examples

  • Humans and most animals: determinate growth.
  • Palms and grasses (monocots): no secondary growth.
  • Twig with three years of growth counted by bud scars; a one-year-old branch formed from a previous axillary bud has no bud scars.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Figure 35.11 primary and secondary growth figures are marked "KNOW THESE."
  • Animation on secondary growth: "Spend some time with this animation... you just might see this again."
  • "With our vascular cambium we add cells to the inside but also to the outside... with more cells actually going towards the inside."
  • Cork cambium "only produces cells that go to the outside." Source conflict: the embedded textbook animation says cork cambium can also make some storage tissue toward the inside. On her exam, answer outward only.
  • When discussing secondary growth she refers to stems instead of roots.
  • Initials: "so the meristem remains the meristem." (explanation)
  • Twig: "having those bud scars gives us an indication of how many years this has been growing." Terminal bud-scale scar rings mark past apical-bud positions and delimit annual growth segments.
Memory hooks
  • Apical = Ahead (length), Lateral = Larger aroundApical meristems give primary growth in length; lateral meristems give secondary growth in thickness.
  • Protoderm, Ground meristem, Procambium make Dermal, Ground, VascularEach primary meristem matches one tissue system in order.
  • X goes in, P goes out (X1 X2 | cambium | P2 P1)Vascular cambium puts xylem inside and phloem outside; youngest cells sit next to the cambium.
  • Cork Cambium only Climbs outwardCork cambium adds cork cells only to the outside.
  • 1, 2, Many: annual, biennial, perennialLife cycle of one year or less, two growing seasons, or many years.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Primary growth figure: shoot apical meristem and root apical meristem at the tips; cutaway of the shoot tip showing leaf primordia, apical meristem (brown dome), primary meristems, then mature tissues colored dermal (blue), ground (yellow), vascular (purple).
  • Apical meristem cell fate figure: cell division in apical meristem, daughter cell in primary meristem, division in primary meristem, growing cells, differentiated cells (for example vessel elements). Over time youngest differentiated cells sit near the tip and older ones lower down.
  • Secondary growth figure: lateral meristems as rings (vascular cambium red, cork cambium blue); increased circumference by cell division then cell growth; vascular cambium cell makes X1, X2 inward and P1, P2 outward; cork cambium cell makes C1, C2 outward only; youngest xylem and phloem cells next to the cambium, oldest farthest away.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • After each vascular cambium division one cell stays meristem and the other differentiates, so the cambium is not used up (animation).
  • Bud scales surround the apical bud of a winter twig.
  • A one-year-old branch formed from a previous axillary bud has no bud scars yet.
  • When she discusses secondary growth she uses stems, not roots.
  • A tree has primary growth (young leaves, elongating stem and root tips) and secondary growth (older parts that finished elongating) going on at the same time. Leaves rarely have secondary growth (Primary and secondary growth animation).
35.3

Primary growth lengthens roots and shoots

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Trace the root tip zonesDraw a root tip from the root cap upward. Label the cap and each zone, say what happens there, and where root hairs appear.Draw and label on paper
  • Root cap: covers and protects the root apical meristem, secretes polysaccharide slime to ease passage through soil
  • zone of cell division (apical meristem, mitosis)
  • zone of elongation (cells lengthen, pushing the tip forward)
  • zone of differentiation = zone of maturation (cells specialize
  • root hairs appear here, not at the very tip).

Common mistake: Putting root hairs at the tip: root hairs form only in the zone of differentiation (maturation), above the elongation zone.

Read a root cross section and explain lateral rootsList root layers from outside in. How do eudicot and monocot vascular cylinders differ? Where do lateral roots form and why does it matter?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Epidermis, cortex (mostly parenchyma), endodermis (innermost cortex layer, regulates water and minerals entering the vascular cylinder), pericycle (single layer), vascular cylinder
  • eudicot root: X-shaped lobed xylem core with phloem between the arms
  • monocot root: ring of alternating xylem and phloem around a parenchyma core
  • lateral roots arise from the pericycle and push through (damage) cortex and epidermis
  • this keeps xylem and phloem continuous with the main root (bridge built as a continuation of the road)
  • lateral shoots grow from axillary buds on the surface and do not damage tissue.

Common mistake: Saying lateral roots start in the epidermis or cortex: they start in the pericycle, just outside the vascular cylinder.

Label the shoot tip and explain apical dominanceDraw a shoot tip with its four required labels. Then explain apical dominance and what happens when you snip the apical bud.Draw and label on paper
  • Shoot apical meristem: dome of dividing cells
  • leaf primordia: protect the meristem and become leaves (her Batman ballerina)
  • young leaves
  • axillary buds: form from meristem cells at the bases of leaf primordia
  • the shoot apical meristem gives rise to protoderm (dermal), ground meristem (ground), procambium (vascular), names only
  • apical dominance: the apical bud keeps axillary buds dormant
  • the closest bud is most inhibited
  • snip or shade the tip and axillary buds grow, so the plant gets bushy.

Common mistake: Thinking the farthest bud is most inhibited: the axillary bud closest to the apical bud is the most inhibited.

Compare stem cross sections and leaf anatomySketch a eudicot and a monocot stem cross section. How do you find xylem in a bundle? Then label a leaf from cuticle to lower epidermis.Draw and label on paper
  • Colors: blue dermal, purple vascular, yellow ground
  • eudicot stem: vascular bundles in a ring, xylem inside, phloem outside, pith center, cortex outside the ring
  • monocot stem: bundles scattered in ground tissue
  • xylem cells are larger with thicker walls, phloem cells smaller
  • leaf: waxy cuticle on epidermis reduces water loss
  • palisade mesophyll on top, packed with chloroplasts
  • spongy mesophyll below with air spaces for gas exchange
  • veins = vascular bundles with bundle sheath cells
  • stomata with two guard cells that open and close by osmotic changes
  • no secondary growth in leaves (treated as nonexistent).

Common mistake: Swapping stem patterns: eudicot stem bundles form a ring; monocot stem bundles are scattered.

Lecture video 35.3 (opens online)
Full notes for 35.3
★ She said this is on the test
  • I want you to know the axillary bud. Our leaf [primordia]. And our shoot apical meristem along with the young leaves.Label the shoot tip with these four structures only.
  • the [pericycle] is going to be very important in the [roots] themselvesLateral roots arise from the pericycle.
  • it allows for that xylem and [phloem] to remain continuous, which is very, very, very importantPericycle origin keeps the lateral root's xylem and phloem continuous with the main root.
  • the zone of differentiation is sometimes called the zone of maturation. So keep that in mind.Zone of differentiation = zone of maturation.
  • I just want you to know what those ... pre tissues areProtoderm to dermal, ground meristem to ground, procambium to vascular (names only).
  • Pay attention to the colors. Right. So this tells you the tissue types.Read stem figures by color: blue dermal, purple vascular, yellow ground.
  • Remember [xylem] has thicker walls.Xylem cells are larger with thicker walls; phloem cells are smaller.
  • for our purposes, we will say non-existentNo secondary growth in leaves.
Big idea
Primary growth comes from apical meristems and elongates roots and shoots; in herbaceous plants it is nearly the whole plant, while in woody plants only the newest tips are primary. Roots grow behind a protective root cap through zones of cell division, elongation and differentiation, and form epidermis, cortex, endodermis, pericycle and a vascular cylinder. Shoots grow from a shoot apical meristem with leaf primordia and axillary buds controlled by apical dominance. Eudicot stems have vascular bundles in a ring and monocot stems have scattered bundles; leaves have stomata, guard cells, palisade and spongy mesophyll, and veins with bundle sheaths.

Must know

  • Root cap covers the root tip, protects the apical meristem, and secretes polysaccharide slime to lubricate passage through soil.
  • Root zones from the tip up: zone of cell division (apical meristem), zone of elongation, zone of differentiation (also called zone of maturation). Root hairs appear only in the zone of differentiation, not at the very tip.
  • Root layers from outside in: epidermis, cortex (mostly parenchyma), endodermis (innermost cortex layer, regulates water and minerals entering the vascular cylinder), pericycle (single layer), vascular cylinder.
  • Root vascular cylinder: eudicot = X or plus-shaped lobed xylem core with phloem between the arms; monocot = a ring of alternating xylem and phloem around a parenchyma core. Roots have one central vascular cylinder; stems have vascular bundles (ring in eudicots, scattered in monocots).
  • Lateral (branch) roots arise from the pericycle, push through (damage) the cortex and epidermis, and keep xylem and phloem continuous with the main root (her bridge analogy: the bridge is built as a continuation of the road). Branching is primary growth.
  • Shoot tip figure, know only four labels: shoot apical meristem (dome of dividing cells), leaf primordia (protect the meristem and become leaves, her "Batman ballerina"), young leaves, axillary buds (form from meristem cells at the bases of leaf primordia).
  • The shoot apical meristem gives rise to the three primary meristems (protoderm, ground meristem, procambium), which become the three tissue systems. Know the names and products only.
  • Apical dominance: the apical bud keeps axillary buds dormant; the closer an axillary bud is to the apical bud, the more inhibited it is. Snipping or shading the tip releases axillary buds and makes the plant bushy.
  • Lateral shoots grow from axillary buds on the stem surface and do not damage stem tissue (the buds already connect to xylem and phloem), unlike lateral roots.
  • Eudicot stem: vascular bundles in a ring, xylem toward the inside and phloem toward the outside, cortex outside the ring, pith in the center. Monocot stem: bundles scattered through ground tissue. ID cue: xylem cells are larger with thicker walls; phloem cells are smaller.
  • Leaf: waxy cuticle on the epidermis reduces water loss; stomata are epidermal pores for CO2 and O2 exchange, each flanked by two guard cells that open and close it by changing osmotic balance. Secondary growth in leaves is treated as nonexistent.
  • Mesophyll (leaf ground tissue): palisade on top (packed with chloroplasts) and spongy below (loose, air spaces for gas exchange). Veins are vascular bundles continuous with the stem that also give support; bundle sheath cells around them regulate movement between vascular tissue and mesophyll.

Terms

herbaceous plant
Nonwoody plant whose body is made by primary growth.
root cap
Covering of the root tip that protects the apical meristem and secretes lubricating slime.
zone of cell division
Root region with the apical meristem where mitotic cells are found.
zone of elongation
Root region behind the zone of cell division where cells lengthen.
zone of differentiation
Root region where cells mature into tissues and root hairs appear; also called zone of maturation.
endodermis
Innermost layer of the cortex that regulates passage of water and minerals into the vascular cylinder.
pericycle
Single cell layer around the root vascular cylinder from which lateral roots arise.
vascular cylinder
The central core of vascular tissue in a root (the stele), surrounded by the pericycle; lobed xylem core in eudicots, a ring of alternating xylem and phloem around parenchyma in monocots.
shoot apical meristem
Dome-shaped mass of dividing cells at the shoot tip.
apical dominance
Inhibition of axillary buds by the apical bud.
stoma (stomata)
Pore in the leaf epidermis for exchange of CO2 and O2.
mesophyll
Ground tissue of a leaf, between the upper and lower epidermis.
palisade mesophyll
Upper mesophyll layer packed with chloroplasts.
spongy mesophyll
Lower, loosely packed mesophyll with air spaces for gas exchange.
vein
A vascular bundle in a leaf.
bundle sheath
Cells around a leaf vein that regulate movement between vascular tissue and mesophyll.

Examples

  • Eudicot vs monocot root cross sections (lab slides).
  • Eudicot vs monocot stem cross sections (ring vs scattered bundles).
  • Snipping the apical bud to make a plant bushy.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Zone of differentiation "is sometimes called the zone of maturation. So keep that in mind."
  • Endodermis: "You guys will find this in lab."
  • Pericycle "is going to be very important": lateral roots grow from it so xylem and phloem stay continuous. Analogy: a bridge built as a continuation of the road.
  • Shoot tip figure: "I want you to know the axillary bud, our leaf primordia, and our shoot apical meristem along with the young leaves... that's all that I want you to know from this figure." She calls the leaf primordia a "Batman ballerina." De-scoped on this figure: "don't worry about the [procambium], the [protoderm], ground meristem."
  • Do not need to locate vascular cambium in the primary eudicot stem, or the sclerenchyma cells: "I don't want you guys to know those either."
  • Eudicot vascular bundle "kind of looks like an acorn."
  • Xylem cells are the larger, thicker-walled cells; phloem cells are smaller.
Memory hooks
  • Divide, Elongate, Differentiate (from the tip up)The three root zones in order behind the root cap.
  • Pericycle Produces branch rootsLateral roots arise from the pericycle inside the endodermis.
  • Endodermis = Entry guardThe endodermis regulates water and minerals entering the vascular cylinder.
  • Eudicot stem = Even ring; Monocot = Messy scatterEudicot vascular bundles form a ring; monocot bundles are scattered.
  • Palisade on toP, Spongy Spaces belowPalisade mesophyll is the upper chloroplast-rich layer; spongy mesophyll is lower with air spaces.
  • Snip the Tip, get a BushRemoving the apical bud ends apical dominance so axillary buds grow.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Root tip longitudinal view: root cap at bottom, apical meristem above it, zone of cell division, zone of elongation, zone of differentiation with root hairs; protoderm, ground meristem, procambium.
  • Root cross sections: eudicot (epidermis, cortex, endodermis, pericycle, X-shaped xylem with phloem between arms) vs monocot (epidermis, cortex, endodermis, pericycle, central parenchyma core surrounded by a ring of alternating xylem and phloem).
  • Lateral root emerging from the pericycle and pushing through cortex and epidermis.
  • Shoot tip: label only shoot apical meristem, leaf primordia, young leaves, axillary buds (the primary meristem labels on this figure are not on the exam).
  • Stem cross sections: eudicot (epidermis, cortex, ring of vascular bundles with xylem inside and phloem outside, pith) vs monocot (epidermis, scattered vascular bundles in ground tissue).
  • Leaf cross section: cuticle, upper epidermis, palisade mesophyll, spongy mesophyll, vein (xylem on top, phloem below, bundle sheath), lower epidermis, stoma with two guard cells.
  • Self-test images (labels replaced by numbers): two root cross sections, the leaf cutaway, and the two stem cross sections. Name every number, then check against the labeled slide.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Herbaceous (nonwoody) plants are mostly primary growth; in woody plants only the new tips are primary growth.
  • Primary growth of roots produces epidermis, ground tissue, and vascular tissue.
  • Root hairs make up about 70 to 90% of total root surface area.
  • Stem ground tissue is mainly parenchyma with some collenchyma and sclerenchyma for strength.
  • Leaves develop from leaf primordia along the sides of the shoot apical meristem.
  • Eudicot vascular bundle "kind of looks like an acorn."
  • Not on the exam: locating the vascular cambium or the sclerenchyma cells in the primary eudicot stem micrograph, and the protoderm, procambium, and ground meristem labels on the shoot tip figure.
35.4

Secondary growth increases the diameter of stems and roots in woody plants

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Explain the vascular cambium's job in secondary growthWhich plants show secondary growth? Where does the vascular cambium form, and what does it add to each side?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Secondary growth occurs in gymnosperms and many (most) eudicots, rarely in monocots
  • the vascular cambium is wholly responsible for secondary vascular tissue
  • it starts between primary xylem and primary phloem and becomes a ring of meristematic cells
  • it adds secondary xylem (wood: tracheids, vessel elements, fibers) inside and secondary phloem outside
  • old secondary phloem is sloughed off and does not accumulate
  • epidermis, cortex, and primary phloem are pushed out, crushed, and sloughed off as the stem widens.

Common mistake: Crediting the cork cambium with secondary xylem or phloem: only the vascular cambium makes secondary vascular tissue.

Read tree rings, heartwood, and sapwoodHow do early wood and late wood make one tree ring? What does a thick or thin ring mean? Contrast heartwood and sapwood.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Early wood (spring): thin walls, wide vessels for maximum water delivery
  • late wood (summer): thick walls, more support
  • a ring forms where late wood meets the next early wood, one per year, so rings give age
  • dendrochronology = analysis of ring patterns
  • thick ring = warm or wet year, thin ring = cold or dry year
  • heartwood: older inner secondary xylem that no longer conducts water and minerals
  • sapwood: outer secondary xylem near the vascular cambium that still conducts
  • the water-conducting cells are dead in both.

Common mistake: Saying sapwood is alive and heartwood dead: conducting cells are dead in both; heartwood simply no longer conducts.

Define periderm and bark in a woody stemList the layers of a woody stem from the center outward. Bracket the periderm and the bark, and explain suberin and lenticels.List from memory
  • Pith, primary xylem, secondary xylem (wood), vascular cambium, secondary phloem, cork cambium, cork
  • periderm = cork cambium + cork
  • bark = everything outside the vascular cambium: secondary phloem + periderm
  • cork cambium forms in the outer cortex and re-forms deeper over time
  • cork cells deposit waxy suberin before they die, sealing out water and gas
  • cork replaces the epidermis
  • lenticels = pores in the periderm for gas exchange (stomata were lost with the epidermis).

Common mistake: Calling bark only the cork: bark is everything outside the vascular cambium, including secondary phloem and periderm.

Lecture video 35.4 (opens online)
Full notes for 35.4
★ She said this is on the test
  • So some terms that you need to know. The [periderm] consist of that cork cambium along with the cork. And our bark is going to include the layers of our [periderm], but also our [phloem] layers as well... Make sure that you know that.Periderm = cork cambium + cork; bark = periderm + secondary phloem (everything outside the vascular cambium).
  • [periderm] is our, uh, cork cambium and our cork. Remember I said, make sure you know that.Periderm = cork cambium + cork (repeated).
  • The vascular cambium is wholly responsible for the production of secondary vascular tissue. Okay, so that's the job of our vascular cambium.Only the vascular cambium makes secondary xylem and secondary phloem.
Big idea
Secondary growth adds thickness through two lateral meristems and occurs in gymnosperms and most (many) eudicots but rarely in monocots. The vascular cambium is a ring of meristematic cells that adds secondary xylem (wood) inward and secondary phloem outward; wood records tree rings of early and late wood. The cork cambium makes cork cells with suberin that, with the cork cambium, form the periderm replacing the epidermis. Bark is everything outside the vascular cambium, and lenticels allow gas exchange through the periderm.

Must know

  • Secondary growth occurs in gymnosperms and most eudicots (she says "many"); it is rare in monocots.
  • The vascular cambium is wholly responsible for secondary vascular tissue. It starts as a layer between primary xylem and primary phloem, becomes a ring of meristematic cells, and adds secondary xylem inside and secondary phloem outside.
  • Wood = accumulated secondary xylem (tracheids, vessel elements, fibers).
  • Early wood (spring): thin walls, wide vessels for maximum water delivery. Late wood (summer): thick walls, more support. A tree ring forms where late wood meets the next early wood, one per year, so rings give the tree's age.
  • Dendrochronology = analysis of tree ring patterns (past climate and ecological conditions). Thick ring = warm or wet year; thin ring = cold or dry year.
  • Heartwood: older inner secondary xylem that no longer transports water and minerals. Sapwood: outer secondary xylem near the vascular cambium that still transports.
  • Old secondary phloem is sloughed off and does not accumulate. As the stem widens, epidermis, cortex, and primary phloem are pushed out, crushed, and sloughed off.
  • Cork cambium forms in the outer cortex and re-forms deeper over time. Cork cells deposit waxy suberin before they die, making a water and gas impermeable barrier; cork replaces the epidermis.
  • Periderm = cork cambium + cork (she said it twice).
  • Bark = all tissues outside the vascular cambium: secondary phloem plus periderm.
  • Lenticels: pores in the periderm that allow gas exchange (stomata are in the epidermis, which cork has replaced).
  • Woody stem order from the center out: pith, primary xylem, secondary xylem (wood), vascular cambium, secondary phloem, cork cambium, cork. Periderm = the last two; bark = everything from secondary phloem outward.

Terms

secondary xylem
Xylem made by the vascular cambium toward the inside; wood.
secondary phloem
Phloem made by the vascular cambium toward the outside.
wood
Accumulated secondary xylem made of tracheids, vessel elements and fibers.
early wood
Spring secondary xylem with thinner walls and wide vessels for water delivery.
late wood
Summer secondary xylem with thicker walls that adds support.
tree ring
Visible boundary where late wood and early wood meet; marks one year of growth.
dendrochronology
Analysis of tree ring growth patterns.
heartwood
Older inner secondary xylem that no longer transports water and minerals.
sapwood
Outer secondary xylem near the vascular cambium that still transports water and minerals.
cork cells
Cells made outward by the cork cambium that deposit suberin and die.
suberin
Waxy substance in cork cell walls that blocks water and gas.
periderm
The cork cambium plus cork; replaces the epidermis.
bark
All tissues external to the vascular cambium, including secondary phloem and periderm.
lenticels
Pores in the periderm that allow gas exchange.
vascular ray
Structure produced by some vascular cambium stem cells, running across the secondary tissues.

Examples

  • Gymnosperms and most eudicots: secondary growth.
  • Monocots: secondary growth rare.
  • Tree trunk anatomy: heartwood, sapwood, vascular cambium, secondary phloem, layers of periderm making bark.
  • Twig and tree trunk both have lenticels.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "The periderm consists of that cork cambium along with the cork. And our bark is going to include the layers of our periderm, but also our phloem layers as well... Make sure that you know that." She repeats: "periderm is our cork cambium and our cork. Remember I said, make sure you know that."
  • "The vascular cambium is wholly responsible for the production of secondary vascular tissue."
  • "You will look at a figure just like this in lab" (vascular cambium ring in cross section).
  • "We're not going to focus on the roots" for secondary growth.
  • The epidermis gets replaced by cork.
Memory hooks
  • Periderm = Cork Cambium + CorkThe periderm is the cork cambium plus the cork it makes.
  • Bark is Beyond the vascular cambiumBark is all tissues outside the vascular cambium: secondary phloem plus periderm.
  • Early = Easy water (thin walls); Late = Load bearing (thick walls)Spring early wood moves lots of water; summer late wood adds support.
  • Heartwood is Halted, Sapwood Still flowsHeartwood no longer transports; sapwood near the cambium still does.
  • Gym and Eu grow wide, Mono Mostly notSecondary growth occurs in gymnosperms and most eudicots, rarely monocots.
  • Suberin SealsCork cells deposit waxy suberin to make a water and gas impermeable barrier.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Primary and secondary growth in a woody stem (Figure 35.19 style): (1) primary growth nearing completion: pith, primary xylem, vascular cambium, primary phloem, cortex, epidermis. (2) secondary growth begins; (3) vascular cambium stem cells give rise to vascular rays; (4) cork cambium develops from parenchyma in the cortex, labels secondary xylem, vascular cambium, secondary phloem, primary phloem, first cork cambium, cork. (5) year 2: more secondary xylem (two years of production) and phloem; (6) outermost tissues rupture and slough off; (7) cork cambium re-forms deeper in the cortex; (8) each cork cambium and its tissues form a layer of periderm; (9) bark is all tissue outside the vascular cambium.
  • Longitudinal view on left: epidermis, cortex, primary phloem, vascular cambium, primary xylem, pith at the top; lower down periderm (mainly cork cambia and cork), primary phloem, secondary phloem, vascular cambium, secondary xylem, primary xylem, pith.
  • Tree trunk: heartwood (center), sapwood, vascular cambium, secondary phloem, periderm layers, bark; early wood and late wood forming rings.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Plants that get very tall would topple over if they did not also get wider.
  • Primary and secondary growth occur at the same time (see 35.2).
  • Not on the exam: vascular cambium position in a woody root (outside the primary xylem, inside the primary phloem and pericycle). She said "we're not going to focus on the roots."
  • Some vascular cambium stem cells give rise to vascular rays.
  • Cork cambium develops from parenchyma cells in the cortex.
  • Flowchart (Figure 35.24): apical meristem to primary meristems to primary tissues; vascular cambium to secondary xylem and phloem; cork cambium to cork (periderm).
35.5

Growth, morphogenesis, and cell differentiation produce the plant body

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Explain plant development and the model plant ArabidopsisDefine development and developmental plasticity with her water plant example. Name the three processes of development. Why is Arabidopsis the model plant, and what are its genome facts?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Development: cells formed by growth give rise to specialized tissues, organs, organisms
  • developmental plasticity = effect of environment on development
  • water plant: surface leaves flat and broad, underwater leaves stay tubular (finely divided)
  • growth (irreversible size increase), morphogenesis (body form and cell organization), cell differentiation (same genes, different cells)
  • Arabidopsis: small size, short generation time, many seeds, small genome
  • mustard family
  • first plant genome sequenced
  • about 27,000 genes on 5 pairs of chromosomes
  • easily given transgenes
  • CRISPR knockouts reveal gene function
  • populations from different environments look different.

Common mistake: Saying 5 chromosomes or 27,000 chromosomes: it has 5 pairs of chromosomes carrying about 27,000 genes.

Lecture video 35.5 (opens online)
Full notes for 35.5
★ She said this is on the test
  • [Arabidopsis] is something that I want you guys to know.Why it is the model plant (small, short generation, many seeds, small genome), mustard family, first plant genome, about 27,000 genes, 5 chromosome pairs, transgenes and CRISPR knockouts.
Big idea
Development is the process by which cells form specialized tissues, organs and organisms, and developmental plasticity is the effect of environment on development. Growth, morphogenesis and cell differentiation together produce a multicellular body. Arabidopsis is the model plant because of its small size, short generation time, many seeds and small genome; it was the first plant genome sequenced (about 27,000 genes on 5 pairs of chromosomes).

Must know

  • Development: cells formed by growth give rise to specialized tissues, organs, and organisms.
  • Developmental plasticity = the effect of environment on development. Her example: on the same water plant, leaves at the surface become flat and broad, while underwater leaves "stay tubular" (in the figure they are finely divided and threadlike).
  • Three processes of development: growth (irreversible increase in size), morphogenesis (development of body form and cell organization), cell differentiation (cells with the exact same genes become different).
  • Arabidopsis is the model organism for plant genetics: small size, short generation time, prolific seed production, small genome.
  • Arabidopsis: mustard family, first plant to have its whole genome sequenced, about 27,000 genes on 5 pairs of chromosomes.
  • Arabidopsis is easily given transgenes (genes from another organism); CRISPR knockouts remove a gene's function to learn what it does.
  • Arabidopsis populations from different environments look different (leaf arrangement, leaf shape, shoot growth); plasticity can lead to evolutionary differences.

Terms

development
Process by which cells form specialized tissues, organs and organisms.
developmental plasticity
The effect of environment on development.
growth
Irreversible increase in size.
morphogenesis
Development of body form and cell organization.
cell differentiation
Process by which cells with the same genes become different from each other.
model organism
A species studied in depth so findings can be applied to others; Arabidopsis for plants.
transgene
A gene from a different organism introduced into the genome of another organism.
knockout
Removing the function of a specific gene (using CRISPR) to study what that gene does.

Examples

  • Aquatic plant: flat surface leaves vs underwater leaves she calls tubular (finely divided in the figure); developmental plasticity.
  • Arabidopsis (mustard family): model plant, first sequenced plant genome, populations differ in appearance by environment.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "Arabidopsis is something that I want you guys to know."
  • Numbers to know: about 27,000 genes, 5 pairs of chromosomes, first plant genome sequenced.
Memory hooks
  • Grow, Mold, DifferentiateDevelopment = growth, morphogenesis (body form), cell differentiation.
  • Small, Short, Seeds, Small genomeFour reasons Arabidopsis is a model organism: small size, short generation time, many seeds, small genome.
  • 27K genes on 5 pairs, 1st sequencedArabidopsis has about 27,000 genes on 5 chromosome pairs and was the first plant genome sequenced.
  • Plasticity = Place changes the plantDevelopmental plasticity is the environment's effect on development (tubular vs flat leaves).

Be able to draw / explain
  • Developmental plasticity figure: on the same plant, broad flat leaves at the surface and finely divided underwater leaves (she calls the underwater leaves tubular).
  • Arabidopsis populations viewed from above: differences in leaf arrangement, leaf shape and shoot growth.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Research goal: a blueprint of plant development by finding the function and pathway of every Arabidopsis gene, then extrapolating to other plants.
Ch 36

Exam sheet: Chapter 36: Resource Acquisition and Transport in Vascular Plants

Memorize this, review it last
  1. Xylem: water and minerals, roots to shoots, mainly pulled by negative pressure; root pressure is a minor positive push. Phloem: sugar, source to sink in either direction, pushed by positive pressure.
  2. Apoplast = cell walls, extracellular spaces, interior of dead cells. Symplast = cytosol of living cells plus plasmodesmata.
  3. Three routes: apoplastic, symplastic (cross one membrane), transmembrane (cross repeatedly, SLOWEST).
  4. Proton pump (ATP) pushes H+ out; H+ flowing back cotransports sucrose and nitrate. K+ channels in guard cells open and close stomata.
  5. Water flows from HIGHER to LOWER water potential. No math.
  6. Flaccid cell in a solution more concentrated than its contents: water leaves, plasmolysis. In a less concentrated solution: water enters, cell becomes turgid. Turgor loss causes wilting.
  7. Perforation plates join vessel elements; sieve plates join sieve-tube elements. Tracheids/vessel elements: no cytoplasm. Sieve-tube elements: cytoplasm, few organelles.
  8. Root path (draw): root hair, epidermis, cortex, endodermis with Casparian strip, pericycle, xylem. Casparian strip blocks the apoplast and forces a selective membrane crossing (keeps out toxins).
  9. Mineral uptake into roots = active transport (against the gradient).
  10. Root pressure: night, positive, minor, guttation. Main force: transpiration pull (cohesion-tension), negative pressure.
  11. Cohesion = water to water; adhesion = water to xylem wall (Spider-Man). Both hydrogen bonds. Cavitation = vapor pocket from drought or freezing.
  12. Bulk flow vs diffusion: pressure potential not solute potential, hollow dead xylem cells, whole solution, much faster; xylem bulk flow is solar powered.
  13. 95% of water loss through stomata. Stoma = the hole; guard cells change shape. Open by day, closed at night.
  14. Transpiration rises when SUNNY, WARM, DRY, WINDY. Transpiration cools leaves (evaporative cooling).
  15. Source = net producer (mature leaves). Sink = net consumer (roots, buds, fruits). Storage root: sink in summer, source in spring. Sinks use the nearest sources.
  16. Her loading model: active sugar loading uses proton pumps and H+/sucrose cotransport. Her sink model: sugar unloads by passive diffusion and water follows by osmosis.
  17. Pressure flow (draw): xylem beside sieve tube; 1 load sugar at source, water in from xylem; 2 positive pressure pushes sap; 3 unload sugar at sink; 4 water recycled to xylem. High pressure at source, low at sink.
  18. Phloem = information superhighway: systemic transport of macromolecules and viruses; defense signals switch on genes in uninfected tissue.
36.1

Adaptations for acquiring resources were key steps in the evolution of vascular plants

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Contrast how xylem and phloem move resourcesWhat does xylem carry, and what does phloem carry? For each, give the direction of movement and whether it moves by positive or negative pressure.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Xylem: water and dissolved minerals
  • roots to shoots (one way)
  • PULLED up by NEGATIVE pressure from evaporation at the leaves
  • root pressure is only a minor positive push. Phloem: sugars (products of photosynthesis)
  • from where they are made or stored to where they are needed, in EITHER direction (mature leaves down to roots, or storage root up to new leaves)
  • PUSHED by POSITIVE pressure.

Common mistake: Saying only phloem ever has positive pressure. Xylem is mainly pulled, but root pressure is a minor positive push.

Explain how land plants evolved to get resourcesHow did algal ancestors get water, minerals and CO2? What did life on land favor, what made long-distance transport possible, and how do stomata and mycorrhizae help?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Algal ancestors were aquatic: absorbed water, minerals and CO2 directly from the surrounding water
  • early nonvascular land plants lived in shallow water with aerial shoots
  • land favored taller plants, flat appendages (leaves), multicellular branching roots, efficient transport
  • long-distance transport was only possible after XYLEM and PHLOEM evolved
  • stomata let CO2 in but most water is lost through them, so shoots are a compromise between photosynthesis and water loss
  • MYCORRHIZAE = mutualism of roots and soil fungi hyphae, more surface area for water and minerals, especially PHOSPHATE
  • helped plants colonize land.

Common mistake: Thinking mycorrhizae make sugar for the plant. The fungus adds absorbing surface area, especially for phosphate.

Lecture video 36.1 (opens online)
Full notes for 36.1
Big idea
Algal ancestors took water, minerals and CO2 straight from the surrounding water. Life on land favored taller plants with flat appendages, multicellular branching roots and efficient long-distance transport, which became possible once xylem and phloem evolved. Water and minerals are pulled up from the roots by negative pressure; sugars are pushed by positive pressure from where they are made or stored to where they are needed. Stomata create a compromise between taking in CO2 and losing water, and mycorrhizae help roots take up water and minerals.

Must know

  • Algal ancestors were aquatic: they absorbed water, minerals and CO2 directly from the surrounding water.
  • Early nonvascular land plants lived in shallow water and had aerial shoots.
  • Natural selection on land favored taller plants with flat appendages (leaves), multicellular branching roots and efficient transport.
  • Long-distance transport of water, minerals and sugar was only possible after XYLEM and PHLOEM evolved.
  • Xylem carries water and minerals from roots to shoots. Phloem carries photosynthetic products from where they are made to where they are needed.
  • Water and minerals are PULLED up by NEGATIVE pressure generated by evaporation from leaves. Sugars are PUSHED by POSITIVE pressure, in either direction (up or down).
  • Stomata let CO2 diffuse into the leaf, but most water a plant loses evaporates through them: shoot adaptations are a compromise between photosynthesis and water loss.
  • MYCORRHIZAE: mutualism between roots and hyphae of soil fungi. The fungus increases surface area for absorbing water and minerals, especially PHOSPHATE.
  • Mutualism with fungi is thought to have helped plants colonize land.

Terms

xylem
Vascular tissue that transports water and minerals from the roots to the shoots.
phloem
Vascular tissue that transports photosynthetic products (sugars) from where they are made to where they are needed.
mycorrhizae
Mutualistic associations between roots and the hyphae of soil fungi that increase surface area for absorbing water and minerals, especially phosphate.
negative pressure
A pulling pressure; it moves water and minerals up the xylem and is generated by evaporation from the leaves.
positive pressure
A pushing pressure; it moves sugars in the phloem from sources to where they are needed.

Examples

  • Algal ancestors: aquatic, so every resource (water, minerals, CO2) came from the surrounding water.
  • Mycorrhizal fungi: increase absorptive surface area, especially for phosphate; this mutualism helped plants colonize land.
  • Figure: sugars move from mature leaves down to roots, and also from a storage root up to new leaves (sugars move both ways).
What she stressed in lecture
  • Xylem and phloem had to evolve for long-distance transport: "This was only possible after the evolution of xylem and phloem."
  • Phosphate is "one of the big ones that the fungi will help with."
  • Shoot adaptations are compromises between photosynthesis and water loss; she returns to this in 36.4.
  • Transcript says 90% of water is lost through stomata in 36.1, but the outline and the 36.4 lecture say 95% (use 95%).
Memory hooks
  • Xylem goes eXit up; Phloem carries PhotosynthateXylem carries water and minerals up from roots to shoots; phloem carries photosynthetic products (sugars) where needed.
  • Water is Pulled, sugar is PushedWater and minerals move by negative pressure (pull); sugars move by positive pressure (push).
  • Myco = fungus, rrhiza = root, P for PhosphateMycorrhizae are root-fungus mutualisms that boost uptake of water and minerals, especially phosphate.
  • Tall plants need Flat leaves, Branching roots, TransportSelection favored taller plants with flat appendages, multicellular branching roots and efficient transport.

  • Same image, right side: sugars are pushed by positive pressure both ways. Green arrow from a mature leaf down to the roots (from mature leaves to roots). 36.2

Different mechanisms transport substances over short or long distances

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Name the two pathways and three routesDefine the apoplast and the symplast. Then name the three routes for water and solutes, say what each crosses, and identify the slowest.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper

APOPLAST = everything outside plasma membranes of living cells: cell walls, extracellular spaces, interior of DEAD cells (tracheids, vessel elements). SYMPLAST = cytosol of all living cells plus the PLASMODESMATA connecting them. Apoplastic route: through cell walls and extracellular spaces. Symplastic route: cross ONE plasma membrane, then cytosol to cytosol through plasmodesmata. Transmembrane route: cross plasma membranes REPEATEDLY cell to cell; the SLOWEST.

Common mistake: Calling the apoplast only cell walls. It also includes extracellular spaces and the interior of dead cells.

Explain proton pumps, cotransport and ion channelsHow do proton pumps set the membrane potential in plant cells? How does the H+ gradient bring sucrose and nitrate into the cell? What do K+ channels in guard cells do?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • PROTON PUMP uses ATP to move H+ out of the cytoplasm into the extracellular fluid
  • inside becomes negative, outside positive (membrane potential). COTRANSPORT: H+ flows back in down its gradient and carries another solute in against its gradient: SUCROSE (a neutral solute) or NITRATE (an ion the plant needs). ION CHANNELS let only certain ions pass
  • K+ channels in GUARD CELLS help open and close stomata.

Common mistake: Saying the pump moves sucrose directly. The pump moves H+; H+ flowing back in through a cotransporter brings sucrose or nitrate.

Predict plasmolysis, turgor and wiltingWhich way does free water move by water potential? Predict what happens to a flaccid cell placed in a higher and in a lower solute concentration, and explain wilting.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • OSMOSIS = diffusion of FREE water
  • free water moves from HIGHER to LOWER water potential (set by solute concentration and pressure
  • no math). Flaccid cell in HIGHER solute concentration: loses water, PLASMOLYSIS, protoplast pulls away from the wall. Flaccid cell in LOWER solute concentration (pure water): gains water, becomes TURGID
  • protoplast presses on the wall (TURGOR PRESSURE, positive). Loss of turgor causes WILTING, reversible by watering. AQUAPORINS are membrane proteins that speed water diffusion.

Common mistake: Reversing it: plasmolysis happens in a HIGHER solute concentration, and water flows from higher to lower water potential.

Match plates and conducting cells for bulk flowWhat is bulk flow? Which plates connect vessel elements and which connect sieve-tube elements? Which conducting cells have cytoplasm?List from memory
  • BULK FLOW = movement of a fluid driven by a PRESSURE gradient
  • used for long-distance transport. Xylem: tracheids and vessel elements, NO cytoplasm at maturity
  • PERFORATION PLATES connect VESSEL ELEMENTS
  • tracheids pass water through pits. Phloem: SIEVE-TUBE ELEMENTS have cytoplasm but very FEW organelles
  • porous SIEVE PLATES connect them. Diffusion, active transport and bulk flow act together to move resources.

Common mistake: Swapping the plates. Perforation plates join vessel elements (xylem); sieve plates join sieve-tube elements (phloem).
