Full notes for 36.2
★ She said this is on the test
  • Plants themselves have two major transport pathways that you should know[:] the [apoplast] and the [symplast].Apoplast = walls, extracellular spaces, dead cell interiors; symplast = cytosol of living cells plus plasmodesmata.
  • I do want you guys to know that if a flaccid or a limp cell is placed in an environment with a higher solute concentration, then the cell will lose water and undergo [plasmolysis].Flaccid cell in higher solute concentration loses water and plasmolyzes; in lower solute concentration it gains water and becomes turgid.
  • So remember we've got perforation plates that are connecting our vessel elements. And we've got the porous [sieve] plates that are connecting the sieve tube elements.Perforation plates join vessel elements; sieve plates join sieve-tube elements.
  • Keep in mind water potential. We're still talking about high concentrations of water versus lower concentrations of water.Free water moves from higher to lower water potential; know the direction only ("we're not going to go in-depth into the math").
  • So again [cotransport] here of [solutes] which are neutral. And also things like nitrate which the plant needs.The H+ gradient from proton pumps drives cotransport of sucrose and nitrate into the cell.
  • So remember we talked about the fact that that stoma can close down or it can open up.K+ ion channels in guard cells help open and close stomata.
Big idea
Plants have two major transport pathways, the apoplast and the symplast, and three routes for water and solutes: apoplastic, symplastic and transmembrane. Short-distance transport across membranes uses proton pumps, cotransport and ion channels, and water moves by osmosis from higher to lower water potential. Solute concentration and pressure set water potential, which explains plasmolysis, turgor and wilting. Long-distance transport uses bulk flow through xylem and phloem.

Must know

  • Two major transport pathways: APOPLAST = everything outside the plasma membranes of living cells (cell walls, extracellular spaces, interior of dead cells such as tracheids and vessel elements). SYMPLAST = cytosol of all living cells plus the PLASMODESMATA that connect them.
  • Three routes: APOPLASTIC (cell walls and extracellular spaces); SYMPLASTIC (cross ONE plasma membrane, then cytosol to cytosol through plasmodesmata); TRANSMEMBRANE (cross plasma membranes REPEATEDLY). Transmembrane is the SLOWEST.
  • Plant membrane potential is set by PROTON PUMPS that use ATP to move H+ out of the cytoplasm into the extracellular fluid.
  • COTRANSPORT: H+ flowing back in down its gradient carries another solute in against its gradient: SUCROSE (a neutral solute) or NITRATE (an ion the plant needs).
  • ION CHANNELS let only certain ions pass. Example: K+ channels in GUARD CELLS help open and close stomata.
  • OSMOSIS = diffusion of FREE water (water not bound to solutes or surfaces). Free water flows from HIGHER to LOWER water potential. Water potential includes solute concentration and physical pressure. No math or equations.
  • PRESSURE POTENTIAL can be positive (push: solution expelled from a syringe) or negative (pull: solution drawn into a syringe). Water in living cells is usually under positive pressure.
  • PROTOPLAST = living part of the cell, including the plasma membrane. TURGOR PRESSURE = positive pressure of the protoplast against the cell wall.
  • Flaccid cell in a HIGHER solute concentration: loses water, PLASMOLYSIS (protoplast pulls away from the wall). Flaccid cell in a LOWER solute concentration: gains water, TURGID. Turgor loss causes WILTING, reversible by watering.
  • AQUAPORINS: transport proteins in the plasma membrane that facilitate water diffusion; opening and closing them changes the rate of osmosis.
  • BULK FLOW = movement of a fluid driven by a PRESSURE gradient; needed for long-distance transport through tracheids and vessel elements (xylem) and sieve-tube elements (phloem). Mature tracheids and vessel elements have NO cytoplasm; sieve-tube elements have cytoplasm but very FEW organelles.
  • PERFORATION PLATES connect VESSEL ELEMENTS; porous SIEVE PLATES connect SIEVE-TUBE ELEMENTS. Diffusion, active transport and bulk flow act together to move resources.

Terms

apoplast
Everything outside the plasma membranes of living cells: cell walls, extracellular spaces and the interior of dead cells such as vessel elements and tracheids.
symplast
The cytosol of all living cells in a plant plus the plasmodesmata that connect them.
plasmodesmata
Cytoplasmic channels that allow symplastic flow from cell to cell.
apoplastic route
Water and solutes move through cell walls and extracellular spaces without entering a cell.
symplastic route
Water and solutes cross one plasma membrane, then move through the cytosol from cell to cell via plasmodesmata.
transmembrane route
Water and solutes repeatedly cross plasma membranes as they pass from cell to cell; the slowest route.
proton pump
Membrane pump that moves H+ out of the cell and establishes membrane potential in plant cells.
cotransport
Using the flow of H+ back down its gradient to carry another solute, such as sucrose or nitrate, into the cell.
ion channel
Membrane channel that lets only certain ions pass, such as the K+ channel of guard cells.
osmosis
Diffusion of free water across a membrane.
free water
Water not bound to solutes or surfaces.
water potential
Physical property that predicts the direction of water flow; free water flows from higher to lower water potential.
pressure potential
The physical pressure on a solution; can be positive (push) or negative (pull).
protoplast
The living part of a plant cell, including the plasma membrane.
turgor pressure
Positive pressure exerted by the protoplast against the cell wall.
flaccid
Limp; a cell without turgor pressure.
turgid
Swollen and firm; a cell that has taken in water and presses against its wall.
plasmolysis
Shrinking of the protoplast away from the cell wall when a cell loses water to a higher solute concentration.
wilting
Drooping of a plant caused by loss of turgor.
aquaporins
Transport proteins in the plasma membrane that facilitate the diffusion of water.
bulk flow
Movement of a fluid driven by a pressure gradient, used for long-distance transport in xylem and phloem.

Examples

  • Sucrose-H+ cotransport: a neutral solute brought into the cell as H+ flows back in.
  • Nitrate-H+ cotransport: nitrate moved from low to high concentration along with H+.
  • Potassium ion channels in guard cells: help open and close stomata.
  • Syringe: drawing solution in = negative pressure; pushing it out = positive pressure.
  • Video: a leaf of the pondweed Elodea placed in concentrated table salt solution; cells lose water by osmosis and undergo plasmolysis (cell contents collapse as the membrane pulls away from the wall).
  • Wilted houseplant: turgor loss, reversed by watering.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "Two major transport pathways that you should know: the apoplast and the symplast."
  • The transmembrane route is the slowest route.
  • Water potential: "we're not going to go in-depth into the math"; know direction of flow (higher to lower) and the flaccid / plasmolyzed / turgid outcomes.
  • "I do want you guys to know that if a flaccid or limp cell is placed in an environment with a higher solute concentration, then the cell will lose water and undergo plasmolysis."
  • Diffusion, active transport and bulk flow all act together to transport resources.
  • "Osmosis, as we know, is the diffusion of free water."
Memory hooks
  • Apoplast = Around the outsideThe apoplast is everything outside plasma membranes: walls, extracellular spaces, dead cell interiors.
  • Symplast = Shared cytosolThe symplast is the cytosol of living cells joined by plasmodesmata.
  • Transmembrane = transit through every doorThe transmembrane route crosses membrane after membrane, so it is the slowest.
  • Water runs High to LowFree water flows from higher water potential to lower water potential.
  • Plasmolysis = Plasma membrane leaves the wallIn a higher solute solution the protoplast shrinks away from the cell wall.
  • Aquaporins = water doorsAquaporins are membrane proteins that speed water diffusion.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Apoplast/symplast diagram: row of cells with cell walls; apoplastic route drawn along the cell walls and extracellular spaces (darker tan region); symplastic route through cytosol and plasmodesmata (lighter peach region); transmembrane route crossing membrane after membrane through each cell (green arrows).
  • Proton pump and cotransport: ATP-driven pump moves H+ from cytoplasm to extracellular fluid (high H+ outside). H+/sucrose cotransporter and H+/nitrate cotransporter bring H+ back in with the solute. K+ ion channel lets K+ pass.
  • Water relations: initial flaccid cell → in higher solute solution, water exits and protoplast shrinks from wall (plasmolyzed); → in lower solute solution, water enters and cell becomes turgid.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Plasma membrane permeability controls short-distance movement. Plants have the same types of pumps and transport proteins as animals (chapter 7), with some differences.
  • "Potential" is water's capacity to do work: water entering a cell makes it expand and push on its surroundings.
  • Water molecules are small enough to cross the membrane, but unaided diffusion is not enough; aquaporins speed it.
  • Branching leaf veins keep every cell within a few cells of vascular tissue.
  • Video: an Elodea leaf in concentrated table salt solution loses water by osmosis and plasmolyzes.
  • (Lower priority: "we're not going to go in-depth into the math") Water potential equations and MPa values.
36.3

Transpiration drives the transport of water and minerals from roots to shoots via the xylem

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Trace water and minerals from soil into root xylemDraw a root strip from root hair to xylem and label each layer. Show the three routes and explain what the Casparian strip does and why minerals need active transport.Draw and label on paper
  • Labels in order: root hair, epidermis, cortex, endodermis with Casparian strip, pericycle, xylem (vascular cylinder). Most absorption near ROOT TIPS
  • root hairs add surface area. Mineral uptake = ACTIVE transport (higher concentration in root than soil)
  • water follows by osmosis. Apoplastic, symplastic and transmembrane routes cross the cortex. CASPARIAN STRIP: waxy band in ENDODERMAL walls BLOCKS the apoplast
  • apoplastic water and minerals must cross a selective endodermal plasma membrane, which keeps out toxins
  • material already in the symplast continues through plasmodesmata. Endodermis = last checkpoint. Endodermal and pericycle cells discharge into the apoplast, then into tracheids and vessel elements (no plasmodesmata into dead xylem).

Common mistake: Thinking water passes through the Casparian strip. It is a barrier in the endodermis; water crosses a plasma membrane instead.

Explain what lifts xylem sap to the leavesDescribe how xylem sap moves from root to leaf and out a stoma. Explain the cohesion-tension hypothesis and compare transpiration pull with root pressure.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • TRANSPIRATION: water evaporates in leaf air spaces (spongy mesophyll) and water vapor diffuses out through open stomata
  • the air-water interface retreats into mesophyll cell walls and surface tension creates NEGATIVE pressure that pulls water from the xylem. COHESION-TENSION: cohesion transmits the pull down the whole xylem to the roots
  • xylem sap is MAINLY PULLED by negative pressure. ROOT PRESSURE: at night roots pump mineral ions into the xylem, water follows from the cortex, positive push
  • causes GUTTATION
  • MINOR, too weak for a tall tree.

Common mistake: Making root pressure the main force. Xylem sap is mainly pulled by transpiration; root pressure is a minor nighttime push.

Distinguish cohesion, adhesion and cavitationDefine cohesion and adhesion and name the bond behind both. How does adhesion help against gravity? What is cavitation, what causes it, and what stops xylem collapsing?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • COHESION = water molecule to water molecule. ADHESION = water molecule to something else (xylem cell walls), like Spider-Man
  • helps offset the force of gravity. Both come from HYDROGEN BONDS. CAVITATION = a water VAPOR pocket from drought stress or freezing that breaks the water chain
  • can kill the plant. Thick secondary walls keep tracheids and vessel elements from collapsing under negative pressure (like a straw).

Common mistake: Swapping the terms. Cohesion is water to water; adhesion is water to the xylem wall. Cavitation is a vapor pocket, not wall collapse.

Compare bulk flow with diffusion in the xylemList four ways bulk flow in the xylem differs from diffusion. Does xylem bulk flow need energy from the plant? Which step in the root does?List from memory
  • Bulk flow: driven by PRESSURE potential, not solute potential
  • in the xylem it runs through hollow DEAD cells, not across membranes of living cells
  • moves the ENTIRE solution
  • much FASTER than diffusion. Maintained against gravity by cohesion-tension. Driven by transpiration, so it needs NO energy from the plant (solar powered). But mineral ions are ACTIVELY loaded into the root xylem
  • water follows by osmosis, which builds root pressure (positive pressure).

Common mistake: Saying bulk flow is driven by solute potential. It is driven by pressure potential and moves the whole solution.

Full notes for 36.3
★ She said this is on the test
  • Make sure you know how to describe the movement of [xylem], and that you know what structures take part in this movement.Trace water root hair, cortex, endodermis/Casparian strip, pericycle, xylem, leaf mesophyll, stoma; name cohesion, adhesion, transpiration.
  • Xylem sap is going to be mainly pulled up by negative pressure rather than pushed from below by root pressure.Cohesion-tension (transpiration pull) is the main mechanism; root pressure is a minor positive push.
  • So spend some time with this concept.Bulk flow vs diffusion: pressure potential not solute potential, hollow dead xylem cells, whole solution, much faster; solar powered.
  • So our permeable membrane remember it's selective is going to prevent the uptake of any toxins that are present.The Casparian strip stops apoplastic material and forces it across a selective membrane; material already in the symplast continues through plasmodesmata.
  • [Cohesion is two] water molecules together. Adhesion is a water molecule to something else.Cohesion = water to water; adhesion = water to the xylem wall (Spider-Man); both hydrogen bonds.
  • Adhesion of the water molecules to the xylem cell walls help offset the force of gravity. Remember Spider-ManAdhesion to xylem walls helps water climb against gravity.
  • So in most of our plants, [root] pressure itself is minor.Root pressure: night, positive, minor; causes guttation.
  • However, remember we have to load um, the water and minerals with that positive pressure.Bulk flow in xylem needs no plant energy, but loading minerals into the root xylem is active.
Big idea
Most water and mineral absorption happens near root tips, where root hairs add surface area and active transport concentrates minerals. The endodermis, with its waxy Casparian strip, is the last checkpoint: minerals must cross a plasma membrane before entering the vascular cylinder. Xylem sap moves by bulk flow, pulled mainly by negative pressure from transpiration (cohesion-tension hypothesis), with root pressure playing only a minor pushing role. Cohesion and adhesion keep the water column intact; cavitation breaks it.

Must know

  • Most water and mineral absorption occurs near ROOT TIPS. Root hairs increase surface area; the epidermis is permeable to water.
  • Absorption starts with ACTIVE TRANSPORT: essential minerals accumulate at HIGHER concentration in the root than in the soil (against the gradient). Water follows by osmosis.
  • ENDODERMIS: innermost layer of the root cortex, surrounding the vascular cylinder (stele); the LAST CHECKPOINT for selective passage of minerals into vascular tissue.
  • CASPARIAN STRIP: waxy band in endodermal walls that BLOCKS the APOPLASTIC route. Water and minerals in the apoplast must cross a selective endodermal plasma membrane, which keeps out toxins and unneeded materials. Material already in the symplast continues through plasmodesmata.
  • Endodermal and pericycle cells discharge water and minerals from their protoplasts into their own walls (symplast to apoplast) by diffusion and active transport; from the apoplast they enter tracheids and vessel elements. No plasmodesmata lead into the dead xylem cells.
  • XYLEM SAP = water and dissolved minerals, moved from roots to leaves by BULK FLOW. TRANSPIRATION = evaporation of water from the plant surface (leaves); transpired water is replaced from the roots.
  • ROOT PRESSURE: at night root cells pump mineral ions into the xylem, water flows in from the cortex, and xylem sap is pushed (POSITIVE pressure). It can cause GUTTATION (droplets on leaf edges in the morning). Its role is MINOR: too weak to lift water to the top of a tall tree.
  • Xylem sap is MAINLY PULLED by NEGATIVE pressure driven by transpiration. COHESION-TENSION HYPOTHESIS: transpiration pulls xylem sap up, and water cohesion transmits the pull along the whole xylem from shoots to roots. Xylem sap is normally under negative pressure.
  • Transpirational pull: water vapor in leaf air spaces (spongy mesophyll) diffuses out through open stomata. As water evaporates, the air-water interface retreats into mesophyll cell walls; surface tension there creates negative pressure that pulls water from the xylem.
  • COHESION = water molecules attracted to each other. ADHESION = water attached to something else (xylem cell walls; "like Spider-Man"), which helps offset gravity. Both come from hydrogen bonds.
  • Thick secondary walls keep vessel elements and tracheids from collapsing under negative pressure (like drinking through a straw). CAVITATION = a water vapor pocket from drought stress or freezing that breaks the water chain; it can kill the plant.
  • Bulk flow review: maintained against gravity by cohesion-tension; driven by transpiration, so it needs NO energy from the plant (solar powered), although loading minerals into the root xylem is active. Bulk flow vs diffusion: driven by PRESSURE potential, not solute potential; in the xylem it runs through hollow DEAD cells, not across membranes of living cells; moves the ENTIRE solution; much FASTER.

Terms

root hairs
Extensions near root tips that increase surface area for water and mineral absorption.
endodermis
Innermost layer of the root cortex surrounding the vascular cylinder; the last checkpoint for minerals entering vascular tissue.
vascular cylinder (stele)
Central core of the root that contains the xylem and phloem.
Casparian strip
Waxy band in endodermal cell walls that blocks apoplastic movement of minerals into the vascular cylinder.
pericycle
Cell layer inside the endodermis that water and minerals pass through before entering the xylem.
tracheids and vessel elements
Dead, hollow xylem cells that carry xylem sap.
xylem sap
Water and dissolved minerals moving through the xylem.
transpiration
Evaporation of water from the plant surface, mainly leaves.
root pressure
Positive pressure (push) on xylem sap created at night when roots pump mineral ions into the xylem and water follows.
guttation
Water droplets on leaf edges in the morning caused by root pressure.
cohesion-tension hypothesis
Transpiration pulls xylem sap up, and water cohesion transmits that pull from leaves to roots.
transpirational pull
The pull on xylem sap created by negative pressure at the air-water interface as water evaporates from leaf cell walls.
cohesion
Attraction of water molecules to each other through hydrogen bonds.
adhesion
Attraction of water molecules to another surface, such as xylem cell walls.
cavitation
Formation of a water vapor pocket that breaks the water column in the xylem, caused by drought stress or freezing.

Examples

  • Guttation droplets on leaf edges in the early morning.
  • Spider-Man: adhesion of water to xylem walls helps it climb.
  • Drinking through a straw: negative pressure; thick secondary walls keep the xylem from collapsing.
  • Animation: "trees move water by losing water"; a film of water on cell walls; hollow tube-like xylem cells (brown) in leaf veins; transpiration creates tension from leaf cells through the xylem to the roots.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "Make sure you know how to describe the movement of xylem, and that you know what structures take part in this movement."
  • "So spend some time with this concept."
  • Root pressure is minor: xylem sap is mainly pulled by negative pressure, not pushed from below. Root pressure can push sap a short way and cause guttation, but it cannot raise water to the top of a tall tree.
  • Cohesion vs adhesion: "cohesion is two water molecules together, adhesion is a water molecule to something else."
  • She skips the water potential numbers on the ascent figure ("we're not going to spend time on this").
  • Though bulk flow needs no energy from the plant, loading water and minerals into the root xylem involves active transport (positive pressure).
  • Minerals accumulate against the gradient: "So because this is going against the concentration gradient it has to be active transport."
Memory hooks
  • Casparian = Checkpoint waxThe waxy Casparian strip in the endodermis blocks the apoplast, forcing apoplastic minerals through a selective plasma membrane, the last checkpoint.
  • Endodermis = the end of the cortexThe endodermis is the innermost cortex layer around the vascular cylinder.
  • Cohesion = co-workers; Adhesion = added to the wallCohesion is water to water; adhesion is water to the xylem wall.
  • Roots Push a little, leaves Pull a lotRoot pressure is a minor positive push; transpiration's negative pressure is the main pull.
  • Guttation = Good morning dropletsRoot pressure at night pushes out droplets seen on leaf edges in the morning.
  • Cavitation = a cave of vaporA water vapor pocket from drought or freezing breaks the water chain.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Root uptake figure (numbered): (1) apoplastic route along cell walls; (2) symplastic route; (3) transmembrane route (switching between symplast and apoplast); (4) endodermis with Casparian strip (deep purple) controls entry to the vascular cylinder, forcing apoplastic water into the endodermal cell; then exit through the plasma membrane and wall (endodermis and pericycle) into the xylem; (5) transport in xylem up the plant. Label: epidermis, root hair, cortex, endodermis, Casparian strip, pericycle, xylem vessels, plasmodesmata.
  • Ascent of xylem sap (tree): root hairs take in water; apoplastic water is stopped by the Casparian strip, crosses an endodermal plasma membrane into the symplast, then leaves the endodermis or pericycle into the xylem apoplast; cohesion (water to water) and adhesion (water to xylem wall) in the xylem column; in the leaf, water leaves the xylem into mesophyll air spaces and exits a stoma as water vapor. Draw arrows root to leaf. Skip the water potential numbers.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Plants move large volumes of water from roots to shoots every day.
  • The large surface area of cortical cell membranes enhances uptake of water and selected minerals.
  • Peak xylem sap velocity is 15 to 45 m per hour in some trees ("pretty darn fast").
  • Water molecules leaving the xylem tug on the adjacent molecules down the column.
  • Bulk flow is driven by a water potential difference at opposite ends of the xylem.
  • Animation: "trees move water by losing water"; water forms a film on cell walls; xylem cells are hollow tubes in leaf veins.
  • (Optional reading, not assigned) Properties of water are in chapter 3 of the textbook.
  • (Lower priority: "We're not going to spend time on this") The water potential numbers on the ascent-of-sap figure.
36.4

The rate of transpiration is regulated by stomata

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Explain stomata structure, gas exchange and timingWhat is a stoma and what are guard cells? What percent of water loss goes through stomata? What passes through them, and when are they open or closed?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • STOMA = the hole (pore)
  • flanked by two half-moon GUARD CELLS that change shape to control its diameter (K+ channels help). About 95% of water loss is through stomata
  • the waxy cuticle limits loss elsewhere. CO2 comes in through the stoma
  • water vapor and O2 exit the same opening. OPEN during the DAY (CO2 for photosynthesis), CLOSED at NIGHT to save water. Guard cells balance water conservation against gas exchange. Water lost per leaf depends on stomatal density and stoma size. Flat leaves: large surface area, high surface-to-volume ratio, more photosynthesis and more water loss.

Common mistake: Calling the guard cells the stoma, or reversing timing. The stoma is the hole; open by day, closed at night.

Predict transpiration rate and its effectsName the four conditions that raise transpiration rate. What happens if lost water is not replaced, and how does transpiration protect the leaf?List from memory

Transpiration rises when SUNNY, WARM, DRY and WINDY. If uptake and transport do not replace lost water, the plant WILTS. Transpiration protects leaves from overheating by EVAPORATIVE COOLING; with stomata closed, less water evaporates, so the leaf cools less and can heat up.

Common mistake: Thinking humid or cool conditions raise transpiration. Sunny, warm, dry and windy raise it.

Lecture video 36.4 (opens online)
Full notes for 36.4
★ She said this is on the test
  • The stoma [remember is] the hole.Stoma = the pore; guard cells = the pair of cells around it that change shape.
  • So carbon dioxide remember is coming in through the [stoma] into the leaf.CO2 enters through the stoma; water vapor and O2 exit the same opening.
Big idea
Flat leaves with large surface area and high surface-to-volume ratios increase both photosynthesis and water loss. About 95% of water loss goes through stomata, so guard cells balance water conservation against gas exchange by opening and closing the stomata. Stomata open during the day and close at night. Sunny, warm, dry and windy conditions raise transpiration; if water is not replaced the plant wilts, and transpiration cools leaves.

Must know

  • Flat leaves have large surface areas and high surface-to-volume ratios, which increase photosynthesis AND water loss.
  • About 95% of the water a plant loses escapes through the STOMATA; the waxy cuticle limits loss elsewhere.
  • STOMA = the hole. Each stoma is flanked by a pair of half-moon GUARD CELLS that control its diameter by changing shape (K+ channels in guard cells help, from 36.2).
  • Guard cells balance water conservation against the need for gas exchange by opening and closing stomata.
  • Water lost per leaf depends on STOMATAL DENSITY (how many) and average STOMA SIZE.
  • Stomata OPEN during the DAY (CO2 enters for photosynthesis; water and O2 exit the same opening) and CLOSE at NIGHT to minimize water loss.
  • Transpiration rate increases in SUNNY, WARM, DRY and WINDY conditions. If uptake and transport do not replace lost water, the plant WILTS.
  • Transpiration protects leaves from overheating by EVAPORATIVE COOLING. With stomata closed, less water evaporates, so the leaf cools less and can heat up.

Terms

stoma
The pore (hole) in the leaf surface through which gases and water vapor pass.
guard cells
Pair of half-moon-shaped cells flanking each stoma that change shape to control its diameter.
stomatal density
How many stomata a leaf has; with stoma size, it sets how much water a leaf loses.
cuticle
Waxy layer on leaves that limits water loss outside the stomata.
evaporative cooling
Lowering of leaf temperature as transpired water evaporates.

Examples

  • Daytime: stomata open so CO2 can enter for photosynthesis; water and O2 leave.
  • Night: stomata close; no CO2 needed, so the plant saves water.
  • Hot, sunny, dry, windy afternoon: transpiration speeds up; plant may wilt if water is not replaced.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "About 95% of the water a plant loses is going to escape through the stomata."
  • Day open / night closed: "So this makes sense, right?"
  • Four conditions that raise transpiration: sunny, warm, dry, windy.
  • "Transpiration also helps protect the leaves from overheating by lowering the internal temperature through evaporative cooling as well."
Memory hooks
  • Sunny, Warm, Dry, Windy = Super Water Drain WeatherThese four conditions raise the transpiration rate.
  • 95 out the stomataAbout 95% of water loss escapes through stomata; the cuticle limits the rest.
  • Day = Doors open, Night = No entryStomata open in the day for CO2 and close at night to save water.
  • Guard cells Guard the gateGuard cells change shape to set stoma diameter, balancing gas exchange and water loss.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Stoma figure: the opening (light blue) between two half-moon guard cells. Label stoma and guard cells; open (guard cells bowed apart) vs closed.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • High transpiration conditions typically happen during the day.
36.5

Sugars are transported from sources to sinks via the phloem

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Identify sugar sources and sinks by seasonDefine sugar source and sugar sink with examples. In summer and in spring, which is the source and which is the sink between leaves and a storage root?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper

TRANSLOCATION = transport of photosynthesis products through the phloem. SOURCE = net PRODUCER of sugar (mature leaves). SINK = net CONSUMER or depository (roots, buds, fruits, storage sites). Summer: leaves are the source, storage root (taproot) is the sink. Spring: storage root is the source, sending sugar to new leaves and growing tips (sinks). Direction reverses, mechanism is the same. Water enters the phloem from the xylem. Sinks usually get sugar from the NEAREST sources.

Common mistake: Assuming a storage root is always a sink. It is a sink in summer and a source in spring.

Explain active loading and passive unloadingWhy is sugar loading at the source active? Describe how proton pumps and cotransport load sucrose. How does sugar leave at the sink, and how does water move?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper

Loading is ACTIVE (ATP) because sugar is MORE concentrated in sieve-tube elements and companion cells than in mesophyll source cells. PROTON PUMP uses ATP to pump H+ out; H+ flows back in through a COTRANSPORTER carrying sucrose, so sucrose accumulates against its gradient. Loading route is species specific: symplast only, or both symplastic and apoplastic. At the SINK, concentration is low, so sugar DIFFUSES out (passive) and is used or stored as starch. Water follows by OSMOSIS, a type of diffusion (passive).

Common mistake: Calling unloading active. In her model sugar diffuses out at the sink passively; only loading at the source needs ATP.

Draw and explain pressure flow in a sieve tubeDraw a xylem vessel beside a sieve tube with source above and sink below. Number and explain the four steps of pressure flow, and name the phloem cells involved.Draw and label on paper
  • Labels: xylem vessel beside sieve tube
  • source cell (leaf) at top
  • sink cell (storage root) at bottom
  • arrows for sucrose and water. 1 Sugar loaded into the sieve tube at the source
  • this lowers water potential, so water enters from the xylem by osmosis. 2 POSITIVE pressure pushes sap toward the sink (bulk flow). 3 Sugar unloaded at the sink. 4 Water leaves and is recycled to the xylem. High pressure at source, low at sink. Cells: living SIEVE-TUBE ELEMENTS carry phloem sap (high in sucrose)
  • COMPANION CELLS sit beside them.

Common mistake: Calling pressure flow negative pressure. Phloem sap is pushed by positive pressure from high (source) to low (sink).

Compare xylem movement with phloem movementCompare xylem and phloem transport: type of pressure, what drives it, energy needed from the plant, direction, and conducting cells.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Xylem: NEGATIVE pressure (pull)
  • driven by transpiration (cohesion-tension)
  • no plant energy for bulk flow (solar powered)
  • roots to shoots only
  • dead tracheids and vessel elements
  • root pressure is the minor positive exception. Phloem: POSITIVE pressure (push, pressure flow)
  • driven by active sugar loading with water following by osmosis
  • ATP needed to load
  • source to sink in either direction
  • living sieve-tube elements with companion cells
  • called translocation.

Common mistake: Mixing up the pressures. Xylem is mainly pulled by negative pressure; phloem is pushed by positive pressure.

Lecture video 36.5-36.6 (opens online)
Full notes for 36.5
★ She said this is on the test
  • So here's the, uh, figure that you need to know. So make sure you spend some time with this and that you can draw it out and explain what is happening.Draw the pressure-flow figure: xylem beside sieve tube, source above, sink below, steps 1 loading, 2 flow, 3 unloading, 4 water recycling.
  • So make sure when you're studying for the exam that you're covering both [xylem] movement and also [phloem] movement as well.Compare xylem (negative pressure, transpiration) with phloem (positive pressure, sugar loading).
  • So make sure you know what types of cells are involved in movement of our sugar.Sieve-tube elements carry phloem sap; companion cells sit beside them.
  • This is our pressure flow. It's a positive pressure, not a negative pressure.Phloem sap flows by positive pressure from high (source) to low (sink).
  • So pay attention to this video. Go back and watch it again if you need toSpring: taproot is the source, new leaves the sink; summer: leaves are the source, taproot the sink; water comes from the xylem into the phloem.
  • Remember low concentration here. So this is a passive transport.Unloading at the sink is passive diffusion (her model), and water follows by osmosis.
  • And remember, osmosis is a type of diffusion. It is a type of passive transport.Water entering and leaving the sieve tube moves by osmosis, which is passive.
  • if you need to go back to, I believe it was chapter seven and review this, then make sure that you do soLoading uses proton pumps (ATP) and H+/sucrose cotransport.
Big idea
Translocation moves phloem sap, a watery solution high in sucrose, through sieve-tube elements from sugar sources to sugar sinks. Sugar is actively loaded into sieve-tube elements by proton pumping and H+/sucrose cotransport; water follows by osmosis and builds positive pressure. Sap moves by bulk flow (pressure flow) from the high-pressure source to the low-pressure sink, where sugar is unloaded and water returns to the xylem. A storage organ can be a source or a sink depending on the season.

Must know

  • TRANSLOCATION = transport of the products of photosynthesis through the phloem. It differs from xylem movement; study BOTH for the exam.
  • PHLOEM SAP (angiosperms): a watery solution very high in SUCROSE that travels through living SIEVE-TUBE ELEMENTS, with COMPANION CELLS beside them.
  • SUGAR SOURCE = organ that is a net PRODUCER of sugar (mature leaves). SUGAR SINK = organ that is a net CONSUMER or depository of sugar (roots, buds, fruits, storage sites).
  • A STORAGE ORGAN can be a source or a sink depending on the season: the storage root is a SINK in summer and a SOURCE in spring, when it sends sugar to growing tips and new leaves. The direction reverses; the mechanism is the same.
  • Sinks usually receive sugar from the NEAREST sources.
  • Sugar must be LOADED into sieve-tube elements at the source. Loading is ACTIVE (needs ATP) because sugar is MORE concentrated in sieve-tube elements and companion cells than in the source (mesophyll) cells.
  • The loading route is species specific: in some species sugar reaches sieve-tube elements through the symplast only; in others through both symplastic and apoplastic pathways.
  • PROTON PUMPING and H+/SUCROSE COTRANSPORT let sucrose accumulate in the phloem against its gradient.
  • At the SINK, sugar DIFFUSES out of the phloem (passive) because sink concentration is lower: sugar is used or converted into STARCH. Water follows by OSMOSIS (a type of diffusion, passive).
  • PRESSURE FLOW: bulk flow by POSITIVE pressure moves phloem sap from the source (high pressure) to the sink (low pressure) in angiosperms.
  • Pressure-flow figure (draw it): (1) sugar loaded into the sieve tube at the source lowers water potential, so water enters from the xylem by osmosis; (2) positive pressure pushes sap toward the sink; (3) sugar is unloaded at the sink; (4) water leaves and is recycled to the xylem.
  • Phloem moves by POSITIVE pressure (push). Xylem sap in a transpiring plant moves mainly by NEGATIVE pressure (pull); root pressure is the minor exception.

Terms

translocation
Transport of the products of photosynthesis through the phloem.
phloem sap
An aqueous solution high in sucrose that moves through sieve-tube elements.
sieve-tube elements
Phloem cells that carry phloem sap; they have cytoplasm but few organelles and are joined by sieve plates.
companion cell
Cell next to a sieve-tube element; sugar is more concentrated in it and the sieve tube than in source cells.
sugar source
An organ that is a net producer of sugar, such as a mature leaf.
sugar sink
An organ that is a net consumer or depository of sugar, such as roots, buds and fruits.
storage organ
An organ that can act as a sugar sink or a sugar source depending on the season.
phloem loading
Active transport of sugar into sieve-tube elements at the source, using proton pumps and H+/sucrose cotransport.
pressure flow
Bulk flow of phloem sap driven by positive pressure from source to sink.
starch
Storage polymer that sink cells make from unloaded sugar.

Examples

  • Mature leaves: sugar source.
  • Roots, buds, fruits: sugar sinks.
  • Taproot (storage root): sink in summer, source in early spring; the image shows sugar moving from a storage root to new leaves.
  • Leaf cell types in the loading figure: mesophyll cell (photosynthesis), bundle-sheath cell, phloem parenchyma cell, companion cell, sieve-tube element.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "Make sure when you're studying for the exam that you're covering both xylem movement and also phloem movement."
  • "Make sure you know what types of cells are involved in movement of our sugar."
  • Pressure flow figure: "here's the figure that you need to know. Make sure you spend some time with this and that you can draw it out and explain what is happening."
  • Watch the source/sink animation again: it shows what the sugar (solute) and the water (from xylem into phloem) do.
  • Loading is active "98, 99% of the time": informal emphasis that loading needs active transport, not a number to memorize.
  • "It's a positive pressure, not a negative pressure."
  • Cotransport was covered in chapter 7; review it.
  • Loading route: "whether sugar [moves] only by [symplastic] or by both [symplastic] and [apoplastic] pathways [is] going to be species specific."
Memory hooks
  • Source Sells, Sink SpendsA source is a net producer of sugar (mature leaves); a sink is a net consumer or store (roots, buds, fruits).
  • Load Leaf, Unload UndergroundSugar is loaded actively at the source leaf and unloaded at the sink, such as a storage root.
  • Phloem Pushes, Xylem X-tracts (pulls)Phloem moves by positive pressure; xylem by negative pressure.
  • Summer Stores, Spring SpendsA storage root is a sink in summer and a source in spring.
  • High at Home (source), Low at the Locker (sink)Pressure is high at the source and low at the sink, so sap flows source to sink.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Pressure flow (draw it): two vertical tubes side by side, xylem vessel and sieve tube (phloem). Top: source cell (leaf). (1) Sucrose actively loaded into sieve tube; water enters from xylem by osmosis → high pressure. (2) Bulk flow by positive pressure down the sieve tube. Bottom: sink cell (storage root). (3) Sucrose unloaded into sink (lower concentration; used or stored as starch). (4) Water returns to the xylem and is recycled upward by transpiration. Label high pressure at source, low pressure at sink.
  • Loading figure: symplastic route (blue) through plasmodesmata from mesophyll to bundle-sheath to phloem parenchyma to companion cell to sieve-tube element; apoplastic route (pink) through cell walls, with active loading across the companion cell or sieve-tube membrane.
  • H+/sucrose cotransport: proton pump uses ATP to move H+ out; H+ flows back in through the cotransporter carrying sucrose into the sieve-tube/companion cell.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Sugars are usually made in mature leaves and must reach lower parts of the plant for energy and growth.
  • Animation: in autumn leaves die and the plant is dormant in winter; in early spring the taproot is the source and new leaves are the sink because rapid leaf growth needs more sugar than they make.
  • Shipping analogy for nearest sources: fresh vegetables are shipped short distances.
  • Leaf cells on the loading route: mesophyll cell (photosynthesis), bundle-sheath cell, phloem parenchyma cell, companion cell, sieve-tube element.
  • Review sieve-tube elements in chapter 35 and proton pumping/cotransport from chapter 7 only as far as these notes explain them.
  • The animation says root cells remove sugar by active transport; the instructor teaches passive diffusion at the sink. Answer with diffusion.
36.6

The symplast is highly dynamic

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Explain the phloem as an information superhighwayWhy is the phloem called an information superhighway? What does it carry systemically, and how does it help defend the plant against a local infection?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper

Phloem = INFORMATION SUPERHIGHWAY. It allows SYSTEMIC transport (throughout the entire body) of MACROMOLECULES and VIRUSES. Systemic communication through the phloem integrates functions of the whole plant. Defense: after a local infection, chemical signals travel through the phloem and switch on DEFENSE GENES in noninfected tissues, protecting the rest of the plant (similar to what our body does).

Common mistake: Thinking the phloem carries only sugar. It also carries macromolecules, viruses and defense signals.

Full notes for 36.6
Big idea
The phloem acts as an information superhighway. It carries macromolecules and viruses throughout the plant (systemic transport), and systemic communication through the phloem integrates the functions of the whole plant. In a defense response, chemical signals travel through the phloem and switch on defense genes in tissues that are not yet infected.

Must know

  • Phloem = an INFORMATION SUPERHIGHWAY.
  • Phloem allows SYSTEMIC transport (throughout the entire body) of MACROMOLECULES and VIRUSES.
  • Systemic communication through the phloem helps integrate the functions of the entire plant.
  • Defense response to a local infection: chemical signals travel through the phloem and activate defense genes in noninfected tissues, protecting the rest of the plant.

Terms

systemic
Throughout the entire body of the plant.
systemic transport
Movement of macromolecules and viruses through the whole plant via the phloem.
systemic communication
Signaling through the phloem that integrates the functions of the whole plant.
information superhighway
The instructor's name for the phloem as a route for signals as well as sugar.

Examples

  • Defense response: local infection → chemical signals through phloem → defense genes turned on in noninfected tissues (like our own body's response).
What she stressed in lecture
  • "We consider phloem within a plant to be an information superhighway."
  • "Very similar to what our body does, the plant does as well."
Memory hooks
  • Phloem = Phone lineThe phloem is an information superhighway that carries signals throughout the plant.
  • Macromolecules and Viruses = MVs on the highwayPhloem allows systemic transport of macromolecules and viruses.
  • Systemic = whole systemSystemic means throughout the entire plant body.
  • One leaf sick, all leaves warnedChemical signals in the phloem turn on defense genes in noninfected tissues.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • "Very similar to what our body does, the plant does as well."
  • Brief aside: research supports communication between plants. She mentions it as something to think about; focus first on systemic signaling within a plant.
Ch 37

Exam sheet: Chapter 37: Soil and Plant Nutrition

Memorize this, review it last
  1. 9 MACRONUTRIENTS (memorize): carbon, oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen, phosphorus, sulfur, potassium, calcium, magnesium (C, O, H, N, P, S, K, Ca, Mg). Hook: C. HOPKiNS CaMg.
  2. 8 MICRONUTRIENTS (memorize): chlorine, iron, boron, manganese, zinc, copper, nickel, molybdenum (Cl, Fe, B, Mn, Zn, Cu, Ni, Mo). Hook: Cats In Boxes Make Zebras Cry Not Mice. Magnesium = macro, manganese = micro.
  3. 17 essential elements = 9 + 8: needed to complete the life cycle and reproduce; found with hydroponic culture. Micronutrients are cofactors. Sodium: only C4/CAM plants (extra).
  4. Mass: 80 to 90% of FRESH mass is water; 96% of DRY mass from CO2 (photosynthesis); 4% of dry mass from soil.
  5. Nitrogen contributes most to growth and crop yield. Most common deficiencies: N, K, P (= fertilizer N-P-K).
  6. Corn deficiencies: N browning along main vein; K brown tip and sides; P purple, older growth. Mobile nutrient = older organs first; less mobile = younger organs first.
  7. Soil horizons: A = topsoil + humus (dark); B = less organic, less weathered; C = partially broken down rock.
  8. Earthworms mix and clump soil (gas diffusion, water retention). Commercial fertilizer = immediate, runs off, algal blooms kill fish; organic (manure, fishmeal, compost) = gradual.
  9. Sustainable agriculture = conservation minded, environmentally safe, profitable. Malnourished = enough calories, lacking essential minerals.
  10. 5 rhizobacteria/endophyte benefits: antibiotics, absorb toxic metals, more nutrient availability, convert N2, root growth chemicals.
  11. Nitrogen bacteria: fixing N2 to NH3 (+H+ = NH4+); ammonifying amino acids (humus) to NH4+; nitrifying NH4+ to NO2- to NO3- (two different bacteria); denitrifying NO3- to N2 (lost).
  12. Plants absorb NO3- (most common) and NH4+; plant ENZYMES turn nitrate into ammonium; N2 unusable (triple bond), only bacteria fix it. N is in proteins and nucleic acids.
  13. Rhizobium in legume nodules (soybeans, beans, peas); crop rotation: maize, then legume plowed in as green manure.
  14. Ecto vs arbuscular: ecto = mantle, apoplast only, 10% of families, woody; arbuscular = through wall not plasma membrane, arbuscules, 85% of species, most crops. 5% have none.
  15. Epiphyte (substrate only, rain: staghorn fern) vs parasite (takes from host: mistletoe, dodder, Indian pipe) vs carnivore (photosynthetic, traps insects for N: pitcher plant, sundew, Venus flytrap).
  16. not on the exam: drawing the nitrogen cycle, the nitrogen fixation formula, the % mass column, C4/CAM pathways.
37.1

Soil contains a living, complex ecosystem

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Describe the three soil horizons and topsoil lifeDraw a soil profile with the A, B and C horizons and label what each contains. Then list the organisms in topsoil and explain what earthworms do.Draw and label on paper
  • Plants get most water and minerals from the UPPER soil layers
  • A horizon = topsoil + humus, darkest
  • topsoil = mineral particles, living organisms, water, air, inorganic material
  • humus = decaying organic material
  • B horizon = less organic material, less weathered than A, looser and lighter
  • C horizon = mainly partially broken down rock, very compact
  • topsoil organisms: bacteria, fungi, algae and other protists, insects, earthworms, nematodes, plant roots
  • their activities change the soil's physical and chemical properties
  • earthworms mix and clump soil particles, improving gas diffusion and water retention.

Common mistake: Humus is not topsoil: humus is decaying organic material; topsoil is mineral particles, organisms, water and air. Both are in A.

Explain fertilization, fertilizer types and runoffWhy do farm soils need fertilization? Compare commercial and organic fertilizers (nutrients, release, problem), explain how runoff harms lakes, and define sustainable agriculture.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Repeated harvesting (same crop, same place, many seasons) causes nutrient depletion and lower crop yields
  • fertilization = adding mineral nutrients to replace those lost
  • commercial fertilizer: varying nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium (N-P-K, "the three biggies"), immediately available but not retained long because of water runoff
  • organic fertilizer (manure, fishmeal, compost): N, P, K released gradually as it decomposes
  • minerals not absorbed are leached into ponds and lakes, cause algal blooms that deplete oxygen and can kill fish
  • N, K, P are also the most common plant deficiencies (37.2)
  • sustainable agriculture = conservation minded, environmentally safe, profitable.

Common mistake: Commercial fertilizer acts fast but is not retained; organic fertilizer is the gradual one, releasing nutrients as it decomposes.

Lecture video 37.1 (opens online)
Full notes for 37.1
★ She said this is on the test
  • commercial fertilizers ... are typically enriched in varying amounts of nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium. Those are the three biggies.N-P-K (nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium) are the three fertilizer nutrients; the same three are the most common deficiencies (37.2).
  • Repeated harvesting of crop plants can lead to nutrient depletion in these agricultural soils.She says it twice: same crop, same place, many seasons depletes nutrients and lowers yield; fertilization replaces the lost minerals.
Big idea
Plants get most of their water and minerals from the upper layers of the soil, and living organisms in those layers shape the soil. Soil is stratified into layers called soil horizons: the A horizon (topsoil and humus), the B horizon and the C horizon. Growing crops in the same place season after season depletes soil nutrients and lowers yields, so fertilization replaces the lost mineral nutrients. Sustainable agriculture uses farming methods that are conservation minded, environmentally safe and profitable.

Must know

  • Plants obtain most of their water and minerals from the UPPER layers of the soil; living organisms live in these upper layers and play an important role there.
  • Soil is stratified into layers called soil horizons. A horizon: topsoil and humus, the darkest layer. Topsoil = mineral particles, living organisms, water, air and inorganic material. Humus = decaying organic material.
  • B horizon: less organic material and less weathered than the A horizon; looser and not as dark.
  • C horizon: mainly partially broken down rock; very compact.
  • Organisms in topsoil: bacteria, fungi, algae and other protists, insects, earthworms, nematodes, and plant roots. Their activities change the physical and chemical properties of the soil.
  • Earthworms mix and clump soil particles, which improves gas diffusion and water retention.
  • Cultivating crops in the same location for many seasons depletes soil nutrients and reduces crop yields. Fertilization = adding mineral nutrients to the soil to replace those lost.
  • Sustainable agriculture: farming methods that are conservation minded, environmentally safe and profitable.
  • Commercial fertilizers are enriched in varying amounts of nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium (N-P-K, "the three biggies"). Their minerals are available immediately, but the soil does not retain them long because of water runoff.
  • Organic fertilizers (manure, fishmeal, compost) also release N, P and K, but gradually, as they decompose.
  • Minerals not absorbed by roots are leached from the soil and carried to aquatic ecosystems in runoff, causing algal blooms that deplete oxygen in ponds and lakes and can kill fish.

Terms

soil horizons
The layers that soil is stratified into (A, B and C horizons).
A horizon
Top soil layer containing topsoil and humus; the darkest layer.
topsoil
Part of the A horizon holding mineral particles, living organisms, water, air and inorganic material.
humus
Decaying organic material in the A horizon.
B horizon
Layer below the A horizon with less organic material and less weathering.
C horizon
Deepest layer, mainly partially broken down rock; very compact.
fertilization
The addition of mineral nutrients to the soil to replace nutrients lost to crop growth.
sustainable agriculture
Farming methods that are conservation minded, environmentally safe and profitable.
commercial fertilizer
Store-bought fertilizer enriched in varying amounts of nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium; minerals are immediately available but quickly lost to runoff.
organic fertilizer
Manure, fishmeal or compost; releases N, P and K gradually as it decomposes.
algal bloom
Overgrowth of algae in ponds and lakes fed by fertilizer runoff; depletes oxygen and can kill fish.

Examples

  • Earthworms: mix and clump soil particles, improving gas diffusion and water retention for plants.
  • Home Depot or Lowe's fertilizer: commercial fertilizer labeled with N-P-K amounts.
  • Algal blooms in ponds and lakes: result of fertilizer runoff; deplete oxygen and kill fish.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Know the three horizons and what each contains: A (topsoil + humus, dark), B (less organic, less weathered), C (partially broken down rock, compact).
  • Nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium: "Those are the three biggies" in commercial fertilizers.
  • Commercial fertilizer minerals are immediately available but leave with runoff; organic fertilizers release them gradually as they decompose.
  • Runoff leads to algal blooms that deplete oxygen and can kill fish.
Memory hooks

  • Dark A layer, Decaying humusHumus is the decaying organic material that makes the A horizon dark.
  • N-P-K = the three biggiesCommercial fertilizers are enriched in nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium.
  • Keeps the cell firmPotassium is an enzyme cofactor and maintains turgor.
  • Quick bag, slow pileCommercial fertilizer minerals are immediately available; organic fertilizers (manure, fishmeal, compost) release them gradually as they decompose.
  • Runoff ruins pondsUnabsorbed minerals leach into lakes and ponds, causing algal blooms that deplete oxygen and kill fish.

Phosphorus labels: DNA and RNA, ATP produced by mitochondria, phospholipids in cell membranes. Potassium: cofactor for enzymes throughout the cell, major role in maintaining turgor.

Plant roots absorb many types of essential elements from the soil

  • found with hydroponic culture

Diagnose the common N, K and P deficienciesWhich nutrient contributes most to growth? Name the three most common deficiencies, describe each one's corn leaf symptom, and explain how nutrient mobility decides whether old or young leaves show it.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper

Big idea
Water, air and soil minerals all contribute to plant growth: water is 80 to 90% of fresh mass, about 96% of dry mass is organic material made in photosynthesis (her shorthand: "from CO2"), and only 4% of dry mass is inorganic substances from the soil. Plants have 17 essential elements, the elements needed to complete the life cycle and reproduce: 9 macronutrients and 8 micronutrients, which she said to memorize. Mineral deficiency symptoms depend on the mineral's function and mobility; the most common deficiencies are nitrogen, potassium and phosphorus.

Must know

  • Water = 80 to 90% of a plant's fresh mass.
  • About 96% of a plant's dry mass is organic material built in photosynthesis. Her exam answer: it comes from CO2 (the AIR), assimilated into carbohydrates. Precisely, the carbon (and most oxygen) comes from CO2 and the hydrogen comes from water.
  • 4% of a plant's dry mass is inorganic substances from the SOIL (what came through the roots).
  • Essential element = a chemical element required for a plant to complete its life cycle and reproduce.
  • 17 essential elements in plants. Researchers use hydroponic culture to determine which elements are essential.
  • MEMORIZE the 9 MACRONUTRIENTS (needed in relatively large amounts): carbon, oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen, phosphorus, sulfur, potassium, calcium, magnesium (C, O, H, N, P, S, K, Ca, Mg).
  • MEMORIZE the 8 MICRONUTRIENTS (needed in very small amounts): chlorine, iron, boron, manganese, zinc, copper, nickel, molybdenum (Cl, Fe, B, Mn, Zn, Cu, Ni, Mo).
  • Nitrogen contributes the most to plant growth and crop yields.
  • Micronutrients function as cofactors: nonprotein helpers in enzymatic reactions.
  • Malnourished = getting enough calories but lacking other dietary factors such as essential minerals.
  • Deficiency symptoms depend on the nutrient's function and its mobility. Mobile nutrient deficiency affects OLDER organs more; less mobile nutrient deficiency affects YOUNGER organs more.
  • Most common deficiencies: nitrogen, potassium, phosphorus (the fertilizer N-P-K). Corn: N = browning along the main vein; K = leaf tip and sides; P = purplish edges, more on older growth.

Terms

essential element
A chemical element a plant requires to complete its life cycle and reproduce.
macronutrient
Essential element required in relatively large amounts (9: C, O, H, N, P, S, K, Ca, Mg).
micronutrient
Essential element required only in very small amounts (8: Cl, Fe, B, Mn, Zn, Cu, Ni, Mo).
hydroponic culture
Growing plants in a nutrient solution so researchers can determine which elements are essential.
cofactor
A nonprotein helper in enzymatic reactions; the role of micronutrients.
malnourished
Getting enough calories but lacking other dietary factors such as essential minerals.
mobile nutrient
A nutrient that moves within the plant; its deficiency shows first in older organs.
fresh mass / dry mass
Plant mass including water (80 to 90% water) versus mass after drying (about 96% organic material from photosynthesis, her "from CO2"; 4% soil minerals).

Examples

  • A weed pulled from a lawn: 80 to 90% of it is water; dried in an oven, 96% of the dry mass came from CO2.
  • Corn leaves: healthy leaf compared with nitrogen-, potassium- and phosphorus-deficient leaves.
  • C4 and CAM plants: also need sodium.
  • Bees: pollinator declines possibly linked to declining food quality.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "I want you to know these. You just flat out have to memorize them." (the 9 macronutrients)
  • "And yes, I want you to know these." (the 8 micronutrients)
  • "Don't memorize this percentage mass" table; it is for interest only.
  • "Nitrogen is going to be needed for growth"; it contributes most to plant growth and crop yields.
  • Most common deficiencies (N, K, P) tie into the fertilizers sold at Home Depot or Lowe's.

  • Mobile hits Mature leavesDeficiency of a mobile nutrient affects older organs more; less mobile nutrient deficiency affects younger organs.

Extra detail, lower priority
  • More than 50 chemical elements have been identified in plants, but not all are essential.
  • Carbon, oxygen and hydrogen are the three largest by dry mass.
  • Plants with C4 and CAM photosynthetic pathways also require sodium.
  • Global food production is predicted to increase with rising CO2 and temperatures, but only in certain parts of the world.
  • Nutritional quality of wild and crop plants has declined, possibly because nutrient uptake has not kept pace with increased carbon fixation.
  • Pollinator declines (bees) may be linked to declining quality of their food source.
  • Animals, including humans, get their essential mineral nutrients from plants.
37.3

Plant nutrition often involves relationships with other organisms

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

List five bacterial benefits and explain RhizobiumDefine rhizosphere and endophyte. List the five ways rhizobacteria and endophytes help plants. Then explain what Rhizobium does in legume root nodules and why farmers rotate maize with legumes.List from memory
  • Rhizosphere = soil layer closely surrounding roots, rich in rhizobacteria
  • some free-living, others endophytes = nonpathogenic bacteria living BETWEEN cells of plant tissue
  • fed by plant secretions, they (1) produce antibiotics protecting roots from disease, (2) absorb toxic metals, (3) increase nutrient availability, (4) convert nitrogen gas into usable forms, (5) make chemicals that stimulate plant (root) growth
  • Rhizobium lives in legume root nodules (soybeans, green beans, peas), a species-specific mutualism ("a good infection") giving the plant fixed nitrogen
  • crop rotation: maize one year, legume the next, often plowed in as green manure to restore nitrogen.

Common mistake: Endophytes are nonpathogenic and live between cells, not inside them.

Match each nitrogen bacterium to its substrate and productWhy do plants need nitrogen and why can't they use N2? For nitrogen-fixing, ammonifying, nitrifying and denitrifying bacteria, write substrate and product. Which forms do roots absorb, and what converts nitrate inside the plant?List from memory
  • N needed for proteins (amino acids) and nucleic acids (DNA, RNA)
  • deficiency can be most limiting
  • N2 unusable (triple bond)
  • plants and humans cannot fix it, only bacteria
  • nitrogen-fixing: N2 to ammonia NH3 (picks up H+ to become NH4+)
  • ammonifying: amino acids from humus to ammonium NH4+
  • nitrifying, two steps by different bacteria: ammonium to nitrite NO2-, nitrite to nitrate NO3-
  • denitrifying: nitrate to N2 gas, lost
  • roots absorb nitrate NO3- (most common) and NH4+
  • most soil nitrogen comes from bacteria
  • plant ENZYMES (not bacteria) convert nitrate to ammonium for organic compounds
  • not on the exam: drawing the cycle, fixation formula.

Common mistake: Nitrifying bacteria MAKE nitrate; denitrifying bacteria REMOVE nitrate as N2 gas. Nitrate to ammonium in the plant is plant enzymes.

Compare ectomycorrhizae and arbuscular mycorrhizaeWhat does each partner gain in a mycorrhiza? Compare ectomycorrhizae and arbuscular mycorrhizae: outside the root, where hyphae go, wall and membrane, special structure, and how common.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Mycorrhizae = mutualistic fungus-root associations
  • plant gives sugar (carbohydrates)
  • fungus increases absorptive surface for water, supplies minerals, secretes growth factors and antibiotics
  • ectomycorrhizae: dense mantle (sheath) of mycelium over the root, hyphae in the apoplast between cortical cells, do not penetrate root cells, about 10% of plant families, mostly woody
  • arbuscular: hyphae into the root cortex, penetrate the cell wall but not the plasma membrane, form arbuscules in membrane invaginations (nutrient exchange), about 85% of plant species, most crops, by far the most common
  • about 5% of species form none.

Common mistake: Arbuscular hyphae cross the cell wall but not the plasma membrane; ectomycorrhizae never enter root cells at all.

Tell epiphytes, parasitic plants and carnivorous plants apartFor epiphytes, parasitic plants and carnivorous plants, state what each obtains and from where, whether it photosynthesizes, and give examples.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Epiphyte: grows on another plant only as a substrate, does not tap the host
  • water and minerals from rain
  • photosynthetic
  • staghorn fern, bromeliads, many orchids
  • parasitic plant: absorbs water, minerals and sugar from a living host
  • mistletoe (green, photosynthetic), dodder (orange, nonphotosynthetic), Indian pipe (white, nonphotosynthetic, parasite of mycorrhizae)
  • carnivorous plant: photosynthetic, makes its own sugar, traps and digests mostly insects for NITROGEN
  • pitcher plant, sundew, Venus flytrap.

Common mistake: Carnivorous plants trap insects for nitrogen, not sugar; epiphytes are not parasites because they take nothing from the host.

  • So bacteria are very, very, very importantMost soil nitrogen that plants absorb (NO3-, NH4+) comes from bacterial activity.
  • the mycorrhizae penetrate the cell wall, but they do not penetrate the plasma membrane ... these extensions do not penetrate the plasma membraneArbuscular mycorrhizae: through the wall, not the plasma membrane; arbuscules sit in membrane invaginations.
