Big idea
Plants have mutualistic relationships with soil bacteria and fungi. Rhizobacteria and endophytes take nutrients from the plant and in return protect it, absorb toxic metals, increase nutrient availability, convert nitrogen gas to usable forms and stimulate root growth. Soil bacteria drive the nitrogen cycle, producing the nitrate and ammonium plants absorb, and Rhizobium in legume root nodules fixes nitrogen, which crop rotation exploits. Mycorrhizae (ectomycorrhizae and arbuscular mycorrhizae) trade minerals and water for sugar, while epiphytes, parasitic plants and carnivorous plants use other organisms in nonmutualistic ways.

Must know

  • Plants have mutualistic relationships with soil bacteria and fungi. Most plant species form mycorrhizae: the fungus absorbs carbohydrates from the plant and expands the root's absorptive surface for water and minerals.
  • Rhizosphere = the layer of soil closely surrounding the roots, named for the abundant rhizobacteria. Some rhizobacteria are free-living; others are endophytes: nonpathogenic bacteria that live between the cells of host plant tissues.
  • FIVE benefits ("make sure you get these down"): rhizobacteria and endophytes, fed by plant secretions, (1) produce antibiotics that protect roots from disease, (2) absorb toxic metals, (3) increase nutrient availability, (4) convert nitrogen gas into usable forms (plants and humans cannot; bacteria can), (5) make chemicals that stimulate plant (root) growth.
  • Nitrogen is needed in large amounts for proteins (amino acids) and nucleic acids (DNA, RNA); nitrogen deficiency can be the most limiting to plant growth.
  • Plants absorb nitrogen as nitrate (NO3-, the most common form) or ammonium (NH4+). Most soil nitrogen comes from soil bacteria. Inside the plant, plant ENZYMES (not bacteria) convert nitrate to ammonium for organic compounds.
  • Atmospheric N2 is abundant but unavailable to plants because of the triple bond between the atoms. Nitrogen fixation = conversion of N2 to ammonia (NH3). Do not memorize the formula.
  • Nitrogen cycle bacteria and products: nitrogen-fixing (N2 to NH3, which picks up H+ from the soil to become NH4+); ammonifying (amino acids from humus to NH4+); nitrifying, two steps by different bacteria (ammonia/ammonium to nitrite NO2-, then nitrite to nitrate NO3-); denitrifying (nitrate to N2 gas, lost to the atmosphere).
  • Some nitrogen-fixing bacteria are free-living in soil; others are rhizospheric (Anabaena has cells that fix nitrogen and share the products). Rhizobium lives in legume root nodules (soybeans, green beans, peas) in a species-specific symbiosis: "a good infection, not a bad infection."
  • Crop rotation: a nonlegume such as maize (corn) one year, a legume the next in the same place; the legume is often plowed in as green manure to restore fixed nitrogen.
  • Mycorrhizae = mutualistic fungus-root associations, two major types. Plant gives sugar; fungus increases surface area for water uptake, supplies minerals, secretes growth factors (root growth and branching) and antibiotics against soil pathogens.
  • Ectomycorrhizae: dense mantle (sheath) over the root, hyphae in the apoplast, do not penetrate root cells; about 10% of plant families, mostly woody. Arbuscular: hyphae into the cortex, through the cell wall but not the plasma membrane, arbuscules in membrane invaginations (nutrient exchange); about 85% of species, most crops, by far the most common. About 5% of species form none.
  • Nonmutualistic: epiphytes use the host only as a substrate and get water and minerals from rain (staghorn fern, bromeliads, orchids); parasitic plants take water, minerals and sugar from a living host (mistletoe green, dodder orange, Indian pipe white on mycorrhizae); carnivorous plants photosynthesize but trap insects for nitrogen (pitcher plant, sundew, Venus flytrap).

Terms

mutualism
A relationship where both the plant and the other organism benefit.
rhizosphere
The layer of soil that closely surrounds the plant's roots.
rhizobacteria
Bacteria abundant in the rhizosphere; some free-living, some endophytes.
endophyte
A nonpathogenic bacterium living between the cells of host plant tissues.
nitrogen fixation
Conversion of gaseous nitrogen (N2) to ammonia (NH3) by bacteria.
ammonifying bacteria
Bacteria that turn amino acids from decomposing organic material into ammonium.
nitrification
Two-step formation of nitrate: ammonia to nitrite, then nitrite to nitrate, by different nitrifying bacteria.
denitrifying bacteria
Bacteria that convert nitrate back to gaseous nitrogen, which is lost to the atmosphere.
nodules
Swellings on legume roots that house Rhizobium bacteria that fix nitrogen.
crop rotation
Alternating a nonlegume (maize) with a legume in the same place to restore fixed nitrogen.
green manure
A legume crop plowed into the soil to decompose and cycle nitrogen back into it.
mycorrhizae
Mutualistic associations of fungi and plant roots.
mantle
Dense sheath of fungal mycelium over the root surface in ectomycorrhizae.
ectomycorrhizae
Mycorrhizae forming a mantle with hyphae in the apoplast that do not penetrate root cells.
arbuscular mycorrhizae
Mycorrhizae whose hyphae enter the root cortex, penetrating cell walls but not plasma membranes, and form arbuscules.
arbuscules
Branched hyphae inside invaginations of the root cell membrane; site of nutrient transfer.
apoplast
The space between root cells that ectomycorrhizal hyphae enter.
epiphyte
A plant that grows on another plant and gets water and minerals from rain.
parasitic plant
A plant that absorbs water, minerals and sugars from a living host plant.
carnivorous plant
A photosynthetic plant that gets nitrogen by trapping and digesting insects and other small animals.

Examples

  • Sorghum (a corn-like molasses plant): root with mycorrhizal fungus growing on it.
  • Anabaena: has cells that fix nitrogen and share products with attached cells.
  • Rhizobium in legume nodules: soybeans, green beans, peas.
  • Maize (corn) and legumes: crop rotation; legume plowed in as green manure.
  • Garlic mustard: invasive exotic plant that prevents mycorrhizal fungus growth, keeping nutrients from other plants.
  • Staghorn fern on a tree, bromeliads, many orchids: epiphytes.
  • Mistletoe: green parasite, often on sycamore trees in Southern California, easy to see in winter.
  • Dodder: orange, vine-like, nonphotosynthetic parasite that wraps around the host; not found in this area.
  • Indian pipe: white, nonphotosynthetic parasite of mycorrhizae of another plant.
  • Pitcher plant: ant slides in after sugar and is digested for nitrogen.
  • Sundew: sticky droplets attract and trap insects.
  • Venus flytrap: insect bends a hair trigger, leaves close, digestive enzymes remove nitrogen.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Rhizobacteria/endophyte benefits: "make sure you get these down": antibiotics, toxic metals, nutrient availability, nitrogen gas conversion, growth chemicals.
  • "I'm not going to ask you to reproduce the nitrogen cycle, but I may ask you questions about the different types of bacteria that are present and the different types of ... nitrogen products that can be produced."
  • Nitrogen fixation formula: "I don't want you guys to know this."
  • Nitrogen gas conversion: "Plants can't do this. Humans can't either. But bacteria can."
  • Arbuscular mycorrhizae (85% of species, most crops) are "by far ... the most common."
  • Ecto = outside mantle, hyphae in apoplast only; arbuscular = into cortex, through wall but not plasma membrane.
Memory hooks
  • Fix, Ammonify, Nitrify, DenitrifyThe four nitrogen cycle bacteria: N2 to NH3, organic matter to NH4+, NH4+ to NO2- to NO3-, NO3- back to N2.
  • Denitrify = Depart to the airDenitrifying bacteria turn nitrate into N2 gas that is lost to the atmosphere.
  • Ecto = exterior mantleEctomycorrhizae form a mantle on the outside and stay in the apoplast without entering root cells.
  • Arbuscular = are inside, 85%Arbuscular hyphae go through the cell wall (not the plasma membrane), form arbuscules, and occur in 85% of species.
  • Rhizobium = root noduleRhizobium lives in legume root nodules and fixes nitrogen.
  • Epiphyte eats rain, Parasite pilfers, Carnivore catches NEpiphytes get water and minerals from rain, parasites take from a host, carnivores get nitrogen from animals.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Nitrogen cycle figure: atmospheric N2 enters soil; nitrogen-fixing bacteria make NH3, which gains H+ to form NH4+; humus proteins are decomposed to amino acids and ammonifying bacteria make NH4+; nitrifying bacteria make NO2- then NO3-; root absorbs NH4+ or NO3-; denitrifying bacteria return NO3- to N2. Know the bacteria and products, not the full drawing.
  • Legume root with nodules containing Rhizobium.
  • Ectomycorrhiza drawing: purple mantle around the root, hyphae between cortex cells (apoplast), not entering cells.
  • Arbuscular mycorrhiza drawing: hyphae enter the cortex, push into cells by invaginating the plasma membrane, form branched arbuscules.
  • Photos: staghorn fern (epiphyte), mistletoe, dodder, Indian pipe (parasites), pitcher plant, sundew, Venus flytrap (carnivores).
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Dead plants provide energy for soil microorganisms; secretions from living roots support many microbes in the near-root environment.
  • Some soil bacteria are mutualistic; others increase nutrient availability by enhancing decomposition of organisms around the root.
  • Species composition of these bacterial communities varies markedly with ecosystem and plant species.
  • Plant enzymes convert nitrate to ammonium, which is incorporated into organic compounds; these are exported in the xylem.
  • Ammonium reaches plants two ways: nitrogen-fixing bacteria (N2 to NH3, plus H+) and ammonifying bacteria decomposing dead organic material.
  • Farmers and foresters inoculate seeds with fungal spores to promote mycorrhizae.
  • Invasive exotic plants can disrupt native plant-mycorrhizal interactions: garlic mustard slows other plants by preventing mycorrhizal fungus growth.
  • Sorghum (a corn-like molasses plant) is the lecture's example of a root with mycorrhizal fungus on it.
Ch 38

Exam sheet: Chapter 38: Angiosperm Reproduction and Biotechnology

Memorize this, review it last
  1. DRAW Figure 38.6 life cycle: flower (2n), microsporocyte, MEIOSIS, 4 microspores (n), pollen grain (generative + tube cell), megasporocyte, MEIOSIS, 1 of 4 megaspores survives, embryo sac, pollen tube, 2 sperm, FERTILIZATION, zygote (2n), endosperm (3n), seed coat (2n), germinating seed.
  2. Double fertilization: sperm + egg = 2n zygote; sperm + 2 polar nuclei = 3n endosperm (food, forms only where an egg is fertilized).
  3. Embryo sac = 7 cells, 8 nuclei: 3 antipodals, central cell with 2 polar nuclei, egg, 2 synergids (egg at the micropyle; synergids guide the pollen tube to the egg).
  4. Pollen grain = male gametophyte (generative cell makes 2 sperm; tube cell makes the pollen tube). Embryo sac = female gametophyte. Neither is a gamete.
  5. 4 microspores are genetically different; of 4 megaspores, only 1 survives.
  6. Ovule becomes seed, ovary becomes fruit, integuments become seed coat, zygote becomes embryo.
  7. DRAW idealized flower: receptacle, sepal, petal, stamen (anther, filament), carpel or pistil (stigma, style, ovary, ovule). Stamens and carpels are sporophylls.
  8. Complete vs incomplete; sterile (no stamens or carpels) vs unisexual (one of them). Simple vs compound pistil.
  9. Pollination = anther to stigma. Wind 20%, insects 65% (bees most important). Moth white/yellow sweet; fly rotten meat; bat light, aromatic, night; bird big red/yellow, no scent, lots of nectar.
  10. Three Fs: flowers, double fertilization, fruits. Sporophyte dominant (backwards from primitive plants). Plants have a multicellular haploid stage; animals do not.
  11. Bean seed: radicle, hypocotyl (below cotyledons), epicotyl (above), plumule = epicotyl + young leaves + SAM. Monocot 1 cotyledon, eudicot 2. Seed 5 to 15% water.
  12. Germination: oxygen, warmth, water for all; light OR dark depending on species. Imbibition, radicle first, hypocotyl hook straightened by light (bean) vs sheath (maize).
  13. Dormancy cues: rainfall (desert), heat or smoke (fire), cold period, animal gut.
  14. Fruit types: simple (pea), aggregate (raspberry, one flower many carpels), multiple (pineapple, many flowers), accessory (apple, receptacle).
  15. Dispersal: water (coconut), wind (dandelion, samara, tumbleweed), animals (burrs, caches, feces, ants).
  16. Asexual reproduction makes clones through fragmentation, adventitious shoots (suckers), or vegetative reproduction. For selfing, recognize her lecture wording: no genetic diversity and all-identical offspring; see the trap for the biological nuance.
  17. Anti-selfing: separate staminate and carpellate plants, different maturation times, pin vs thrum (pin = pistil tall), self-incompatibility (most common; plants reject SELF, animals reject NONSELF).
  18. GM: Bt (insects not vertebrates), golden rice (vitamin A), cassava (iron, beta-carotene), papaya (ringspot), biofuels (carbon neutral). Biggest risk = transgene escape (superweeds); prevent by male sterility, chloroplast DNA, closed flowers. GMO is not organic.
38.1

Flowers, double fertilization, and fruits are key features of the angiosperm life cycle

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Draw and label the idealized flowerDraw an idealized flower and label every part. Which organs are sporophylls? Define complete, incomplete, sterile and unisexual flowers, and simple versus compound pistil.Draw and label on paper
  • Receptacle (where parts attach)
  • sepals (leaflike, protect the bud)
  • petals (bright colors attract pollinators)
  • stamen = filament + anther (pollen sacs = microsporangia)
  • carpel or pistil = stigma, style, ovary with ovules. Stamens and carpels are sporophylls
  • sepals and petals are sterile modified leaves. Complete = all four organs
  • incomplete = lacks one or more
  • sterile = no stamens and no carpels
  • unisexual = only stamens or only carpels. Simple pistil = one carpel
  • compound pistil = several fused carpels (one ovary chamber per carpel). She accepts either carpel or pistil for the whole female structure. Flower cluster = inflorescence.

Common mistake: Sterile is not unisexual: sterile lacks both stamens and carpels; unisexual has only one of them.

Build the male and female gametophytesStarting from the 2n flower, draw both halves of Figure 38.6 up to the mature gametophytes. Label meiosis, mitosis and ploidy, and state how many microspores and megaspores survive.Draw and label on paper
  • Flower (sporophyte) is 2n and dominant
  • gametophytes are microscopic and depend on it (backwards from primitive plants). Male: microsporocyte (2n) in pollen sac
  • MEIOSIS
  • 4 microspores (n), genetically different
  • mitosis
  • pollen grain = generative cell + tube cell = male gametophyte. Female: megasporocyte (2n) in the ovule
  • MEIOSIS
  • 4 megaspores, 3 die, only 1 survives
  • 3 mitoses without cytokinesis = 8 nuclei
  • embryo sac = 7 cells: egg at the micropyle, 2 synergids (guide the pollen tube), 3 antipodals, central cell with 2 polar nuclei = female gametophyte. Neither gametophyte is a gamete. Plants have a multicellular haploid stage
  • animals do not. Three Fs: flowers, double fertilization, fruits.

Common mistake: Pollen grain and embryo sac are gametophytes, not gametes; the embryo sac has 8 nuclei but only 7 cells.

Pollination and double fertilization, with ploidyDefine pollination and match flower traits to wind, bees, moths, flies, bats and birds. Then draw the pollen tube reaching the embryo sac and both fertilizations, with each product's ploidy.Write 3 to 5 lines, then draw
  • Pollination = pollen from anther to stigma
  • it is not fertilization. Wind 20% (small, no scent or nectar, lots of pollen)
  • insects 65%, bees most important (yellow or blue, sweet)
  • moth white or yellow, sweet
  • fly rotten meat
  • bat light, aromatic, night
  • bird big red or yellow, no scent, lots of nectar (coevolution). Her wording: selfing gives no genetic diversity, so you want the sperm to go to a different plant. Pollen germinates on the stigma
  • tube cell makes the pollen tube
  • generative cell divides into 2 sperm
  • tube grows down the style and enters the ovule through the micropyle. Sperm + egg = zygote (2n)
  • sperm + 2 polar nuclei = endosperm (3n, food, forms only where an egg is fertilized).

Common mistake: Pollination is not fertilization. The zygote is 2n; the endosperm is 3n (one sperm plus two polar nuclei).

Turn the ovule into a seed and the ovary into fruitState what the ovule, ovary, integuments and zygote become, with seed ploidy. Label a bean seed. Name the four fruit types with an example each, and three dispersal agents.Draw the seed, then list from memory
  • Ovule becomes seed
  • ovary becomes fruit
  • integuments become seed coat
  • zygote becomes embryo. Seed = embryo (2n) + endosperm (3n) + seed coat (2n). Bean (eudicot, 2 cotyledons
  • monocot 1): hypocotyl below the cotyledons ends in the radicle (embryonic root)
  • epicotyl above
  • plumule = epicotyl + young leaves + SAM. Bean endosperm food moves into the cotyledons. Mature seed is dormant, 5 to 15% water. Fruit = mature ovary
  • protects seeds, aids dispersal, forms only after fertilization
  • dry or fleshy. Simple (pea)
  • aggregate (raspberry, one flower, many carpels)
  • multiple (pineapple, many flowers)
  • accessory (apple, receptacle). Dispersal: water (coconut)
  • wind (dandelion, samara, tumbleweed)
  • animals (burrs, caches, feces, ants).

Common mistake: Do not reverse them: ovule becomes seed, ovary becomes fruit. The seed coat comes from the integuments, not the ovary wall.

Break dormancy and germinate a seedWhat do all seeds need to germinate, and what varies by species? Name four dormancy-breaking cues. Describe germination in order from the dry seed, comparing bean and maize shoots.Write 3 to 5 lines on paper

All seeds need oxygen, warmth and water; light OR dark depends on species. Dormancy cues: rainfall (desert), heat or smoke (fire), a cold period, passage through an animal gut. The dry seed is only 5 to 15% water, so imbibition (water uptake) starts germination. Radicle (embryonic root) emerges first: it anchors the plant and takes up water. Bean: a hook in the hypocotyl pushes up through the soil, and light straightens it, pulling up the cotyledons and shoot tip. Maize: a sheath around the shoot pushes up through the soil.

Common mistake: The radicle, not the shoot, emerges first. Light need varies by species; oxygen, warmth and water are required by all.

Big idea
Plant life cycles alternate between a multicellular diploid sporophyte and a multicellular haploid gametophyte. In angiosperms the sporophyte is dominant and the gametophytes are microscopic and depend on the sporophyte for nutrients. The angiosperm life cycle has three Fs: flowers, double fertilization and fruits. Pollen (male gametophyte) is carried to the stigma, a pollen tube delivers two sperm to the embryo sac (female gametophyte), one sperm makes the 2n zygote and the other makes the 3n endosperm; the ovule becomes the seed and the ovary becomes the fruit.

Must know

  • Alternation of generations: sporophyte (2n) makes haploid spores by meiosis; spores divide by mitosis into gametophytes (n); gametophytes make gametes (egg, sperm) by mitosis. Plants have a multicellular haploid stage; in animals the only haploid stage is unicellular (the gametes).
  • Angiosperms: the sporophyte is dominant; the gametophytes are reduced, microscopic and depend on the sporophyte for nutrients. This is backwards from the more primitive plants, where the gametophyte is prominent. The life cycle has three Fs: flowers, double fertilization, fruits.
  • Flower = reproductive shoot of the sporophyte, attached at the receptacle. Four floral organs: sepals (leaflike, protect the bud), petals (bright colors attract specific pollinators), stamens (filament + anther; the anther's microsporangia are the pollen sacs), carpels (stigma, style, ovary with ovules). Stamens and carpels are sporophylls; sepals and petals are sterile modified leaves.
  • Pistil = one carpel (simple pistil) or several fused carpels (compound pistil; one ovary chamber per carpel). She accepts carpel or pistil. Complete flower has all four organs; incomplete lacks one or more; sterile lacks both stamens and carpels; unisexual has only one of them. Flower clusters = inflorescences.
  • Pollination = transfer of pollen from anther to stigma, by wind or water (abiotic) or animals (most common). About 20% of angiosperms are wind pollinated (small flowers, no scent or nectar, lots of pollen); about 65% need insects, and bees are the most important. Flower traits match the pollinator (see table); this is coevolution.
  • Male gametophyte: microsporocyte (2n) in a microsporangium (pollen sac) does meiosis to make 4 haploid microspores, genetically different; each microspore divides by mitosis into a generative cell and a tube cell; with the spore wall this is the pollen grain (male gametophyte).
  • Female gametophyte: in the ovule, the megasporocyte (2n) in the megasporangium does meiosis to make 4 megaspores; 3 die, 1 survives; it divides by mitosis 3 times without cytokinesis (8 haploid nuclei), then is partitioned into 7 cells = embryo sac: egg at the micropyle end flanked by 2 synergids (they help guide the pollen tube to the egg), 3 antipodal cells at the far end, central cell with 2 polar nuclei. Micropyle = gap between the two integuments.
  • Pollen lands on a stigma of the right species, absorbs water and germinates; the tube cell (tube nucleus) makes the pollen tube; the generative cell divides into 2 sperm; the tube grows down the style to the ovary, enters the ovule through the micropyle and discharges the 2 sperm.
  • Double fertilization: sperm + egg = zygote (2n); sperm + 2 polar nuclei = triploid cell = endosperm (3n), food storage. It makes sure endosperm develops only where an egg is fertilized, so food is ready for the embryo; endosperm development usually precedes embryo development.
  • After fertilization: ovule becomes the seed, ovary becomes the fruit, zygote becomes the embryo (new sporophyte), integuments become the seed coat. Seed = embryo (2n) + endosperm (3n) + seed coat (2n, maternal). In monocots and many eudicots the endosperm stores the food; in other eudicots (garden bean) its food is exported to the cotyledons. Monocots have one cotyledon, eudicots two.
  • Mature seed: dormant, 5 to 15% water. Bean embryo: hypocotyl (below cotyledons) ends in the radicle; epicotyl above; plumule = epicotyl + young leaves + shoot apical meristem. Germination: all seeds need oxygen, warmth and water (light or dark depends on species); imbibition first, radicle emerges first.
  • Fruit = mature ovary; protects seeds and aids dispersal; develops only after fertilization. Types: simple (pea), aggregate (raspberry), multiple (pineapple), accessory (apple, receptacle); dry vs fleshy. Flowering: environmental cues plus internal signals switch the apical meristem from vegetative to reproductive.

Terms

alternation of generations
Plant life cycle that alternates between a multicellular diploid sporophyte and a multicellular haploid gametophyte.
sporophyte
The diploid (2n) generation; it makes haploid spores by meiosis. Dominant in angiosperms.
gametophyte
The haploid (n) generation; it makes gametes (eggs or sperm) by mitosis.
receptacle
The part of the stem where the flower parts attach.
sporophyll
A leaf specialized for reproduction; stamens and carpels are sporophylls.
carpel
Female floral organ made of stigma, style and ovary.
pistil
A single carpel (simple pistil) or a group of fused carpels.
microsporangia (pollen sacs)
Chambers in the anther where microsporocytes undergo meiosis to make pollen.
complete flower
A flower with all four floral organs.
incomplete flower
A flower missing one or more of the four floral organs.
inflorescence
A cluster of flowers.
pollination
Transfer of pollen from an anther to a stigma.
coevolution
Joint evolution of two or more interacting species in response to selection imposed by each other.
embryo sac
The female gametophyte: 7 cells (egg, 2 synergids, 3 antipodal cells, central cell with 2 polar nuclei) formed from one megaspore.
megasporangium
Diploid tissue in the ovule where a megasporocyte undergoes meiosis to make 4 megaspores.
megaspore
Haploid spore from meiosis in the ovule; only 1 of 4 survives to make the embryo sac.
integuments
Two layers surrounding the megasporangium; they become the seed coat.
micropyle
The opening between the integuments where the pollen tube enters the ovule.
synergids
Two cells on either side of the egg that help guide the pollen tube to the egg.
antipodal cells
Three cells of unknown function at the end of the embryo sac opposite the micropyle.
polar nuclei
Two nuclei in the central cell that fuse with a sperm to make the triploid endosperm.
microspore
One of four haploid, genetically different cells made by meiosis of a microsporocyte; becomes a pollen grain.
generative cell
Small cell of the pollen grain that divides to make two sperm.
tube cell
Large cell of the pollen grain whose tube nucleus produces the pollen tube.
double fertilization
One sperm fuses with the egg to form a 2n zygote and the other fuses with the two polar nuclei to form 3n endosperm.
endosperm
Triploid (3n) food-storing tissue rich in starch, lipids and proteins, used by the embryo and seedling.
cotyledons
Seed leaves, the first leaves of the embryo; one in monocots, two in eudicots.
hypocotyl
Embryonic axis below the cotyledons; becomes the stem and ends in the radicle.
radicle
Embryonic root; the first part to emerge at germination.
epicotyl
Embryonic axis above the cotyledons and below the first pair of leaves.
plumule
Epicotyl plus young leaves plus shoot apical meristem.
dormancy
Resting state of a dehydrated seed until an environmental cue signals good conditions.
imbibition
Uptake of water by a dry seed because of its low water potential; starts germination.
outbreeding
Reproduction between two genetically distinct individuals; promoted by synchronized flowering.
fruit
Mature ovary of a flower that protects the seeds and aids dispersal.

Examples

  • Brassica rapa: flowering plant used to show the life cycle from seed to seed.
  • Grasses and many trees: wind pollinated (about 20% of angiosperms).
  • Dandelion seen by a bee under UV light: shows nectar guides.
  • Bees: most important insect pollinators; bee-pollinated flowers are yellow or blue with a sweet fragrance; populations declining (colony collapse over the last ten years or so).
  • Moth on a yucca flower: moth-pollinated flowers are usually white or yellow and sweet smelling; butterfly-pollinated flowers are brightly colored.
  • Blowfly on a carrion flower: fly-pollinated flowers look and smell like rotten meat; flies lay eggs on them, but the larvae have nothing to eat.
  • Bat feeding on agave flowers at night: bat-pollinated flowers are light colored and aromatic.
  • Hummingbird: bird-pollinated flowers are large, bright red or yellow, have little scent, make large amounts of nectar, and have tube-shaped petals that fit the beak.
  • Darwin predicted a moth with a 28 cm long tongue from the shape of one flower (coevolution).
  • Garden bean: eudicot seed with two fleshy cotyledons, radicle, hypocotyl, epicotyl; hypocotyl hook during germination.
  • Maize: cotyledon stays underground; young leaves protected in a finger-shaped sheath.
  • Desert plants germinate after rainfall; fire-area seeds need heat or smoke; cold-area seeds need a period of cold; some seeds must pass through an animal gut.
  • Pea pod: simple fruit. Raspberry and blackberry: aggregate fruits. Pineapple: multiple fruit. Apple: accessory fruit (eaten part is the receptacle).
  • Tomatoes and peaches: fleshy fruits that invite animal dispersal.
  • Coconut: buoyant husk, water dispersal.
  • Dandelion (parachutes of branched hairs), tumbleweed, maple samara, winged seed of a tropical Asian climbing gourd: wind dispersal.
  • Barbs on fur or clothing, squirrel caches, seeds in black bear feces, ants carrying seeds: animal dispersal.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Figure 38.6 angiosperm life cycle: "This is a figure that you guys need to know."
  • "Make sure that you know this. Make sure that you can draw this out. And explain everything that happens in detail." (life cycle)
  • Double fertilization: "So a couple of different ways to look at this. Make sure that you know it."
  • She accepts either carpel or pistil: "I will take either term when you're talking about this entire structure."
  • Plants vs animals: plants have a multicellular haploid stage, animals do not.
  • Angiosperm gametophyte dependence is "backwards from what we saw with our more primitive plants."
  • Her wording on selfing: "you're not going to get any genetic diversity that way... you want the sperm to go to a different plant." Recognize this lecture framing on her exam (selfing gives no genetic diversity); the biological nuance is in the selfing trap.
  • Meiosis in the ovule: 4 megaspores, only 1 survives: "This is very common in females."
  • Microspores are genetically different from each other (stated twice).
  • Flower structure, seed parts (monocot vs eudicot cotyledons) and fruit types will also be covered in lab.
  • Idealized flower: "Here is our structure of an idealized flower. We're going to learn this in lab as well." Draw and label it: receptacle, sepal, petal, stamen (anther, filament), carpel (stigma, style, ovary, ovule).
  • "[Monocots] have one cotyledon. [Eudicots] have two. We will look at these in lab." Monocot = 1 cotyledon, eudicot = 2.
  • "...we will look at fruit based upon these characteristics in lab": distinguish dry and fleshy fruits; she says these characteristics will also be examined in lab.
  • "Chapter 38 is an important chapter in that. It looks at angiosperms, reproduction": she introduces Chapter 38 as important because it covers angiosperm reproduction and biotechnology.
Memory hooks
  • The 3 Fs: Flowers, Fertilization (double), FruitsThe three key features of the angiosperm life cycle.
  • Can Some Plants SmileThe four floral organs from center out: carpels, stamens, petals, sepals.
  • Stigma Style Ovary: S.S.O., top to bottomThe three parts of the carpel (pistil), in order from tip to base.
  • 3-2-1-2: 3 antipodals, 2 polar nuclei, 1 egg, 2 synergidsThe 8 nuclei in 7 cells of the embryo sac, read from the far end to the micropyle.
  • Sperm 1 + egg = 2n; sperm 2 + 2 polars = 3nDouble fertilization makes a diploid zygote and a triploid endosperm.
  • Simple Aggregate Multiple Accessory: Pea Raspberry Pineapple AppleThe four fruit types matched to the lecture example for each.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Bundle image (How do angiosperms reproduce sexually?): pollen grain from an anther is transferred to the stigma of another flower; a pollen tube grows down the style to the ovary and delivers sperm to an ovule; the egg is fertilized; the ovule becomes a seed, the ovary becomes a fruit, and the embryo develops into a plant. Labels: anther, pollen grain, stigma, pollen tube, ovary, ovule, sperm, fruit, seed.
  • Idealized flower: receptacle at the base, sepals around it, petals behind, stamens (anther on filament) around a central carpel (stigma, style, ovary with ovules).
  • Simple vs compound pistil: single carpel with one chamber in cross-section vs three fused carpels with three chambers and multiple stigmas.
  • Figure 38.6 life cycle: sporophyte flower at top; male side (anther cross-section → 4 microsporangia → microsporocyte (2n) → meiosis → 4 microspores (n) → generative cell + tube cell = pollen grain); female side (ovule → megasporangium (2n) → meiosis → 1 surviving megaspore → embryo sac with 3 antipodal cells, 2 polar nuclei, egg, 2 synergids); pollen on stigma → pollen tube → 2 sperm through micropyle → zygote (2n) + endosperm (3n) → embryo, endosperm and seed coat = seed → germination → sporophyte.
  • Embryo sac drawing: antipodal cells (3) at the end away from the micropyle; central cell with 2 polar nuclei in the middle; egg flanked by 2 synergids at the micropyle end; integuments around the outside.
  • Garden bean seed: seed coat, two cotyledons, embryo axis with radicle (bottom), hypocotyl (middle), epicotyl with young leaves and shoot apical meristem (top).
  • Bean germination sequence: radicle grows down, lateral roots, hypocotyl hook pulls cotyledons up, light straightens the hook, first leaves open, cotyledons yellow and fall off.
  • Fruit types figure: simple (pea), aggregate (raspberry), multiple (pineapple), accessory (apple).
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Angiosperm sporophytes can be several hundred feet tall and live longer than the gametophyte.
  • Bee populations are declining (bee colony collapse over the last ten years or so).
  • Darwin correctly predicted a moth with a 28 cm long tongue from the shape of one flower (coevolution).
  • Antipodal cells have unknown function.
  • Endosperm is rich in starch, lipids and proteins; the seedling uses food in the endosperm and cotyledons until it can photosynthesize.
  • After pollination the flower discards its petals and stamens: the bright petals would now attract animals that might eat the young fruit. "At each step, there is an emphasis on protecting the offspring" (animation).
  • Seed viability: most seeds 1 to 2 years, some only days, some centuries. Dormancy makes germination happen at the best time and place.
  • Some seeds need a treatment that weakens the seed coat or prolonged chilling before they germinate (animation).
  • Bean germination: lateral roots form, cotyledons turn yellow and fall off once the leaves photosynthesize.
  • Fruit ripens when the seeds finish developing. Dry fruits: hooks, wings, parasols, hairs, or tension that shoots seeds out. Fleshy fruits (tomato, peach) turn from green to a contrasting color and make sugar to attract animals.
  • Dispersal examples: coconut (buoyant husk, water); dandelion parachutes, tumbleweed, maple samara, winged seed of a tropical Asian climbing gourd (wind); barbs on fur, squirrel caches, seeds in black bear feces, ants (animals).
  • Brassica rapa time-lapse: life cycle from seed to seed; the stamens dry up and the ovary swells into a pod capped by the old style and stigma.
  • Dormancy is broken by species-specific cues: rainfall (desert), heat or smoke (fire areas), a period of cold, passing through an animal's gut (see the essay and exam sheet).
  • Shoot protection in germination: bean cotyledons enclose the shoot tip and a hypocotyl hook pulls them up, and light straightens the hook; in maize the cotyledon stays underground and the leaves push up in a finger-shaped sheath.
  • Dispersal by water, wind or animals keeps seeds from competing with the parent (see the dispersal table).
  • Flowering is synchronized to a time of year to promote outbreeding.
38.2

Flowering plants reproduce sexually, asexually, or both

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Explain how plants clone themselvesWhat is asexual reproduction, and how is the offspring related to the parent? Name and describe three asexual mechanisms. Why are vegetative progeny an advantage over seedlings?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Asexual reproduction: one parent, no fusion of egg and sperm
  • the offspring is a clone, genetically identical to the parent. Very common in angiosperms. Fragmentation: the parent separates into parts that grow into whole plants. Adventitious shoots from a parent root system become separate shoot systems
  • she calls them suckers (aspen clones). Vegetative reproduction: progeny from mature vegetative fragments, such as planting a leaf or a piece of stem. Vegetative progeny are more resilient than fragile seedlings
  • sexual plants compensate by making enormous numbers of seeds.

Common mistake: Suckers are adventitious shoots arising from roots. The outline says adventitious roots; follow her lecture.

Name four ways plants prevent selfingWhat does selfing guarantee, and what does she say it costs? List four mechanisms that prevent selfing, describe pin versus thrum, and contrast self-incompatibility with the animal immune response.List from memory
  • Selfing ensures every ovule develops into a seed, but in her wording it reduces genetic diversity: a whole lot of offspring, but they're all identical. Mechanisms: (1) staminate flowers (no carpels) and carpellate flowers (no stamens) on different plants
  • (2) stamens and carpels mature at different times
  • (3) spatial arrangement: pin = tall style (pistil tall), short stamens
  • thrum = tall stamens, short style
  • (4) self-incompatibility, rejecting its own or close relatives' pollen, the most common mechanism. Plants reject SELF
  • the animal immune response rejects NONSELF.

Common mistake: Do not reverse self and nonself: plants reject self pollen; the animal immune response rejects nonself.

Lecture video 38.2 (opens online)
Full notes for 38.2
★ She said this is on the test
  • [Self-incompatibility]... is our most common mechanism to avoid [selfing].Self-incompatibility (rejecting own pollen or close relatives' pollen) is the most common anti-selfing mechanism.
  • self incompatibility [in] plants is really a rejection of self. Whereas in the animal immune response um the rejection is non self.Plants reject self; the animal immune response rejects nonself.
  • So in essence the offspring is a clone. It is genetically identical to the parent.Asexual reproduction: one parent, no egg and sperm fusion, clone offspring.
  • um, but this process of [selfing], um reduces genetic diversity among offspring. Yes. You get a whole lot of offspring, but they're all identical.For her exam contrast, recognize the lecture wording that selfing gives no genetic diversity and all-identical offspring; see the selfing trap for the biological nuance.
Big idea
Asexual reproduction makes offspring from one parent without fusion of egg and sperm, so the offspring are clones. Fragmentation and adventitious shoots from roots are common mechanisms; when progeny come from mature vegetative fragments it is vegetative reproduction. Selfing guarantees seeds but reduces genetic diversity, so many species have mechanisms that prevent self-fertilization, the most common being self-incompatibility.

Must know

  • Asexual reproduction: offspring from a single parent, no fusion of egg and sperm; the offspring is a clone, genetically identical to the parent. Very common in angiosperms.
  • Fragmentation: separation of a parent plant into parts that develop into whole plants.
  • Some parent root systems give rise to adventitious shoots that become separate shoot systems; she calls them suckers at the base of a plant (the outline says "adventitious roots"; follow the lecture: adventitious shoots from roots).
  • Vegetative reproduction: asexual progeny from mature vegetative fragments (planting a leaf or a piece of stem).
  • Vegetative progeny are more resilient than fragile seedlings; sexual plants compensate for poor seedling survival by making enormous numbers of seeds.
  • Self-fertilization (selfing) ensures every ovule develops into a seed. Her lecture describes selfing as giving no genetic diversity and all-identical offspring; recognize that simplified contrast on her exam.
  • Mechanisms that prevent selfing: (1) staminate flowers (lack carpels) and carpellate flowers (lack stamens) on different individuals, in effect male and female plants; (2) stamens and carpels mature at different times; (3) spatial arrangement that prevents contact between stamens and stigma (pin vs thrum flowers); (4) self-incompatibility.
  • Thrum flower: tall stamens, short style. Pin flower: tall style, short stamens. Same species.
  • Self-incompatibility: the plant rejects its own pollen or that of close relatives; the most common mechanism to avoid selfing. Analogous to the animal immune response, but plants reject self while animals reject nonself.

Terms

asexual reproduction
Making offspring from a single parent without fusion of egg and sperm; offspring are clones.
fragmentation
Separation of a parent plant into parts that develop into whole plants.
adventitious shoots
Shoots arising from a parent's root system that become separate shoot systems.
vegetative reproduction
Asexual reproduction in which progeny arise from mature vegetative fragments.
staminate flower
A flower with stamens but no carpels.
carpellate flower
A flower with carpels but no stamens.
self-incompatibility
A plant's ability to reject its own pollen or the pollen of close relatives.
pin flower / thrum flower
Two flower forms of one species: pin has a tall style and short stamens, thrum has tall stamens and a short style.

Examples

  • Forest photo: patches of genetically identical trees produced asexually.
  • Houseplants: a leaf or a piece of stem planted in soil grows into a new plant (vegetative reproduction).
  • Staminate flower and carpellate flower on different plants of the same species (male and female plants).
  • Pin and thrum flowers in the same species: spatial separation of stamens and stigma.
What she stressed in lecture
  • Asexual offspring is "a clone. It is genetically identical to the parent."
  • Self-incompatibility is "our most common mechanism" to avoid selfing.
  • Plants reject self, animals reject nonself: "the analogy is kind of the same."
  • Her wording on selfing: "this process of [selfing], um reduces genetic diversity among offspring. Yes. You get a whole lot of offspring, but they're all identical." Recognize this lecture framing (no genetic diversity, all-identical offspring) on her exam; the biological nuance is in the selfing trap.
  • "I like to call these suckers": adventitious shoots from a parent root system (asexual reproduction).
Memory hooks
  • Asexual = All alikeAsexual offspring are clones, genetically identical to the one parent.
  • Different plants, Different times, Different places, Don't accept selfThe four mechanisms that prevent selfing: separate staminate and carpellate plants, staggered maturation, spatial arrangement, self-incompatibility.
  • Pin = Pistil on topPin flowers have the tall style; thrum flowers have the tall stamens.
  • Plants reject self, animals reject nonselfSelf-incompatibility is the reverse of the animal immune response.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Pin vs thrum flower: draw a thrum with anthers near the top of the tube and a short style; draw a pin with the stigma near the top and short stamens low in the tube.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Forest photo (aspen): each patch of same-colored trees is one clone produced asexually from the root system.
  • Houseplants: a leaf or a piece of stem planted in soil grows roots and becomes a new plant.
38.3

People modify crops by breeding and genetic engineering

Practice first: try each on paper, then open it to check

Match each GM crop to its traitHow were most crops domesticated, and how do breeders get new traits faster than natural mutation? Give the GM trait of Bt crops, golden rice, cassava, papaya and biofuel crops.List from memory
  • Domestication: artificial selection about 10,000 years ago (maize cannot disperse its seeds). Natural mutation is slow, so breeders induce mutations with radiation or chemicals. Natural gene transfer: wheat (hybridization), sweet potato (gene from Agrobacterium). Transgene = gene moved between organisms
  • CRISPR-Cas9 edits genes. Global hunger affects nearly a billion people. Bt corn and cotton: toxin kills insect pests, not vertebrates
  • less insecticide, less Fusarium toxin. Golden rice: vitamin A. Cassava: more iron and beta-carotene. Papaya: resists ringspot virus. Biofuels (switchgrass, poplar): carbon neutral, since CO2 taken up in photosynthesis balances CO2 released when burned.

Common mistake: Bt toxin kills insect pests only; it is not toxic to vertebrates such as humans, cows and pigs.

Weigh the biggest GMO risk and its fixesWhat is the most serious concern about GM crops, and what could it create? Give three ways to prevent it. What does the evidence say about allergens? Is GMO the same as organic?Write 3 to 5 lines on paper
  • Most serious: transgene escape by crop-to-weed hybridization, creating superweeds possibly resistant to herbicides. Prevention: male sterility (no functional pollen)
  • transgenes in chloroplast DNA (not passed in pollen)
  • flowers that never open (self-pollination only). No credible evidence that GM foods transfer allergens. Bt cotton in India: 41% less insecticide, 80% fewer acute farmer poisonings. The monarch butterfly study on Bt pollen was discredited. GMOs are not the same as organic produce.

Common mistake: Allergens are not the main worry; transgene escape into related weeds is the most serious concern.

Lecture video 38.3 (opens online)
Full notes for 38.3
★ She said this is on the test
  • Perhaps the most serious concern is the possibility of [introduced] genes escaping into related weeds through crop to weed hybridization.Transgene escape (crop-to-weed hybridization, superweeds) is the most serious concern; know the 3 prevention methods.
  • I want you guys to realize that genetically modified organisms are quite different from organic produceGMO is not the same as organic.
  • read in your textbook about these examples of genetically modified organisms and the benefits or the detrimentsEach GM example with its trait (Bt corn and cotton, golden rice, cassava, papaya, biofuels) and the benefits vs risks.
  • Keep in mind that new desirable traits can arise spontaneously through mutationNatural mutation is slow, so breeders induce mutations with radiation or chemicals.
  • Keep in mind that global hunger currently affects nearly a billion people.Nearly a billion people face hunger; GM crops aim to raise yield and nutrition.
Big idea
Most crops were domesticated about 10,000 years ago by artificial selection. Breeders also induce mutations with radiation or chemicals, and natural gene transfer has happened too (wheat by hybridization, sweet potato from Agrobacterium). Genetic engineering and CRISPR-Cas9 move or edit genes even between distant species, giving Bt crops, biofortified golden rice and cassava, virus-resistant papaya and biofuel crops. Concerns include allergens, effects on nontarget organisms and transgene escape into weeds.

Must know

  • Domestication of most crops happened over a short period about 10,000 years ago by artificial selection for favorable traits. Maize kernels are permanently attached to the cob inside the husk, so maize cannot spread its seeds and would go extinct in the wild.
  • New desirable traits arise by mutation, but natural mutation rates are slow, so breeders induce mutations by treating seeds or seedlings with radiation or chemicals.
  • Natural genetic modification predates humans: wheat evolved by natural hybridization of grass species; an ancestor of the sweet potato got a gene by horizontal gene transfer from the soil bacterium Agrobacterium.
  • Transgene = a gene transferred from one organism to another (can happen in nature). Genetic engineering moves genes between species as distant as daffodils and rice without intermediates.
  • Plant biotechnology: general sense, innovations in using plants to make useful products; specific sense, use of genetically modified (GM) organisms in agriculture and industry. CRISPR-Cas9 is a gene editing technology rapidly changing plant biology.
  • Bt toxin crops (corn): toxic to insect pests, not to vertebrates (humans, cows, pigs).
  • Biofortification improves nutritional quality: golden rice (vitamin A, for eyesight); cassava, a starchy root crop, with more iron and beta-carotene.
  • Transgenic papaya resistant to ringspot virus saved the papaya industry.
  • Biofuels from living biomass (switchgrass, poplar) are carbon neutral: CO2 taken up in photosynthesis balances CO2 released when burned. Biomass = total mass of organic matter in a group of organisms in a habitat.
  • Health: no credible evidence that GM food plants transfer allergens. Bt maize is less infected by Fusarium and has 90% less of its cancer-causing toxin. Bt cotton in India: 41% less insecticide use, 80% fewer acute poisonings of farmers. The monarch butterfly study on Bt pollen was discredited; spraying non-Bt maize harms monarchs more.
  • Most serious concern: transgene escape by crop-to-weed hybridization, making superweeds possibly resistant to herbicides. Prevention: male sterility, transgenes in chloroplast DNA (not passed in pollen), flowers that never open (self-pollination only).
  • GMOs are not the same thing as organic produce.

Terms

artificial selection
Humans breeding plants for favorable traits; how most crops were domesticated about 10,000 years ago.
transgene
A gene transferred from one organism to another.
plant biotechnology
Generally, innovations in using plants to make useful products; specifically, use of GM organisms in agriculture and industry.
genetically modified (GM) organism
An organism whose genes have been changed by genetic engineering.
CRISPR-Cas9
A gene editing technology that is rapidly changing plant biology and genetics.
Bt toxin
A toxin made by transgenic crops that kills insect pests but not vertebrates.
biofortification
Improving the nutritional quality of plants, for example golden rice and cassava.
golden rice
Transgenic rice developed to address vitamin A deficiency where rice is a staple.
biofuels
Fuels derived from living biomass.
biomass
Total mass of organic matter in a group of organisms in a particular habitat.
crop-to-weed hybridization
Transfer of transgenes from a crop into a related weed by interbreeding.
superweeds
Weeds that gain transgenes and could be resistant to many herbicides.
male sterility
Engineering GM plants so they do not make functional pollen, to stop transgene escape.

Examples

  • Maize: artificially selected kernels attached to the cob; cannot survive in the wild.
  • Wheat: evolved by natural hybridization among grass species.
  • Sweet potato ancestor: horizontal gene transfer from Agrobacterium.
  • Daffodils and rice: genes transferred between distantly related species by genetic engineering.
  • Bt corn: resists insect pests (photo of damaged vs undamaged corn).
  • Golden rice: vitamin A; approved as safe in 2018 by Canada, New Zealand, United States.
  • Cassava: biofortified with iron and beta carotene.
  • Papaya: transgenic resistance to ringspot virus saved the industry.
  • Switchgrass and poplar: biofuel crops.
  • Bt maize and Fusarium: 90% less cancer-causing toxin.
  • Bt cotton in India: 41% less insecticide, 80% fewer acute poisonings.
  • Monarch butterfly larvae: Bt pollen study discredited.
What she stressed in lecture
  • "Think about your feelings on GMOs." GMOs "are quite different from organic produce... it is not the same thing."
  • Transgene escape is "perhaps the most serious concern" and could make superweeds.
  • Releasing GM organisms could be "an unstoppable experiment, so to speak."
  • Read the textbook examples of GM organisms and their benefits and drawbacks.
Memory hooks
  • 10,000 years: 10 fingers picked the cropsDomestication by artificial selection happened about 10,000 years ago.
  • Bt = Bugs die, Backboned animals fineBt toxin kills insect pests but not vertebrates.
  • Golden rice = Good eyesight (vitamin A)Golden rice addresses vitamin A deficiency; vitamin A is needed for eyesight.
  • India Bt cotton: 41 less spray, 80 fewer poisonings41% decrease in insecticide use and 80% reduction in acute poisoning of farmers.
  • Stop escape: Male sterile, Chloroplast genes, Closed flowersThree ways to prevent transgene escape: male sterility, transgenes in chloroplast DNA, flowers that never open.

Slides

Be able to draw / explain
  • Wild corn vs domesticated maize: small wild ear vs large ear with kernels fixed to the cob inside a husk.
  • Bt corn vs non-Bt corn: insect-damaged ear vs undamaged ear.
Extra detail, lower priority
  • Wild corn looks very different from domesticated maize.
  • Plant breeders search the world for wild or domesticated plants with desirable traits.
  • CRISPR-Cas9 won a Nobel Prize; one recipient is a professor at UC Berkeley.
  • Global hunger affects nearly a billion people; food production must rise 40% per hectare by 2030 (lecture figures). Biotechnology can raise yield and quality without expanding farmland.
  • Golden rice received positive food safety evaluations in 2018 from Canada, New Zealand and the United States.
  • Cassava feeds about 800 million people (slide; the transcript's "8000 million" is a slip); biofortified cassava reduces the need for cyanide-producing chemicals in processing.
  • Releasing GM organisms could be "an unstoppable experiment, so to speak."
  • Many ecologists worry GM crops may affect non-target organisms (the monarch study was the main example).
Review

Compare tables, traps, essays

Her study guide, question by question

Q1. Define plants by listing the characteristics (traits) that make them plants. Some of these are shared by green algae. There should be a total of 5. (p. 619)

(1) Multicellular; (2) eukaryotic; (3) photosynthetic autotrophs; (4) cell walls made of cellulose; (5) chloroplasts with chlorophylls a and b. Each is also found in some algae.

Q2. List the three distinctive characteristics that are shared with charophytes. (p. 619)

(1) Circular rings of cellulose-synthesizing proteins in the plasma membrane (other algae have linear sets); (2) flagellated sperm structure that resembles charophyte sperm; (3) similarities in nuclear, chloroplast and mitochondrial DNA.

Q3. Name the terrestrial adaptations (derived traits) of land plants. (p. 620-621; see Figs. 29.5, 29.6, 29.7)

(1) Alternation of generations; (2) multicellular, dependent embryos (fed through placental transfer cells; plants are embryophytes); (3) walled spores produced in sporangia (sporopollenin walls resist drying); (4) apical meristems (cell division at root and shoot tips). Charophytes lack all four.

Q4. Alternation of generations occurs in plants and some algae. How is this different from the alternate haploid and diploid conditions found in animals? (p. 620; see Fig. 29.5)

Plants alternate two MULTICELLULAR generations: a haploid gametophyte that makes gametes by mitosis and a diploid sporophyte that makes haploid spores by meiosis; each gives rise to the other. In animals the only multicellular stage is diploid: meiosis makes gametes directly, and the single-celled gametes are the only haploid stage.

Q5. Name the additional derived traits that evolved in many plant species as a result of movement onto land. (p. 621)

Cuticle (waxy layer that prevents water loss and gives some protection from microbes); stomata (pores for CO2 and O2 exchange, main route of water loss, close in dry heat); mycorrhizae (symbiotic fungi that pass soil nutrients to the plant).

Q6. Name the three bryophyte phyla. List characteristics of each. Give common name. (Make a table!) (p. 626; see Fig. 29.13)

Hepatophyta | liverworts | liver-shaped gametophyte, thalloid or leafy; tiny sporophyte with short seta; NO stomata | Marchantia. Bryophyta | mosses | leafy gametophyte carpet from a protonema; sporophyte with foot, seta, capsule and stomata | Polytrichum, Sphagnum. Anthocerophyta | hornworts | horn-shaped sporophyte with NO seta, splits from the tip, has stomata; nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria | Anthoceros. All three: gametophyte dominant, sporophyte dependent, no vascular tissue, sperm swim through water.

Q7. Define protonema, gametophyte, sporophyte, and rhizoids as they apply to bryophytes. (p. 624-628; see Fig. 29.11)

Protonema: green, branched, one-cell-thick filaments from a germinating spore; makes buds that grow into gametophytes. Gametophyte: the dominant haploid generation, larger and longer-lived; makes eggs in archegonia and sperm in antheridia. Sporophyte: the diploid spore-producing generation, attached to and dependent on the gametophyte; a moss sporophyte has a foot, seta and capsule, while hornworts lack a seta. Rhizoids: thin anchoring cells or filaments of the gametophyte; not roots, no conducting cells, not the main absorbers.

Q8. What are some of the ecological and economic benefits of mosses? (Give 3) (p. 627-628; see Figs. 29.14, 29.15)

(1) Keep nitrogen in soil: Polytrichum cuts nitrogen leaching from sandy soil, and feather moss Pleurozium hosts nitrogen-fixing cyanobacteria. (2) Peat from Sphagnum is harvested as fuel. (3) Peat moss is a soil conditioner and packing for plant roots (absorbs about 20 times its weight in water). Also acceptable: peatlands store about one-third of the world's soil carbon.

Q9. List and briefly describe the characteristics (origins and traits) that are common to all vascular plants (5 characteristics). (p. 629-631; see Fig. 29.18)

Origin: ancestors had branched sporophytes that did not depend on the gametophyte. (1) Dominant sporophyte; (2) xylem and phloem: xylem carries water and minerals through dead, lignified conducting cells; phloem carries sugars through living conducting cells; (3) roots that absorb water and nutrients and anchor; (4) leaves: microphylls (one vein, lycophytes only) or megaphylls (branched veins); (5) sporophylls, leaves that bear sporangia (sori in ferns, strobili in lycophytes).

Q10. Define homosporous and heterosporous. Define megasporangium and microsporangium. Be sure to include what each of these structures make. (p. 631; see homosporous vs heterosporous diagram)

Homosporous: one type of sporangium and one type of spore, which usually grows into a bisexual gametophyte. Heterosporous: two types of spores. Megasporangium (on a megasporophyll) makes megaspores, which develop into female gametophytes (eggs). Microsporangium (on a microsporophyll) makes microspores, which develop into male gametophytes (sperm).

Q11. Describe the seedless vascular plants. List and briefly describe all phyla including major members. (p. 631-632; see Fig. 29.19)

Vascular tissue but no seeds; flagellated sperm need water, so most live in damp places; formed the first forests. Lycophyta: club mosses (homosporous), spikemosses and quillworts (heterosporous); microphylls; strobili. Monilophyta: ferns (megaphyll fronds, fiddleheads, sori, homosporous), horsetails (Equisetum, jointed gritty stems that do the photosynthesis), whisk ferns (Psilotum, the only vascular plants without true roots).

Q12. List the five key adaptations that are needed for life on land. (p. 637; see Figs. 30.2, 30.3)

Seeds; reduced gametophytes; heterospory; ovules; pollen.

Q13. What are the advantages of having a reduced gametophyte? (2 total) (p. 637-638; see Fig. 30.2)

(1) Protection: the gametophyte develops from a spore kept inside the parent sporophyte's sporangium, so moist sporophyte tissue shields it from UV radiation and drying. (2) Nutrition: it gets nutrients from the parent sporophyte.

Q14. List the four phyla of gymnosperms. List at least one example of each and all major characteristics (make a table). (p. 642-643; see Fig. 30.7)

Cycadophyta | Cycas revoluta | large cones, palmlike leaves, flagellated sperm, most endangered plant group. Ginkgophyta | Ginkgo biloba | one living species, fanlike deciduous leaves, flagellated sperm, tolerates pollution, smelly fleshy seeds. Gnetophyta | Gnetum, Ephedra, Welwitschia | three genera grouped by DNA; Gnetum has flowering-plant-like leaves in tropical habitats; Ephedra is a desert shrub associated with ephedrine; Welwitschia has straplike leaves and lives in southwestern African deserts. Coniferophyta | pines, firs, redwoods | largest phylum (about 600 species), woody cones, needle or scale leaves, mostly evergreen, wind pollen.

Q15. Define "naked seed". How long does it take a pine tree to produce male and female gametophytes and bring them together after the young cones first appear on the plant? (p. 640-641; see Figs. 30.3, 30.4)

Naked seed: a seed not enclosed in an ovary, exposed on sporophylls (cone scales). A pine takes nearly three years from cone appearance to producing gametophytes, bringing them together, and forming mature seeds (fertilization comes more than a year after pollination).

Q16. What are the two key adaptations of the phylum anthophyta? (p. 644-645)

Flowers and fruits.

Q17. Draw and label figure 30.8. (p. 644; see Fig. 30.8)

Outer to inner on the receptacle: sepal (green, encloses the bud), petal (attracts pollinators), stamen = anther (makes pollen) + filament, carpel (simple pistil) = stigma (receives pollen) + style + ovary (holds ovules). Label ovule inside the ovary.

Q18. What is a fruit? Name the major ways that fruit can be dispersed into the environment (3). (p. 645; see Figs. 30.10, 30.11)

Fruit: a mature ovary; it protects seeds and aids dispersal. Three ways: wind (dandelion parachutes, maple wings), water (coconut), animals (burrs cling to fur; edible fruits eaten and seeds passed in feces).

Q19. Describe double fertilization in angiosperms. Pay attention to detail! You are describing figure 30.12. (p. 646; see Fig. 30.12)

Pollen lands on the stigma and germinates; the tube cell grows a pollen tube down the style; the generative cell has divided into two sperm. The tube enters the ovule through the micropyle and releases both sperm into the embryo sac (egg, synergids, central cell with two polar nuclei, antipodal cells). Sperm 1 + egg = 2n zygote (becomes the embryo). Sperm 2 + two polar nuclei = 3n cell (becomes the endosperm, food). The ovule becomes a seed and the ovary a fruit.

Q20. What are the four major clades of angiosperms? (p. 649-650; see Figs. 30.14b, 30.17)

Basal angiosperms (Amborella, water lilies, star anise), magnoliids (magnolias, laurels, black pepper), monocots (orchids, grasses, palms), eudicots (legumes, roses, oaks).

Q21. What are the differing characteristics between monocots and eudicots? Make a chart by using columns. (p. 649; see Fig. 30.16)

Trait | monocot | eudicot. Cotyledons | 1 | 2. Leaf veins | parallel | netlike. Stem vascular tissue | scattered | ring. Roots | fibrous | taproot. Pollen | 1 opening | 3 openings. Floral organs | multiples of 3 | multiples of 4 or 5.

Q22. From which plant does Atropine come from? What is it used for? (p. 651; see Table 30.1)

The belladonna plant; it dilates the pupil of the eye.

Q23. Describe some threats to plant diversity. (p. 651-652; see Fig. 30.18)

The growing human population and its demand for space and resources destroys habitat, especially by clear-cutting tropical forests (worst in the tropics). Results: plant extinctions plus the animals that depend on them, less CO2 absorbed by photosynthesis (possible global warming), and loss of unexplored foods and medicines. Plant diversity is nonrenewable.

Q24. Know the general plant life cycle, moss life cycle and angiosperm life cycle. (p. 620, 624, 646; see Figs. 29.5, 29.11, 30.12)

General: gametophyte (n) makes gametes by mitosis, fertilization, zygote (2n), mitosis to sporophyte (2n), meiosis in sporangia, spores (n), mitosis to gametophyte. Moss: spore, protonema, buds, male and female gametophytes, sperm swim to the egg in the archegonium, zygote (2n), sporophyte (foot, seta, capsule) grows on and depends on the female gametophyte, meiosis in the capsule, spores released through the peristome. Angiosperm: flower on the sporophyte; anther: microsporocyte meiosis, microspores, pollen grain (tube cell, generative cell); ovule: megasporocyte meiosis, one of four megaspores survives, three mitoses, embryo sac; pollination, pollen tube through the micropyle, double fertilization (zygote 2n, endosperm 3n); seed inside fruit; germination into a new sporophyte.

The three bryophyte phyla (Guide Q6)

Trait lists the guide asks for (Q1, Q2, Q3, Q5, Q9, Q12)

Guide questionAnswer list
Q1 Plant traits (5)Multicellular; eukaryotic; photosynthetic autotroph; cellulose cell walls; chloroplasts with chlorophylls a and b
Q2 Shared only with charophytes (3)Rings of cellulose-synthesizing proteins; similar flagellated sperm; nuclear, chloroplast and mitochondrial DNA similarity
Q3 Derived land-plant traits (4)Alternation of generations; multicellular dependent embryos; walled spores produced in sporangia; apical meristems
Q5 Additional traits in many plantsCuticle; stomata; mycorrhizal associations with fungi
Q9 Vascular plant traits (5)Dominant sporophyte; xylem and phloem; roots; leaves; sporophylls (from branched, independent sporophyte ancestors)
Q12 Seed plant adaptations (5)Seeds; reduced gametophytes; heterospory; ovules; pollen
Traps (ch29-30)
  • Alternation of generations vs animal life cycles: plants have a MULTICELLULAR haploid stage (gametophyte) and a multicellular diploid stage (sporophyte). In animals the diploid body makes gametes directly by meiosis and the gametes are the only haploid cells.
  • In plants, gametes are made by MITOSIS (gametophyte is already haploid). Meiosis makes SPORES, not gametes.
  • Charophytes do not have alternation of generations, even though some other algae do.
  • Chloroplasts, cellulose walls and chlorophyll a and b are shared with other algae, so they are not evidence for charophytes specifically. The charophyte-only evidence is protein rings, sperm, and DNA.
  • Bryophyte = informal group of all nonvascular plants; Bryophyta = the moss phylum only. Bryophytes are not a clade.
  • Club moss, Irish moss, reindeer moss and Spanish moss are not mosses.
  • Rhizoids are not roots: they anchor but lack conducting cells and are not the main absorbers.
  • In bryophytes the dominant, long-lived generation is the haploid gametophyte; in ferns and seed plants it is the diploid sporophyte.
  • The hornwort sporophyte lacks a seta; liverworts lack stomata.
  • Microphylls are found only in lycophytes; ferns have megaphylls.
  • Club mosses are homosporous; spikemosses and quillworts are heterosporous, so not all seedless vascular plants are homosporous.
  • Megaspore leads to the FEMALE gametophyte; microspore leads to the MALE gametophyte.
  • Xylem conducting cells are dead at maturity; phloem cells are alive.
  • Whisk ferns lack true roots, but DNA shows they are close relatives of ferns, not living fossils of early vascular plants.
  • A pollen grain CONTAINS the male gametophyte; the pollen wall comes partly from the sporophyte.
  • Pollination (pollen reaches the ovule or stigma) is not fertilization (sperm fuses with egg). In pines, fertilization occurs more than a year after pollination.
  • Pine timing: nearly THREE years from cone appearance to gametophytes meeting and mature seeds.
  • Cycads and ginkgos have flagellated sperm; most other gymnosperms and all angiosperms do not.
  • Fruit = mature ovary; seed = mature ovule. A cereal grain is a fruit, not a seed.
  • Endosperm is triploid (3n) from sperm + two polar nuclei; the zygote is diploid (2n). Double fertilization in gnetophytes makes two embryos, not endosperm.
  • Stamen = microsporophyll (pollen); carpel = megasporophyll (ovules). Sepals and petals are sterile.
  • "Dicots" is not a clade; the clade is eudicots. The four clades are basal angiosperms, magnoliids, monocots, eudicots.
  • Atropine comes from belladonna and DILATES (widens) the pupil; it does not constrict it. It is the only Table 30.1 medicine the guide asks for (Q22).
  • Guide Q12 calls the five items "adaptations needed for life on land"; the textbook (p. 637) calls them the key adaptations of seed plants. The list is the same: seeds, reduced gametophytes, heterospory, ovules, pollen.
  • Guide Q8 (give 3): answer with clear benefits (nitrogen retention, peat as fuel, peat moss as soil conditioner or root packing, carbon storage). Surviving drought is an adaptation of mosses, not a benefit.
  • In pine pollen cones, meiosis of microsporocytes makes microspores; each microspore then develops into a pollen grain. Meiosis does not make pollen grains directly.
  • Guide Q18: the three major dispersal ways are wind, water and animals. Explosive action (Fig. 30.11) is an extra mechanism, not one of the three.

Essay / short-answer prompts

Draw and explain the general plant life cycle (alternation of generations). Label each generation, its ploidy, and where mitosis, meiosis and fertilization occur. Then explain how it differs from the haploid and diploid stages of an animal life cycle.

  • Two multicellular generations alternate: the haploid (n) gametophyte and the diploid (2n) sporophyte; each gives rise to the other.
  • Step 1: the gametophyte produces haploid gametes (egg and sperm) by MITOSIS.
  • Step 2: two gametes fuse (FERTILIZATION) to form a diploid zygote.
  • Step 3: the zygote divides by mitosis into a multicellular diploid sporophyte.
  • Step 4: the sporophyte produces haploid spores by MEIOSIS in sporangia.
  • Step 5: each spore divides by mitosis into a new multicellular haploid gametophyte.
  • Meiosis and fertilization are the two switch points between n and 2n; charophytes lack this cycle, so it is a derived plant trait.
  • Animals: the only multicellular stage is diploid; meiosis makes gametes directly, and the single-celled gametes are the only haploid stage.
  • Plants: meiosis makes spores, not gametes; the spores grow into a MULTICELLULAR haploid gametophyte, which makes gametes by mitosis.

Describe the life cycle of a moss. Name each stage and structure, give its ploidy, and explain why the gametophyte is called dominant.

  • Haploid spores (n) released from the capsule germinate into protonemata: green, branched, one-cell-thick threads.
  • Protonemata produce buds that grow by mitosis into male and female gametophytes (n), anchored by rhizoids.
  • Antheridia on male gametophytes make many flagellated sperm; archegonia on female gametophytes each hold one egg.
  • Sperm swim through a film of moisture to the egg; fertilization occurs inside the archegonium, making a 2n zygote.
  • Zygote develops into a sporophyte embryo retained in the archegonium; the sporophyte grows a long seta out of the archegonium.
  • Sporophyte (2n) = foot (absorbs nutrients from the gametophyte), seta, capsule (sporangium); it stays attached and nutritionally dependent on the gametophyte.
  • Meiosis in the capsule makes haploid spores; the lid pops off and the peristome releases spores gradually.
  • The gametophyte is dominant because it is larger, longer-living and independent; the sporophyte is present only part of the time.

Draw and label Figure 30.8, the structure of an idealized flower. Give the function of each labeled part.

  • Sepal: outermost, usually green; encloses the flower before it opens; sterile.
  • Petal: inside the sepals, usually brightly colored; attracts pollinators; sterile.
  • Stamen (microsporophyll) = filament (stalk) + anther (terminal sac where pollen is produced).
  • Carpel (megasporophyll, simple pistil) = stigma (sticky tip that receives pollen) + style (leads to the ovary) + ovary (base; contains ovules).
  • Ovule: inside the ovary; contains the female gametophyte; becomes a seed if fertilized.
  • Receptacle: base of the flower where the floral organs attach.
  • Order from outside in: sepals, petals, stamens, carpels; the ovary becomes the fruit.

Describe the life cycle of an angiosperm (Fig. 30.12) from the mature flower to the germinating seed. Describe double fertilization in detail, with the cells involved, their ploidy, and the products.

  • The mature flowering plant is the diploid sporophyte; stamens (microsporophylls) and carpels (megasporophylls) make spores.
  • Anther: microsporocytes (2n) undergo meiosis to make four microspores (n); each becomes a pollen grain (male gametophyte) with a generative cell (divides into two sperm) and a tube cell (makes the pollen tube).
  • Ovule: the megasporocyte undergoes meiosis to make four megaspores; usually one survives and divides three times by mitosis to form the embryo sac (female gametophyte): egg, synergids, two polar nuclei in the central cell, antipodal cells.
  • Pollination: pollen lands on the sticky stigma, absorbs water and germinates; the pollen tube grows down the style, enters the ovule through the MICROPYLE and discharges two sperm into the embryo sac.
  • Sperm 1 (n) + egg (n) = diploid zygote (2n), which becomes the embryo.
  • Sperm 2 (n) + two polar nuclei (n + n) = triploid cell (3n), which becomes the endosperm, the food supply for the embryo.
  • Double fertilization is unique to angiosperms (gnetophyte double fertilization gives two embryos, not endosperm).
  • Ovule becomes a seed (embryo 2n, endosperm 3n, seed coat 2n from integuments); ovary becomes a fruit; the seed germinates into a new sporophyte.

Compare the relationship between gametophyte and sporophyte in mosses, ferns and seed plants. Then explain the two advantages of the reduced gametophyte of seed plants and list the five key adaptations of seed plants.

  • Mosses: gametophyte dominant; sporophyte reduced and dependent on the gametophyte for nutrition.
  • Ferns: sporophyte dominant; gametophyte reduced but independent, photosynthetic and free-living.
  • Seed plants: sporophyte dominant; gametophyte reduced, usually microscopic, and dependent on the surrounding sporophyte tissue (in cones or flowers).
  • Advantage 1: gametophytes develop from spores retained in the parent sporophyte's sporangia, so moist sporophyte tissue protects them from UV radiation and drying out.
  • Advantage 2: the gametophyte obtains nutrients from the parent sporophyte.
  • Five seed plant adaptations: seeds, reduced gametophytes, heterospory, ovules, pollen.
  • Pollen frees fertilization from water; seeds protect the embryo, supply food and allow dormancy and long-distance dispersal.
