Traps (ch31)
  • Fungi are more closely related to ANIMALS than to plants (opisthokonts). The old view put them with plants and in botany classes.
  • Fungal walls are chitin, not cellulose. Cellulose is the plant wall polymer.
  • Plasmogamy fuses CYTOPLASM; karyogamy fuses NUCLEI. Plasmogamy comes first, and karyogamy can be delayed hours, days or centuries.
  • In the generalized fungal cycle taught here, the normal condition is HAPLOID and the only diploid cell is the short-lived zygote. Exception (supplementary yeast video only): diploid Saccharomyces cells can bud. Answer her exam with the haploid rule.
  • Heterokaryotic and dikaryotic (n+n) are used interchangeably in this class; n+n is not the same as 2n because the nuclei have not fused.
  • Asexual reproduction happens in GOOD conditions; sexual in POOR conditions. Students often reverse this.
  • Arbuscules pass through the plant cell WALL but not the plasma MEMBRANE. Ectomycorrhizae stay outside cells, between them.
  • Hyphae grow in length, not girth. Multicellular fungi are not motile; they spread by growing.
  • Yeasts reproduce asexually by budding WITHOUT spores; molds reproduce asexually by mitosis with spores.
  • Septa have pores that let cytoplasm and organelles move; in her framing the nuclei stay in their compartment. Coenocytic hyphae have no septa.
  • Ascus = sac with 8 ascospores in Neurospora (meiosis gives 4, then one mitosis makes 8; yeast asci hold 4). Basidium = club with 4 basidiospores (meiosis only).
  • Conidia are asexual naked spores of ascomycetes. Ascospores and basidiospores are sexual spores.
  • Chytrids (and cryptomycetes) have flagellated spores. Microsporidians use a harpoon-like organelle, not flagella. Zoopagomycetes and later groups have nonflagellated, wind-dispersed spores.
  • Source conflict: the outline says zygosporangia are "metabolically active"; the lecture says they are metabolically INACTIVE and resistant to freezing and drying. Follow the instructor's lecture (inactive, dormant).
  • Source conflict: the Ascomycete outline says "fusion of conidia from different mating type"; the lecture says a conidium fuses with hyphae of a mycelium of a different mating type. Follow the lecture.
  • Name change: Mucoromycota "used to be referred to as Zygomycota," and the lab and the Zygomycota video use Zygomycota. Same bread-mold group (Rhizopus).
  • Species counts: the lecture says about 145,000 known species and 2.2 to 3.8 million estimated; the "What are Fungi?" video says more than 70,000 described and more than 1.5 million estimated. Use the lecture numbers.
  • The Zygomycota video calls it one of "five major phyla"; the lecture tree shows seven lineages. Use the lecture tree.
  • Lichen asexual structures are SOREDIA (the transcript's "meridia" is a mishearing).
  • Lichen fungal partners are usually ASCOMYCETES; about 25% of ascomycete species form lichens. Most endophytes are also ascomycetes.
  • About 30% of fungi are parasites or pathogens, and mostly of PLANTS. Animals are less susceptible.
  • Ergot is a fungus (an ascomycete) on rye. It causes ergotism, but an ergot compound is also used medically to lower blood pressure and stop bleeding after childbirth.
  • Valley fever (coccidioidomycosis) is a systemic mycosis of the lungs. Yeast infections (Candida albicans) are opportunistic mycoses. Ringworm and athlete's foot are skin mycoses.
  • Penicillium is the fungus; penicillin is the antibiotic.
  • Saccharomyces cerevisiae is a yeast (an ascomycete). It reproduces asexually by budding; the Ascomycota video adds that its sexual cycle (conjugation of a and alpha cells) skips the dikaryotic stage and gives 4 ascospores per ascus, unlike the 8 in Neurospora.
  • The Basidiomycota video says some basidiomycetes also reproduce asexually by fragmented hyphae; the lecture mushroom figure shows only the sexual cycle.
  • Conidiophore appears in the lecture, but the instructor says she will not hold you responsible for that term.
  • Extracellular digestion animation says RNA is broken into "nucleic acids"; RNA already is a nucleic acid, and the absorbable products are nucleotides.
  • Nucleariids are printed on the opisthokont slide, but she said "we're not going to talk about" them. Not required.
  • Her lecture contrast: asexual offspring are genetically identical and variation comes from karyogamy plus meiosis. Answer her exam that way. Nuance: this classroom contrast does not exclude mutation, which can still arise during asexual reproduction.

Essay / short-answer prompts

Draw and label the generalized fungal life cycle. Show both the asexual and sexual loops, indicate the ploidy of each stage, and explain what environmental conditions favor each loop.

  • Mycelium (n) is shared by both loops.
  • Asexual loop: mycelium makes spore-producing structures (n), which release spores (n) by mitosis; germination gives a new mycelium; offspring are genetically identical.
  • Sexual loop: hyphae of + and - mating types (signaled by pheromones) fuse cytoplasm in plasmogamy.
  • Heterokaryotic / dikaryotic stage (n+n): two haploid nuclei in one cell, not fused.
  • Karyogamy (fusion of nuclei) makes a short-lived diploid (2n) zygote.
  • Meiosis gives haploid spores (4 per diploid cell) that germinate into mycelium.
  • Haploid is the normal fungal state; diploid is transient and only in the sexual cycle.
  • Good conditions favor asexual reproduction; unfavorable conditions trigger sexual reproduction, which gives genetic variation.

Describe the life cycle of Rhizopus (a mucoromycete). Include asexual and sexual reproduction and explain why the zygosporangium helps the fungus survive.

  • Coenocytic hyphae spread over and penetrate food (bread), absorbing nutrients.
  • Asexual: sporangia develop at the tips of upright hyphae and release hundreds of air-dispersed haploid spores while conditions are good.
  • Sexual (deteriorating conditions): hyphae of + and - mating types meet and fuse (plasmogamy).
  • A young zygosporangium forms that is heterokaryotic (n+n).
  • Karyogamy inside the zygosporangium gives diploid nuclei.
  • The zygosporangium is durable, metabolically inactive and resistant to freezing and drying, so it waits out poor conditions.
  • When conditions improve, meiosis occurs and the zygosporangium germinates into a sporangium that releases haploid spores.
  • Both cycles end in haploid spores, the normal fungal condition.

Compare the sexual life cycles of an ascomycete (Neurospora crassa) and a basidiomycete (mushroom). Identify where karyogamy and meiosis happen, how many spores form, and what the fruiting body is called in each. Include the asexual spores of Neurospora.

  • Both: plasmogamy between + and - mating types makes dikaryotic (n+n) hyphae; karyogamy then meiosis make haploid sexual spores.
  • Neurospora: asexual conidia (naked spores at hyphal tips); a conidium fuses with a mycelium of the other mating type.
  • Neurospora: asci form at tips of dikaryotic hyphae; karyogamy and meiosis occur in the ascus; one mitosis after meiosis gives 8 ascospores, released one at a time.
  • Ascomycete fruiting body = ascocarp. Ascomycetes are sac fungi.
  • Neurospora is a bread mold and model organism; genome published in 2003.
  • Mushroom: long-lived dikaryotic mycelium grows into a basidiocarp (mushroom) of tightly packed dikaryotic hyphae.
  • Gills under the cap are lined with basidia; karyogamy in the basidium is followed right away by meiosis, giving 4 basidiospores, dispersed by wind.
  • Basidiomycetes are club fungi, named for the club-shaped basidium.

Explain how the structure of a fungus (hyphae, mycelium, cell walls, septa) fits its mode of nutrition. Then compare ectomycorrhizal and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi.

  • Fungi are heterotrophs that secrete hydrolytic enzymes and absorb the small molecules released (extracellular digestion).
  • Hyphae are thin tubular filaments; the mycelium (mass of hyphae) infiltrates food and maximizes surface-to-volume ratio for absorption.
  • Hyphae grow in length, not girth, with cytoplasmic streaming to the tips; fungi are not motile but grow into new food.
  • Chitin walls strengthen hyphae and keep cells from bursting under osmotic pressure during absorption.
  • Septa with pores let cytoplasm and organelles flow; coenocytic hyphae lack septa and are multinucleate.
  • Mycorrhizae: fungus gives phosphate ions and minerals; plant gives organic nutrients. Most vascular plants depend on them.
  • Ectomycorrhizal: sheath over root surface, hyphae in extracellular spaces of the root cortex.
  • Arbuscular: arbuscules pass through the root cell wall but not the plasma membrane, which is pushed in around them; about 85% of plant species.

Describe lichens: the partners, what each partner gives, the three growth forms, how lichens reproduce, and why they matter on new land. Then name one fungal decomposer role, one plant pathogen and one animal mycosis from the lecture.

  • Lichen = fungus (usually an ascomycete) plus photosynthetic green algae or cyanobacteria; named with their own binomials because the symbiosis is so complete.
  • Fungus gives shape, most of the mass and a suitable environment; the partner sits in a layer below the surface and gives carbon compounds; cyanobacteria also fix nitrogen.
  • Forms: crustose (crusty, on rock), foliose (leaf-like), fruticose (shrub-like).
  • Asexual reproduction by fragmentation or soredia (hyphae with embedded algae, both partners); the fungus can also reproduce sexually.
  • Pioneers on new rock and soil (volcanic flows, burned forests); break rock down chemically; probably came onto land first and helped plants make the transition.
  • Decomposer: fungi and bacteria break down cellulose and lignin and recycle elements; without them life as we know it would cease.
  • Plant pathogen example: ergots on rye (Claviceps purpurea, ergotism, about 40,000 deaths in France around 944 C.E.), corn smut or tar spot; about 30% of fungi are parasites or pathogens, mostly of plants.
  • Animal mycosis example: chytrids and about 500 amphibian species, ringworm or athlete's foot, valley fever, or opportunistic Candida albicans.

Tracheid (xylem)Lignified secondary wall with pitsNoLong, thin, tapered; water passes through pits
Vessel element (xylem)Lignified secondary wall, perforation platesNoWider, shorter, thinner walled; forms vessels
Sieve-tube element (phloem)Sieve plates at endsYes, but no nucleus, ribosomes, vacuoleSugar transport in angiosperms; served by companion cell
Sieve cell (phloem)Primary wallYesSugar transport in seedless vascular plants and gymnosperms

MesophyllNot split into two layers in lecture2 layers: palisade and spongy

Primary vs secondary growth

FeaturePrimary growthSecondary growth
DirectionLengthThickness (circumference)
MeristemApical meristems (root and shoot tips, axillary buds)Lateral meristems: vascular cambium and cork cambium

Direction of new cellsInside and outside (more inside)Outside only (her exam answer; the textbook animation adds some storage tissue inward)

Root zones (tip upward)

Zone of cell divisionApical meristem, mitosis

Modified roots, stems and leaves

OrganModifications (example)
RootProp roots, storage roots (beet), pneumatophores, strangling aerial roots, buttress roots
StemRhizomes, stolons (strawberry), tubers (potato)

Traps (ch35)
  • Potato tubers and rhizomes are modified stems, not roots.
  • Onion layers are storage leaves, not stems or roots.
  • Pith is ground tissue inside the vascular tissue; cortex is ground tissue outside it. Both are ground tissue, not vascular.
  • Periderm is cork cambium plus cork only. Bark is broader: everything outside the vascular cambium, including secondary phloem.
  • Vascular cambium adds cells both ways (more xylem inside); cork cambium adds only to the outside. The embedded textbook animation says cork cambium can also make some storage tissue inward; on her exam, answer outward only.
  • Wood is secondary xylem, not secondary phloem. Old secondary phloem is sloughed off and does not accumulate.
  • Heartwood is the older inner xylem that no longer transports; sapwood is outer and still conducts.
  • Xylem conducting cells are dead at maturity; phloem sieve-tube elements are alive but lack nucleus, ribosomes and vacuole.
  • Collenchyma is alive at maturity with primary walls; sclerenchyma is usually dead with lignified secondary walls.
  • Tracheids use pits; vessel elements have perforation plates. Sieve plates belong to phloem, not xylem.
  • Sieve cells are in seedless vascular plants and gymnosperms; sieve-tube elements with companion cells are in angiosperms.
  • Lateral roots come from the pericycle and damage overlying tissue; lateral shoots come from surface axillary buds and do not.
  • The endodermis is the innermost cortex layer; the pericycle is just inside it, the outer layer of the vascular cylinder.
  • Zone of differentiation and zone of maturation are the same zone.
  • Xylem flow is one direction (roots up); phloem is multidirectional.
  • Eudicot stem bundles are in a ring; monocot stem bundles are scattered. Do not mix up with root patterns.
  • Outline says secondary growth occurs in "most eudicots"; the lecture says "many eudicots" and notes many short-lived eudicots lack it. Either way monocots rarely have it.
  • Determinate growth is in most animals and some plant organs (such as leaves); plants as a whole show indeterminate growth.
  • A closer axillary bud to the apical bud is more inhibited, not less.
  • Thick tree rings mean warm or wet years; thin rings mean cold or dry years.
  • The root stele is called a solid vascular cylinder, but a monocot root has parenchyma in the center: a ring of alternating xylem and phloem around a parenchyma core. "Alternating rings" means alternating xylem and phloem regions in one ring, not concentric rings.
  • Lenticels are pores in the periderm (woody stems); stomata are pores in the epidermis (leaves, young stems). Pits connect tracheids; sieve plates connect sieve-tube elements.
  • She calls the underwater leaves of the water plant tubular; the figure shows them finely divided. Either way the point is developmental plasticity.
  • Companion cells do not give their nucleus or ribosomes to the sieve-tube element. They keep their own and support it through plasmodesmata.
  • Pneumatophores, prop roots, buttress roots, strangling aerial roots, storage roots are roots; rhizomes, stolons, potato tubers are stems; the tendrils shown in lecture, spines, onion layers are leaves. Nuance: some plants (grape) have stem tendrils, so a tendril is not always a leaf.

Essay / short-answer prompts

Draw and label the body of a flowering plant. Explain the three organs, the two systems, and the three tissue systems. Name all five root modifications with their functions, and give one modified stem and one modified leaf.

  • Labels: shoot system, root system, apical bud, axillary bud, node, internode, blade, petiole, stem, vegetative shoot, reproductive shoot (flower), taproot, lateral roots.
  • Cells form tissues and tissues form organs (emergent properties). Shoot system = stems + leaves; root system = roots; roots need shoot sugar, shoots need root water and minerals.
  • Roots anchor, absorb water and minerals, store carbohydrates. Prop roots and buttress roots support; storage roots such as beet store food; pneumatophores reach air for oxygen; strangling aerial roots wrap a host tree.
  • Stems: nodes, internodes, apical bud elongates, axillary bud can become branch, thorn or flower. Modified stem example: tuber (potato), rhizome, stolon (strawberry), all stems not roots.
  • Leaves: main photosynthetic organ, blade + petiole. Modified leaf example: leaf tendril (lecture example), spine (cactus), storage leaf (onion), reproductive leaf.
  • Dermal (epidermis, cuticle, guard cells, trichomes; periderm in woody parts), vascular (xylem and phloem), ground (pith inside, cortex outside); all three continuous through the plant.

Name and describe the five major plant cell types. For each give the wall, whether it is alive at maturity, and its function. Draw a tracheid beside a vessel element, and a sieve-tube element with its companion cell.

  • Parenchyma: thin flexible primary walls, large central vacuole, alive, most metabolism, keeps the ability to divide and differentiate.
  • Collenchyma: strands, unevenly thickened primary walls, alive at maturity, flexible support of young shoots that does not restrain growth.
  • Sclerenchyma: rigid lignified secondary walls, many dead at maturity, walls remain for support; sclereids (pear grit) and fibers.
  • Xylem water-conducting cells: dead, lignified walls; tracheids long, thin, tapered, water moves through pits; vessel elements wider, shorter, thinner walled, joined end to end by perforation plates. Xylem flow is one way, roots to shoots.
  • Phloem sugar-conducting cells: sieve-tube elements alive but lack nucleus, ribosomes, vacuole; sieve plates at the ends; companion cell keeps its own nucleus and ribosomes and supports it through plasmodesmata. Sieve cells in seedless vascular plants and gymnosperms. Phloem flow is multidirectional.

Compare primary and secondary growth, then describe secondary growth in a woody stem over several years. Draw the cell-addition diagram and a woody stem section. Define wood, tree ring, heartwood, sapwood, periderm, bark, and lenticels.

  • Primary growth = length from apical meristems (root tips, shoot tips, axillary buds); initials keep the meristem a meristem; primary meristems: protoderm to dermal, ground meristem to ground, procambium to vascular.
  • Secondary growth = thickness from lateral meristems; in gymnosperms and most eudicots, rare in monocots, none in leaves; occurs at the same time as primary growth.
  • Vascular cambium (wholly responsible for secondary vascular tissue) adds secondary xylem inward and secondary phloem outward, more xylem, so wood is thicker than bark; youngest cells next to the cambium.
  • Cork cambium adds cork only outward; cork cells deposit suberin and die; cork replaces the epidermis; cork cambium re-forms deeper in the cortex as outer tissues slough off.
  • Wood = secondary xylem; early wood (spring, thin walls, wide vessels) and late wood (summer, thick walls); a ring = one year (thick warm/wet, thin cold/dry); heartwood no longer conducts, sapwood still does; old secondary phloem is sloughed.
  • Periderm = cork cambium + cork. Bark = everything outside the vascular cambium (secondary phloem + periderm). Lenticels = pores in the periderm for gas exchange.

Draw and label a root tip in long section and the cross sections of a eudicot root and a monocot root. Describe each zone and explain how lateral roots form and why their origin matters.

  • Root cap protects the apical meristem and secretes polysaccharide slime.
  • Zone of cell division (apical meristem), zone of elongation, zone of differentiation (= maturation) where root hairs appear.
  • Cross section from outside in: epidermis, cortex (parenchyma), endodermis (innermost cortex, regulates water and minerals entering), pericycle, vascular cylinder.
  • Eudicot root: X-shaped lobed xylem core with phloem between the arms. Monocot root: ring of alternating xylem and phloem around a parenchyma core.
  • Lateral roots arise from the pericycle, push through cortex and epidermis, and keep xylem and phloem continuous with the main root (bridge built from the road).

Draw and label a shoot tip, cross sections of a eudicot stem and a monocot stem, and a eudicot leaf cross section. Explain apical dominance and the function of each leaf tissue.

  • Shoot tip: shoot apical meristem, leaf primordia (protect, become leaves), young leaves, axillary buds (only these four).
  • Apical dominance: apical bud keeps axillary buds dormant, closer buds more inhibited; snipping the tip makes the plant bushy. Lateral shoots from axillary buds do not damage stem tissue.
  • Eudicot stem: epidermis, cortex, ring of vascular bundles (xylem inside, phloem outside), pith. Monocot stem: epidermis, scattered bundles in ground tissue. Xylem cells larger and thicker walled.
  • Leaf: cuticle and epidermis reduce water loss; stomata with two guard cells exchange CO2 and O2 and open or close osmotically.
  • Palisade mesophyll (top, chloroplasts) and spongy mesophyll (air spaces); veins = vascular bundles (xylem on top, phloem below) giving support; bundle sheath regulates movement to the mesophyll.

Xylem transport vs phloem transport

FeatureXylemPhloem
CarriesWater and dissolved minerals (xylem sap)Sugars, mostly sucrose (phloem sap); also macromolecules, viruses, signals
DirectionOne way: roots to shootsSource to sink, either direction (up or down)
PressureMainly negative pressure (pull, tension); root pressure is a minor positive pushPositive pressure (push, pressure flow)
Main driverTranspiration (cohesion-tension); root pressure is minorActive loading of sugar at source, water follows by osmosis
Energy from plantNone for bulk flow (solar powered)ATP needed to load sugar
Conducting cellsTracheids and vessel elements: dead, hollow, no cytoplasm. Vessel elements are joined end to end by perforation plates; water passes between tracheids through pits.Sieve-tube elements: living, cytoplasm but few organelles, joined by sieve plates; companion cells beside them
Process nameAscent of xylem sapTranslocation

Three transport routes

RoutePathSpeed / note
ApoplasticCell walls and extracellular spaces, outside plasma membranesBlocked at the endodermis by the Casparian strip
SymplasticCross one plasma membrane, then cytosol to cytosol through plasmodesmataAlready inside cells at the endodermis
TransmembraneRepeatedly cross plasma membranes cell to cellSlowest route

Root pressure vs transpirational pull

FeatureRoot pressureTranspirational pull
Type of pressurePositive (push)Negative (pull, tension)
WhenAt nightDay, when stomata are open
CauseRoots pump mineral ions into xylem; water follows from cortexEvaporation from leaves; surface tension at the air-water interface
ImportanceMinor; can push sap a short way and cause guttation, but too weak to lift water to the top of a tall treeMain force moving xylem sap
Visible signGuttation droplets on leaf edges in the morningWilting if water is not replaced

Bulk flow vs diffusion (her xylem review list)

Bulk flowDiffusion
Driven by pressure potential differences (a pressure gradient)Driven by solute potential (concentration) differences
In the xylem: through hollow dead cells, no membranes to cross (phloem bulk flow uses living sieve-tube elements)Each molecule moves on its own; in cells it often means crossing membranes of living cells
Moves the entire solutionMoves only water or only solutes
Much faster; long distanceSlower; short distance

Sugar sources and sinks

RoleDefinitionExamples
Sugar sourceNet producer of sugarMature leaves; storage root in spring
Sugar sinkNet consumer or depository of sugarRoots, buds, fruits, growing tips; storage root in summer; new leaves in early spring

Cohesion vs adhesion

TermWhat sticks to whatRole in xylem
CohesionWater to water (hydrogen bonds)Transmits the pull down the whole water column
AdhesionWater to another surface, the xylem wall (hydrogen bonds)Helps offset gravity ("Spider-Man")
Traps (ch36)
  • Percent water lost through stomata: the 36.1 transcript says 90%, but the outline and the 36.4 lecture say 95%. Use 95%.
  • Xylem sap moves MAINLY by NEGATIVE pressure (pull); phloem sap moves by POSITIVE pressure (push). Root pressure is a real but minor positive pressure in xylem, so "only phloem has positive pressure" is false.
  • Apoplast is not only cell walls: it also includes extracellular spaces and the interior of DEAD cells (tracheids, vessel elements).
  • Symplastic route crosses ONE plasma membrane; transmembrane route crosses membranes REPEATEDLY and is the slowest.
  • The Casparian strip is in the ENDODERMIS (not the epidermis or pericycle) and blocks the APOPLASTIC route, not the symplastic route.
  • Root hairs are near the root TIPS; most absorption happens there.
  • Mineral uptake into roots is ACTIVE transport (minerals are more concentrated in roots than in soil).
  • Bulk flow in the xylem needs NO energy from the plant (solar powered), but loading sugar into the phloem DOES need ATP.
  • Bulk flow is driven by PRESSURE potential, not solute potential; it moves the whole solution and is faster than diffusion.
  • Cohesion = water to water; adhesion = water to a different surface (xylem wall). Both come from hydrogen bonds.
  • Plasmolysis happens in a HIGHER solute concentration (cell loses water); turgid cells result from a LOWER solute concentration.
  • Water flows from HIGHER to LOWER water potential (not lower to higher).
  • Turgor pressure is POSITIVE pressure of the protoplast against the wall. Water in living cells is usually under positive pressure; xylem sap is usually under negative pressure.
  • A storage organ is not always a sink: it is a sink in summer and a source in spring.
  • Phloem loading is active because sugar is MORE concentrated in sieve-tube elements/companion cells than in source cells. Unloading at the sink: the instructor teaches sugar DIFFUSES out (passive) because sink concentration is lower; one animation says root cells remove sugar by active transport. Follow the instructor: diffusion at the sink, water follows by osmosis.
  • Translocation is a phloem term (sugar transport), not xylem.
  • Stomata open in the DAY and close at NIGHT (not the reverse).
  • Mature tracheids and vessel elements have NO cytoplasm; sieve-tube elements HAVE cytoplasm but few organelles.
  • Perforation plates join vessel elements (xylem); sieve plates join sieve-tube elements (phloem).
  • Cavitation is a water VAPOR pocket (from drought or freezing), not a collapse of the xylem wall; thick secondary walls prevent collapse.
  • Mycorrhizae especially help with PHOSPHATE uptake.
  • The Casparian strip is a BARRIER, not a route: apoplastic water does not pass through it. It crosses an endodermal plasma membrane into the symplast, then exits into the xylem apoplast.
  • Perforation plates join VESSEL ELEMENTS only. Tracheids pass water to each other through pits.
  • "Bulk flow occurs in hollow dead cells" is her xylem review point. Phloem bulk flow runs through LIVING sieve-tube elements. Bulk flow and diffusion differ mainly in what drives them (pressure vs concentration) and what moves (whole solution vs single substances).
  • "Root pressure is too weak to overcome gravity" (her wording) means it cannot lift water to the top of a tall tree. It can still push sap up some distance and cause guttation.
  • Closing stomata saves water but stops evaporative cooling, so a leaf with closed stomata on a hot day can overheat.
  • Water potential numbers (MPa) on the plasmolysis, turgor and ascent figures are not required; know only the direction of water movement.

Essay / short-answer prompts

Trace the path of water and minerals from the soil into the xylem of a root. Name the three routes (as numbered on her root figure) and explain the role of the endodermis and Casparian strip.

  • Most absorption happens near root tips; root hairs increase surface area; epidermis is permeable to water.
  • Active transport lets minerals accumulate in roots at higher concentration than in soil.
  • Apoplastic route: through cell walls and extracellular spaces. Symplastic route: cross one plasma membrane, then cytosol to cytosol through plasmodesmata. Transmembrane route: repeatedly cross membranes (slowest).
  • Endodermis: innermost cortex layer surrounding the vascular cylinder; the last checkpoint.
  • Casparian strip: waxy band in endodermal walls blocks the apoplastic route, forcing apoplastic water and minerals across a selective plasma membrane, which keeps out toxins and unneeded materials. Water and minerals already in the symplast continue through plasmodesmata.
  • Endodermal/pericycle cells discharge water and minerals from symplast into the apoplast (diffusion and active transport), then into tracheids and vessel elements (no plasmodesmata into dead xylem cells).

Explain how water moves from the roots to the top of a tall tree, and name the structures involved. Include the cohesion-tension hypothesis, root pressure, cohesion, adhesion, cavitation, and how bulk flow differs from diffusion.

  • Xylem sap (water and dissolved minerals) moves by bulk flow through dead tracheids and vessel elements.
  • Transpiration: water evaporates from leaves; water vapor in air spaces exits through open stomata.
  • As water evaporates, the air-water interface retreats into mesophyll cell walls; surface tension creates negative pressure that pulls water from the xylem.
  • Cohesion-tension: cohesion (water to water, hydrogen bonds) transmits the pull down the entire column to the roots; xylem sap is normally under negative pressure.
  • Adhesion (water to xylem walls) helps offset gravity; thick secondary walls stop the xylem from collapsing.
  • Root pressure: at night roots pump ions into the xylem and water follows; a positive push that causes guttation, but minor: too weak to lift water to the top of a tall tree. Xylem sap is mainly pulled by negative pressure.
  • Cavitation: water vapor pocket from drought or freezing breaks the chain; can kill the plant.
  • Needs no energy from the plant (solar powered); loading minerals into the root xylem is active.
  • Bulk flow vs diffusion: driven by pressure potential, not solute potential; in the xylem it runs through hollow dead cells, not across living membranes; moves the entire solution; much faster.

Draw and explain the pressure flow model of translocation in angiosperms. Define source and sink and explain why loading is active.

  • Translocation: transport of photosynthetic products in phloem sap (aqueous, high in sucrose) through sieve-tube elements.
  • Source = net producer (mature leaves); sink = net consumer or depository (roots, buds, fruits).
  • Sinks usually receive sugar from the nearest sources. A storage root is a sink in summer and a source in spring.
  • Loading at source is active: sugar is more concentrated in sieve-tube elements and companion cells than in source cells; proton pumps (ATP) and H+/sucrose cotransport. Route is species specific (symplast only, or symplast plus apoplast).
  • Sugar in the sieve tube lowers water potential; water enters from the xylem by osmosis; pressure rises at the source.
  • Positive pressure pushes sap by bulk flow from high pressure (source) to low pressure (sink).
  • At the sink, sugar diffuses out (lower concentration because it is used or converted to starch) and water follows by osmosis.
  • Water is recycled back to the xylem.
  • Contrast: phloem = positive pressure (push), xylem = negative pressure (pull).

Explain how stomata regulate transpiration. Include guard cells, day versus night, conditions that change transpiration, and benefits and costs of transpiration.

  • Flat leaves have large surface area and high surface-to-volume ratio, increasing both photosynthesis and water loss.
  • About 95% of water loss is through stomata; the waxy cuticle limits loss elsewhere.
  • Each stoma (the hole) is flanked by two guard cells that change shape to control its diameter; K+ ion channels in guard cells help.
  • Water loss per leaf depends on stomatal density and stoma size.
  • Open during the day for CO2 (photosynthesis); close at night to reduce water loss.
  • Sunny, warm, dry, windy conditions increase transpiration; if water is not replaced, the plant wilts.
  • Benefit: evaporative cooling prevents overheating, so closing stomata on a hot day saves water but reduces cooling. Transpiration also drives the pull of xylem sap.

Describe short-distance transport in plant cells. Explain how proton pumps, cotransport and ion channels move solutes, then describe what happens to a flaccid plant cell placed in a higher and in a lower solute concentration (water potential, turgor pressure, plasmolysis, wilting). Do not use water potential math.

  • Proton pumps use ATP to move H+ out of the cell and set the membrane potential.
  • Cotransport: H+ flowing back in carries sucrose (neutral) or nitrate (ion) in against their gradients.
  • Ion channels pass only certain ions; K+ channels in guard cells help open and close stomata.
  • Free water moves by osmosis from higher to lower water potential; water potential includes solute concentration and pressure.
  • Higher solute outside: water leaves, protoplast shrinks and pulls away from the wall (plasmolysis), as in the Elodea leaf in salt solution.
  • Lower solute outside: water enters, cell swells and becomes turgid; turgor pressure is the protoplast pushing on the wall (positive pressure).
  • Pressure potential can be positive or negative (syringe example); water in living cells is usually under positive pressure.
  • Loss of turgor causes wilting, reversible by watering.
  • Aquaporins facilitate water diffusion; their opening and closing changes the rate.
  • Diffusion, active transport and bulk flow act together; bulk flow (pressure gradient) handles long distances.

Traps (ch37)
  • Humus vs topsoil: humus is decaying organic material; topsoil is mineral particles, organisms, water and air. Both are in the A horizon.
  • Fresh mass vs dry mass: 80 to 90% of FRESH mass is water; about 96% of DRY mass is organic material from photosynthesis (her answer: from CO2), only 4% from soil.
  • Most of a plant's dry mass comes from the air (CO2), not from the soil. Her "96% from CO2" is shorthand: carbon comes from CO2, but the hydrogen in carbohydrates comes from water. On her exam, answer "CO2 from the air" when the choice is air vs soil.
  • Iron is a MICROnutrient, not a macronutrient. Sulfur, calcium and magnesium are MACROnutrients.
  • Magnesium (Mg) is a MACROnutrient; manganese (Mn) is a MICROnutrient. Do not swap them when listing.
  • Chlorine (a micronutrient) is not the same as chlorophyll (which contains nitrogen).
  • Sodium is required only by C4 and CAM plants; it is not one of the 17.
  • Mobile nutrient deficiency shows in OLDER organs; less mobile nutrient deficiency shows in YOUNGER organs.
  • Ammonia is NH3; ammonium is NH4+. The transcript calls NH4+ "ammonia" once; the outline correctly lists NO3- (nitrate) and NH4+ (ammonium) as the forms plants absorb.
  • Outline says nitrifying bacteria convert ammonia to nitrite; in the figure walkthrough she says ammonium to nitrite. Either way, step 1 makes nitrite and a different bacterium makes nitrate.
  • Nitrate to ammonium inside the plant is done by PLANT ENZYMES, not bacteria. Nitrate to N2 is denitrifying bacteria.
  • Nitrifying vs denitrifying: nitrifying MAKES nitrate; denitrifying REMOVES nitrate to N2 gas.
  • Ammonifying bacteria work on dead organic material (humus); nitrogen-fixing bacteria work on atmospheric N2.
  • Endophytes are NONpathogenic and live between cells, not inside them.
  • Ectomycorrhizae do not penetrate root cells; arbuscular mycorrhizae penetrate the cell wall but not the plasma membrane.
  • Ectomycorrhizae: about 10% of plant FAMILIES; arbuscular: about 85% of plant SPECIES; about 5% of species form none.
  • Epiphytes are not parasites: they use the host only as a substrate.
  • Carnivorous plants still photosynthesize; they trap animals for NITROGEN, not sugar.
  • Indian pipe parasitizes mycorrhizae, not the host plant directly. Dodder and Indian pipe are nonphotosynthetic; mistletoe is green.
  • In crop rotation the legume is often plowed under (green manure), not harvested.
  • Her word for the nitrogen-deficient corn leaf is browning along the main vein (the photo looks yellow-brown). N = main vein, K = tip and sides, P = purple edges on older growth.
  • Not on the exam: drawing the nitrogen cycle, the nitrogen fixation formula, the percent mass column, C4/CAM pathways.

Essay / short-answer prompts

Describe the three soil horizons and the organisms in topsoil. Explain why fertilization is needed, compare commercial and organic fertilizers, explain how fertilizer runoff harms lakes, and connect N-P-K to the most common plant deficiencies and their corn leaf symptoms.

  • A horizon: topsoil (mineral particles, organisms, water, air, inorganic material) and humus (decaying organic material); darkest.
  • B horizon: less organic material, less weathered than A. C horizon: mainly partially broken down rock, compact.
  • Topsoil organisms: bacteria, fungi, algae and other protists, insects, earthworms, nematodes, plant roots; earthworms mix and clump soil, improving gas diffusion and water retention.
  • Same crop, same place, many seasons depletes nutrients and lowers yield; fertilization replaces lost mineral nutrients.
  • Commercial fertilizer: N, P, K ("the three biggies"), immediately available, not retained (runoff). Organic (manure, fishmeal, compost): gradual release as it decomposes.
  • Leached minerals reach ponds and lakes, cause algal blooms that deplete oxygen and kill fish.
  • Most common deficiencies are N, K, P. Mobile nutrient deficiency shows in older organs, less mobile in younger organs. Corn: N browning along main vein; K tip and sides; P purplish edges on older growth.

Define an essential element and explain how researchers identify one. Then, FROM MEMORY, list all 9 macronutrients and all 8 micronutrients in a two-column table, explain how the two groups differ, and state where a plant's fresh and dry mass come from.

  • Essential element: required for the plant to complete its life cycle and reproduce; 17 total (9 + 8), identified with hydroponic culture (omit one element and see whether the plant can complete its life cycle).
  • Macronutrients (9, relatively large amounts): carbon, oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen, phosphorus, sulfur, potassium, calcium, magnesium. Full credit requires all nine; each omission or wrong element loses credit.
  • Micronutrients (8, very small amounts): chlorine, iron, boron, manganese, zinc, copper, nickel, molybdenum. Full credit requires all eight; magnesium is not manganese.
  • Micronutrients act as cofactors, nonprotein helpers in enzymatic reactions. Nitrogen contributes most to growth and crop yield. C4 and CAM plants also need sodium (not one of the 17).
  • 80 to 90% of fresh mass is water; about 96% of dry mass comes from CO2 via photosynthesis (shorthand: carbon from CO2, hydrogen from water); 4% of dry mass is inorganic from the soil.

Explain how bacteria supply plants with nitrogen. List the five ways rhizobacteria and endophytes help plants, explain why plants cannot use N2 directly, match each type of nitrogen cycle bacterium to what it converts and what it produces, and explain how legume nodules and crop rotation raise soil nitrogen.

  • Rhizosphere and rhizobacteria (free-living or endophytes, nonpathogenic, between cells). Five benefits: antibiotics, absorb toxic metals, increase nutrient availability, convert N2 to usable forms, chemicals that stimulate root growth.
  • Nitrogen is needed for proteins (amino acids) and nucleic acids; deficiency can be most limiting.
  • N2 has a triple bond; plants and humans cannot convert it, bacteria can. Plants absorb NO3- (most common) or NH4+.
  • Nitrogen-fixing: N2 to NH3, which gains H+ to form NH4+ (free-living or rhizospheric, e.g. Anabaena).
  • Ammonifying: amino acids from decomposed humus to NH4+. Nitrifying (two steps, different bacteria): ammonia to nitrite, nitrite to nitrate. Denitrifying: nitrate to N2, lost to the atmosphere.
  • Plant enzymes (not bacteria) convert absorbed nitrate to ammonium.
  • Rhizobium in legume root nodules (soybeans, green beans, peas), species-specific; crop rotation: maize one year, legume the next, plowed in as green manure.

Compare ectomycorrhizae and arbuscular mycorrhizae and explain what each partner gains. Then contrast these mutualisms with the nonmutualistic strategies of epiphytes, parasitic plants and carnivorous plants, giving an example of each.

  • Mycorrhizae: plant gives sugar; fungus increases surface area for water uptake, supplies minerals, secretes growth factors and antibiotics.
  • Ectomycorrhizae: mantle over root, hyphae into apoplast, do not penetrate cells; about 10% of plant families, mostly woody.
  • Arbuscular: hyphae into cortex, through cell wall but not plasma membrane, arbuscules in membrane invaginations; about 85% of species, most crops, most common. About 5% of species form none.
  • Epiphyte: grows on a plant as a substrate, water and minerals from rain (staghorn fern, bromeliads, orchids).
  • Parasitic: water, minerals, sugar from a living host (mistletoe; dodder; Indian pipe on mycorrhizae).
  • Carnivorous: photosynthetic, gets nitrogen from trapped insects (pitcher plant, sundew, Venus flytrap).

FeatureMaleFemale
WhereMicrosporangia (pollen sacs) in the antherMegasporangium in the ovule, inside the ovary
Diploid cell that does meiosisMicrosporocyte (2n)Megasporocyte (2n)
Products of meiosis4 microspores (n), all survive, genetically different4 megaspores (n), 3 die, 1 survives
Mitosis after meiosisOnce: generative cell + tube cell3 times without cytokinesis: 8 haploid nuclei, then 7 cells
GametophytePollen grainEmbryo sac
Gametes2 sperm (from generative cell)1 egg (plus 2 polar nuclei used for endosperm)

Mechanisms that prevent self-fertilization

MechanismHow it works
Separate individualsStaminate flowers (no carpels) and carpellate flowers (no stamens) on different plants
TimingStamens and carpels mature at different times
Spatial arrangementPin vs thrum flowers keep stamens and stigma apart
Self-incompatibilityPlant rejects its own pollen or close relatives' pollen; most common

Traps (ch38)
  • Pistil vs carpel: a carpel is one unit; a pistil is a single carpel or several fused carpels. The instructor accepts either term for the whole female structure.
  • Pollination vs fertilization: pollination is pollen moving from anther to stigma; fertilization is fusion of gametes in the embryo sac.
  • Megasporangium vs megaspore: the megasporangium (2n) is the tissue in the ovule; the megaspore (n) is the surviving product of meiosis that makes the embryo sac.
  • Microsporangia are the pollen sacs; microspores are the haploid cells inside that become pollen grains.
  • The pollen grain IS the male gametophyte; the embryo sac IS the female gametophyte. Neither is a gamete.
  • Generative cell divides to make the 2 sperm; the tube cell makes the pollen tube. Do not swap them.
  • Zygote is 2n; endosperm is 3n (sperm + 2 polar nuclei). Endosperm is not an embryo.
  • Ovule becomes the seed; ovary becomes the fruit. Do not reverse them.
  • Seed coat comes from the integuments, not from the ovary wall.
  • Embryo sac: 8 haploid nuclei but only 7 cells, because the central cell holds 2 polar nuclei.
  • Synergids (flank the egg, guide the pollen tube) vs antipodal cells (opposite end, unknown function).
  • Hypocotyl is below the cotyledons and ends in the radicle; epicotyl is above the cotyledons. Radicle emerges first in germination.
  • Aggregate fruit = one flower, many carpels; multiple fruit = many flowers. Apple is accessory because the eaten part is the receptacle.
  • In angiosperms the sporophyte dominates and the gametophyte depends on it; in more primitive plants it was the reverse.
  • Self-incompatibility rejects SELF; the animal immune response rejects NONSELF.
  • Selfing, lecture vs biology: her lecture says selfing gives no genetic diversity and all-identical offspring, and that is the framing to recognize on her exam. Biological nuance: selfing is sexual reproduction and can produce genetically different offspring through segregation in meiosis. Asexual reproduction produces clones.
  • Bt toxin is toxic to insects, not to vertebrates.
  • The monarch butterfly study on Bt pollen was discredited; spraying non-Bt maize hurts monarchs more.
  • GMO and organic are not the same thing.
  • Cassava: the transcript says "8000 million" people; the slide reads about 800 million. Use 800 million if a number appears.
  • Seed germination: ALL seeds need oxygen, warmth and water, but light is species dependent: some seeds need light, others need dark. Do not call darkness an invalid germination condition.
  • Outline says "adventitious roots" under asexual reproduction; the lecture describes adventitious SHOOTS arising from a parent root system (suckers). Answer with adventitious shoots from roots.
  • Shoot protection in germination: bean cotyledons enclose the shoot tip and the hypocotyl hook pulls it up; maize cotyledon stays underground and a finger-shaped sheath protects the leaves.
  • Seed coat is 2n maternal tissue (from the integuments), not 3n and not a product of fertilization.

Essay / short-answer prompts

Draw and label the angiosperm life cycle (Figure 38.6) from the mature sporophyte back to a new sporophyte. Give each structure's ploidy, show where meiosis, mitosis, pollination and double fertilization occur, and explain each step.

  • Mature flowering plant is the sporophyte (2n) and is dominant; the gametophytes are microscopic and depend on it.
  • Male: anther has 4 microsporangia (pollen sacs); microsporocyte (2n) undergoes meiosis to make 4 microspores (n), genetically different.
  • Each microspore divides by mitosis into a generative cell and a tube cell; with the spore wall this is the pollen grain (male gametophyte, n).
  • Female: ovule in the ovary holds the megasporangium (2n); megasporocyte (2n) undergoes meiosis to make 4 megaspores (n); 3 die, 1 survives.
  • Megaspore divides by mitosis 3 times without cytokinesis (8 haploid nuclei, 7 cells) = embryo sac (female gametophyte): egg at the micropyle flanked by 2 synergids, 3 antipodal cells at the far end, central cell with 2 polar nuclei; two integuments leave the micropyle opening.
  • Pollination: pollen moves from anther to stigma (wind, water or animals); pollen of the right species absorbs water and germinates; the tube cell makes the pollen tube; the generative cell divides into 2 sperm (n).
  • Pollen tube grows down the style into the ovary, enters the ovule through the micropyle, and discharges the 2 sperm.
  • Double fertilization: sperm + egg = zygote (2n); sperm + 2 polar nuclei = endosperm (3n), food. This makes endosperm form only where an egg is fertilized.
  • Seed ploidies: embryo (2n), endosperm (3n), seed coat (2n, maternal tissue from the integuments). Embryo + endosperm + seed coat = seed (from the ovule); ovary = fruit.
  • Seed is dispersed, germinates (radicle first), and grows into a new sporophyte (2n) that flowers; the cycle repeats.

Draw and label an idealized flower. Give the function of each floral organ, define complete, incomplete, sterile and unisexual flowers, and explain how flower traits match wind, bee, moth or butterfly, fly, bat and bird pollination.

  • Flower = reproductive shoot of the sporophyte attached at the receptacle.
  • Sepals: leaflike, protect the floral bud. Petals: brightly colored, attract specific pollinators. Both are sterile modified leaves.
  • Stamen (male): filament + anther; the anther's microsporangia (pollen sacs) make pollen.
  • Carpel or pistil (female): stigma (sticky, catches pollen), style, ovary with ovules. Pistil = one carpel (simple) or fused carpels (compound). Stamens and carpels are sporophylls.
  • Complete = all four organs; incomplete = lacks one or more; sterile = lacks both stamens and carpels; unisexual = has only one of them.
  • Pollination = pollen from anther to stigma; transfer to a different plant keeps genetic diversity.
  • Wind (about 20%): small, inconspicuous, no scent or nectar, lots of pollen. Bees (insects, about 65%): yellow or blue, sweet, UV nectar guides.
  • Moths: white or yellow, sweet (yucca); butterflies: bright colors. Flies: look and smell like rotten meat (carrion flower). Bats: light colored, aromatic, open at night (agave). Birds: large, red or yellow, little scent, much nectar, tube shape (hummingbird).
  • Coevolution: flower shape matches the pollinator's pollen-carrying parts (Darwin's 28 cm moth tongue prediction).

Describe the structure of a mature eudicot seed (garden bean) and the process of germination, including dormancy, how it is broken, and what all seeds need to germinate.

  • Seed = dormant embryo + stored food + protective seed coat (from the integuments).
  • Endosperm develops before the embryo; in the bean its food is exported to the two fleshy cotyledons (seed leaves); monocots have one cotyledon.
  • Hypocotyl below the cotyledons ends in the radicle (embryonic root); epicotyl above the cotyledons; plumule = epicotyl + young leaves + shoot apical meristem.
  • Seed dehydrates to 5 to 15% water and becomes dormant; dormancy makes germination happen at the best time and place.
  • Dormancy broken by species-specific cues: rainfall (desert), heat or smoke (fire areas), a period of cold, passage through an animal gut.
  • All seeds need oxygen, warmth and water; some also need light, others need dark (species dependent).
  • Germination: imbibition (water uptake due to low water potential), radicle emerges first to anchor and take up water, then the shoot breaks the soil.
  • Bean: cotyledons enclose the shoot tip; the hypocotyl hook pulls the cotyledons up; light straightens the hook; cotyledons yellow and drop. Maize: cotyledon stays underground; leaves push up in a finger-shaped sheath.

What is a fruit? Describe the four fruit types by developmental origin with an example of each, contrast dry and fleshy fruits, and explain how fruits are dispersed.

  • Fruit = mature ovary; protects seeds and aids dispersal; develops in response to hormonal changes from fertilization; ripens when seeds finish developing.
  • Simple: single carpel or fused carpels of one flower (pea pod: pod is the ovary, seeds were the ovules).
  • Aggregate: one flower with many separate carpels, each with its own ovary (raspberry, blackberry).
  • Multiple: a group of flowers, an inflorescence (pineapple).
  • Accessory: includes other floral parts; apple, where the eaten part is the receptacle and the ovary is the core.
  • Dry fruits: ovary dries out; hooks, wings, parasols, hairs, or tension that shoots seeds.
  • Fleshy fruits: ovary wall becomes soft, sweet, colorful (tomato, peach), turning from green to a contrasting color and making sugar to attract animals.
  • Dispersal moves seeds away from competition with the parent: water (coconut), wind (dandelion, tumbleweed, maple samara, Asian climbing gourd), animals (barbs, squirrel caches, seeds in feces, ants).

Fill in a table of GM crops with the trait each carries (Bt corn, Bt cotton, golden rice, cassava, papaya, switchgrass and poplar). Then give two benefits and the most serious risk of GM crops, and three ways to prevent transgene escape.

  • Bt corn: makes Bt toxin, kills insect pests but not vertebrates. Bt cotton: same toxin.
  • Golden rice: vitamin A. Cassava: iron and beta-carotene (biofortification). Papaya: resists ringspot virus. Switchgrass and poplar: carbon-neutral biofuels.
  • Benefits: higher yield and nutrition without more farmland; Bt maize has 90% less Fusarium cancer-causing toxin; Bt cotton in India cut insecticide use 41% and farmer poisonings 80%; no credible evidence of allergens; the monarch study was discredited.
  • Most serious risk: transgene escape by crop-to-weed hybridization, making superweeds possibly resistant to herbicides.
  • Prevention: male sterility; transgenes in chloroplast DNA (not passed in pollen); flowers that never open (self-pollination only).
  • GMOs are not the same as organic produce.
